<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct a smooth Kähler metric in the Fubini–Study class of $`\mathbb{CP}^{10}`$ whose Calabi flow develops unbounded scalar curvature in finite time. This disproves Chen's smooth long-time existence conjecture, even in a class containing a constant-scalar-curvature metric.

- **claim-finite-time-calabi-singularity** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is a smooth $U(10)$-invariant Kähler form $\omega_0$ on $\mathbb{CP}^{10}$, in the class $[\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}]$, whose maximal smooth Calabi flow has existence interval $[0,T_*)$ with $0<T_*<\infty$. There are a point $p\in\mathbb{CP}^{10}$ and a constant $a>0$ such that $$S(\omega(t))(p)\sim a(T_*-t)^{-1/2}\qquad\text{as }t\uparrow T_*.$$
  - Explanation: A smooth invariant initial metric in the Fubini–Study class develops unbounded scalar curvature at a fixed point in finite time. Having a constant-scalar-curvature representative in the class therefore does not guarantee smooth global existence for arbitrary initial metrics.
- **claim-stationary-product-singularities** (construction, [source](#stmt-cor-product-singularities)): Let $\omega(t)$, $T_*$, $p$, and $a$ be as in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, and let $(Y,\eta)$ be a compact connected Kähler manifold with $\eta$ of constant scalar curvature. With $\pi_X$ and $\pi_Y$ the projections from $\mathbb{CP}^{10}\times Y$, the maximal smooth Calabi flow from $\Omega_0=\pi_X^*\omega_0+\pi_Y^*\eta$ is $$\Omega(t)=\pi_X^*\omega(t)+\pi_Y^*\eta, \qquad 0\leq t<T_*.$$ For every $y\in Y$, $S(\Omega(t))(p,y)\sim a(T_*-t)^{-1/2}$. In particular, finite-time smooth Calabi-flow singularities occur in every complex dimension $n\geq10$, on $\mathbb{CP}^{10}\times\mathbb{CP}^{n-10}$, in a class containing a Kähler–Einstein metric.
  - Explanation: Taking a product with a stationary constant-scalar-curvature Kähler factor preserves the singular time and leading curvature divergence. Appropriate projective factors give examples in every stated higher dimension in classes containing Kähler–Einstein metrics.
- **claim-exact-momentum-reduction** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-geo-reduction)): Let $I$ be an open time interval, and let $H\in C^\infty(I\times[0,1])$ satisfy $$\label{geo:positivity} b(t,x):=1+v_*(x)H(t,x)>0.$$ Define $$\label{geo:momentum} v=v_*+v_*^2H=v_*b,\qquad K(H)=w^{-1}(wv_*^2H)'',\qquad \mathcal AH=\big[w^{-1}(wv_*^2H)''\big]''.$$ At each time these data, together with an affine choice in the Legendre potential, determine a smooth $U(n)$-invariant Kähler form in $[\omega_*]$. Its scalar curvature and average scalar curvature are $$\label{geo:scalar} S(t,x)=n(n+1)-K(H)(t,x),\qquad \bar S=n(n+1).$$ If, in addition, $$\label{geo:equation} H_t+(1+v_*H)^2\mathcal AH=0,$$ then for any $t_0\in I$ and any initial affine choice there is a smooth family of these forms satisfying the Calabi flow exactly. More precisely, its global potential relative to $\omega(t_0)$ satisfies $$\label{geo:potential-flow} \phi(t_0)=0,\qquad \omega(t)=\omega(t_0)+i\partial\bar\partial\phi(t),\qquad \phi_t=S(\omega(t))-\bar S.$$
  - Explanation: A smooth positive momentum perturbation reconstructs an invariant Kähler metric in the fixed class. The scalar evolution reconstructs the normalized Calabi flow when the affine Legendre terms follow the prescribed evolution.
- **claim-sphere-and-flat-operator-identities** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geo-operators)): This identification is a bijection between $C^\infty[0,1]$ and the smooth invariant functions on $\Sigma$. The pushforward of spherical volume under $x$ is a positive constant multiple of $wv_*^2\,\mathrm{d}x$. With the nonpositive Laplacian convention, put $L=\frac14\Delta_\Sigma$. On invariant functions, $$\label{geo:sphere-operator} L=x(1-x)\partial_x^2+\big(n+2-(n+5)x\big)\partial_x, \qquad \mathcal A=(-L+n+3)(-L+2(n+2)).$$ The right-hand expression defines a smooth elliptic operator of order four on all of $\Sigma$.* *In the core coordinates $(z,\theta)\mapsto(z,\sqrt{1-\|z\|^2}\,\theta)$, with $\theta\in S^5$, its Laplacian on functions independent of $\theta$ is $$\label{geo:local-laplacian} \Delta_\Sigma =\Delta_z-\sum_{i,j=1}^m z_i z_j\partial_{z_i z_j} -(m+5)\sum_{i=1}^m z_i\partial_{z_i}.$$ The flat momentum operator obtained by replacing $v_*$ by $x$ in <a href="#geo:momentum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:momentum">[geo:momentum]</a> satisfies $$\label{geo:flat-operator} \widehat\mathcal AH :=\big[x^{1-n}(x^{n+1}H)''\big]'' =\big(x\partial_x^2+(n+2)\partial_x\big)^2H =\frac1{16}\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^m}^2H,\qquad x=\|z\|^2.$$
  - Explanation: The auxiliary sphere converts endpoint regularity into ordinary invariant smoothness. Its factored elliptic operator agrees with the compact momentum operator, while the flat radial operator is a multiple of the Euclidean biharmonic operator.
- **claim-subleading-correction-curvature-obstruction** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-geo-obstruction)): Let $G\in C^\infty[0,\infty)$ satisfy $G(0)=1$ and $G'(0)\ne0$, and define $F$ by <a href="#geo:selfsimilar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:selfsimilar">[geo:selfsimilar]</a>. Suppose that for some $T>0$ and $\delta>0$ there is a smooth invariant solution $H=F+V$ of <a href="#geo:equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:equation">[geo:equation]</a> on $(-T,0)\times\Sigma$ such that $1+v_*H>0$ and $$\label{geo:correction-bound} V(t,0)=O(\rho^{-1+\delta})\qquad(t\uparrow0).$$ Then a smooth $U(10)$-invariant metric $\omega_0\in[\omega_*]$ has maximal smooth Calabi-flow existence interval $[0,T_*)$, where $T_*=T/2<\infty$. At the fixed affine origin $p$, the scalar curvature of this flow satisfies $$\label{geo:curvature-rate} S(\tau,p)=a(T_*-\tau)^{-1/2} +O\big(1+(T_*-\tau)^{(-1+\delta)/2}\big),\qquad a=-110G'(0)\ne0.$$ In particular $S(\tau,p)\sim a(T_*-\tau)^{-1/2}$, and $a>0$ when $G'(0)<0$.
  - Explanation: A positive compact solution whose correction is lower order at the origin yields a genuine smooth Calabi flow with finite maximal lifetime. The origin derivative of the profile determines the sign and leading coefficient of curvature divergence.
- **claim-positive-shrinking-profile** (construction, [source](#stmt-prof-profile)): There exist a constant $c>0$ and a function $G\in C^\infty([0,\infty))$ such that $$\label{prof:profile-equation} Q(y\partial_y)(G-1)=\frac{y^2}{2}\left(\frac1G-\frac1c\right), \qquad Q(D)=(D-1)(D+9)(D+10).$$ The function $G$ is bounded above and bounded away from zero, satisfies $G(0)=1$ and $G'(0)<0$, and is analytic near zero. There are real coefficients $g_j$, $j\ge1$, such that, for every $J,\ell\ge0$, $$\label{prof:expansion} \frac{\mathrm{d}^\ell}{\mathrm{d}y^\ell} \left(G(y)-c-\sum_{j=1}^{J}g_jy^{-2j}\right) =O\bigl(y^{-2(J+1)-\ell}\bigr)\qquad(y\longrightarrow\infty).$$ In particular, $u\mapsto G(u^{-1/2})$ extends smoothly to $u=0$ from the right, with value $c$.* *Set $h(y)=(G(y)-1)/y$, with its smooth value at zero. For $t<0$, the function $$\label{prof:flat-shrinker} F(t,x)=\rho^{-1}h(x/\rho),\qquad \rho=\sqrt{-t},$$ solves $$F_t+(1+xF)^2\widehat{\mathcal A}F=0, \qquad \widehat{\mathcal A}=(x\partial_x^2+12\partial_x)^2.$$
  - Explanation: The profile is positive and uniformly separated from zero, has a negative origin derivative, and admits differentiated asymptotics at infinity. It generates an exact shrinking solution of the flat scalar equation.
- **claim-holomorphic-origin-family** (construction, [source](#stmt-prof-origin)): For $\|k-k_0\|\le .1$ and $\|d-d_0\|\le .1$ in $\mathbb{C}$, there is a unique solution germ of <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a>, analytic in $r=s^{3/2}$, of the form $$\label{prof:origin-series} p=d+kr+\sum_{i\ge2}a_ir^i,\qquad r=s^{3/2}.$$ The series is jointly holomorphic in $r,k,d$ for $\|r\|<3$ and satisfies $$\label{prof:origin-majorant} 3\|k\|+\sum_{i\ge2}\|a_i\|3^i<.8.$$ Each of $p(1),p'(1),p''(1)$ has modulus at most $2$ on this parameter polydisc. For the real parameters in <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a>, $1+p>0$ on $0\le s\le1$.
  - Explanation: The singular origin equation has a uniquely determined analytic germ after prescribing the free parameters. A uniform power-series majorant controls its endpoint jet and ensures positivity for the real shooting family.
- **claim-root-projection-convolution-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-prof-root-bounds)): The roots in <a href="#prof:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:coordinates">[prof:coordinates]</a> are distinct and satisfy $$\label{prof:root-boxes} \begin{split} -1.08659&<\lambda_0<-1.08655,\\ .40933&<\Re\lambda_1<.40938,\qquad .8671<\Im\lambda_1<.86721. \end{split}$$ Every entry of $\Gamma-\Lambda$ has modulus less than $.001$. The absolute row sums of $\Gamma$ are less than $.92,.68,.68$, respectively. With $$\label{prof:convolution-constants} M_i=\sum_{j=0}^2 \frac{\|\lambda_j\|^i}{\|\Re\lambda_j\|\,\|D_0'(\lambda_j)\|},\qquad 0\le i\le2,$$ one has $$M_0<1.96,\qquad M_1<1.92,\qquad M_2<1.88, \qquad \frac1{\Re\lambda_1\|D_0'(\lambda_1)\|}<.82.$$
  - Explanation: Exact root isolation establishes the stable and growing directions used in the tail construction. Projection and convolution bounds then control the Green representation quantitatively.
- **claim-tail-polynomial-residual** (bound, [source](#stmt-prof-tail-residual)): Uniformly for the parameters in <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a>, $$\sup_{s\ge56}\|\mathcal E_d(p_{\rm a})(s)\|<3\cdot10^{-13}.$$ Each derivative of this residual tends to zero at infinity. If $P_{\rm a}(d)=(p_{\rm a},p_{\rm a}',p_{\rm a}'')(56;d)$, then $$\label{prof:tail-parameter-bound} \lVert P_{\rm a}(d)-P_{\rm a}(d_0)\rVert_\infty\le .01\|d-d_0\|.$$
  - Explanation: The explicit asymptotic polynomial has a uniformly small residual throughout the half-line. Its matching jet also varies mildly with the shooting parameter.
- **claim-bounded-tail-family** (construction, [source](#stmt-prof-tail)): For each $d$ arising in <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a> and each real $\|q\|\le .26R$, there is a solution $p_{\rm tail}$ of <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> on $[56,\infty)$ such that $$\|p_{\rm tail}\|<.001, \qquad q_{\rm tail}=q,\qquad \|\xi_{\rm tail}\|<.25R.$$ Its first three jet entries tend to zero at infinity. The solution, as a bounded jet, and its initial coordinates depend continuously on $q,d$.
  - Explanation: A contraction constructs bounded tail solutions with a prescribed stable coordinate and controlled growing coordinates. Continuous dependence makes this family suitable for topological matching.
- **claim-differentiated-weighted-asymptotics** (bound, [source](#stmt-prof-weighted-remainder)): Let $p$ be the matched solution above. There are uniquely determined coefficients $d_j$, extending <a href="#prof:asymptotic-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:asymptotic-polynomial">[prof:asymptotic-polynomial]</a>, such that for every $J,\ell\ge0$, $$\label{prof:s-expansion} \frac{\mathrm{d}^\ell}{\mathrm{d}s^\ell} \left(p(s)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}d_js^{-3j}\right) =O\bigl(s^{-3(J+1)-\ell}\bigr).$$
  - Explanation: The matched solution has asymptotic expansions with differentiated remainder estimates at every fixed order. Uniqueness among bounded remainders identifies the actual solution with the solution constructed in the required polynomial weight.
- **claim-local-fourth-order-schauder-estimates** (bound, [source](#stmt-ana-schauder)): Under these assumptions the following estimates hold.* 1. *If $Q_R=B_R\times(-R^4,0]$ and $\mathcal L u=f$ on $Q_2$, then $$\label{ana:interior-schauder} \mathcal N_\alpha(u;Q_1) \le C\bigl(\|f\|_{\alpha;Q_2}+\\|u\\|_{\infty;Q_2}\bigr).$$* 2. *If $0<h\le1$, $\mathcal L u=f$ on $B_2\times[0,h]$, and $u(\cdot,0)=0$, then $$\label{ana:initial-schauder} \mathcal N_\alpha(u;B_1\times[0,h]) \le C\bigl(\|f\|_{\alpha;B_2\times[0,h]} +\\|u\\|_{\infty;B_2\times[0,h]}\bigr).$$ Here $C$ depends only on $d,\alpha,\lambda,K$ and the fixed spatial gaps, and in <span class="upright">(ii)</span> is independent of $h$.* *These are a priori estimates for classical solutions in the indicated spaces; smooth approximation gives the same conclusions for limits of such solutions. The usual interior regularity theorem also applies: with smooth coefficients, distributional solutions in $L^2_{\rm loc}$ and Hölder sources are locally in the space <a href="#ana:local-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:local-norm">[ana:local-norm]</a>, and are smooth when the source is smooth. The estimates are from preceding times and require no terminal data.
  - Explanation: Uniformly elliptic scalar parabolic operators admit interior estimates and zero-data initial-face estimates in the full local parabolic norm. The initial-face constant is independent of the elapsed time, and no terminal data are required.
- **claim-interior-estimate-from-spacetime-energy** (bound, [source](#stmt-ana-l2)): Let $U_1\Subset U_2$ be fixed bounded spatial domains and $b_0<b_1<b$, with $b-b_1$ bounded above. Suppose that the coefficient assumptions of Lemma <a href="#ana:schauder" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:schauder">13</a> hold on $U_2\times(b_0,b)$ and that $\mathcal Lu=f$ there. Then $$\label{ana:l2-estimate} \mathcal N_\alpha(u;U_1\times[b_1,b)) \le C\bigl(\|f\|_{\alpha;U_2\times(b_0,b)} +\\|u\\|_{L^2(U_2\times(b_0,b))}\bigr).$$ The constant depends on the spatial gap, $b_1-b_0$, the upper bound for $b-b_1$, and the coefficient constants, but requires no terminal trace. The estimate is unchanged if the upper face is included and the solution is defined there. In particular it holds on fixed nested annuli through an open upper time endpoint.
  - Explanation: The interior parabolic estimate can use the spacetime square-integrable norm in place of a supremum input. A fixed preceding-time buffer permits estimates through an open upper endpoint without prescribing a terminal trace.
- **claim-uniform-scaled-forward-estimate** (bound, [source](#stmt-ana-short)): Fix $p\in\mathbb{R}$, $0<\alpha<1$, and the preceding scale and coefficient constants. There exist $\eta_0>0$ and $C<\infty$, independent of $\inf s$, with the following property. If $$0<T\le\eta_0(\inf s)^4,\qquad \mathcal Lu=f\quad\hbox{on }M\times[0,T],\qquad u(\cdot,0)=0,$$ then, with $\eta=T/(\inf s)^4$, $$\label{ana:short-bounds} \\|u\\|_{X_{\alpha,p}(s)}\le C\\|f\\|_{Y_{\alpha,p}(s)},\qquad \sup_{z,t}s(z)^{-p}\|u(z,t)\| \le C\eta\\|f\\|_{Y_{\alpha,p}(s)}.$$ For each fixed positive scale and smooth coefficients the zero-data Cauchy problem has a unique solution with this regularity for $f\in Y_{\alpha,p}(s)$. The same estimates hold on every interval $[0,T')$, $T'\le T$, on which the coefficients and source obey the stated bounds.
  - Explanation: The zero-data forward problem has scale-uniform bounds on sufficiently short scaled intervals. The weighted supremum gains an elapsed-time factor even though the full parabolic norm need not become small.
- **claim-short-time-spatial-smallness** (bound, [source](#stmt-ana-interpolation)): Under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#ana:short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:short">15</a>, the local scaled parabolic $C^\alpha$ norms of the spatial derivatives of order $0\le j\le3$, with weight $p-j$, satisfy $$\label{ana:lower-small} \sup_P\bigl\|D_y^j(s_0^{-p}u\circ\Psi_P)\bigr\|_{\alpha;D_P} \le C\eta^{(4-j-\alpha)/4}\\|f\\|_{Y_{\alpha,p}(s)}.$$ If the coefficient and source bounds also hold at an exponent $\alpha'\in(\alpha,1)$, then $$\label{ana:fourth-small} \\|u\\|_{X'_{\alpha,p}(s)} \le C\eta^{(\alpha'-\alpha)/4} \\|f\\|_{Y_{\alpha',p}(s)}.$$ Constants may depend on $\alpha'$ but remain independent of the scale and elapsed time.
  - Explanation: Interpolation makes lower spatial derivatives small on short scaled intervals. Smallness through the highest spatial derivative requires a strictly higher source Hölder exponent and concerns the norm omitting the time derivative.
- **claim-ancient-flat-right-inverse** (construction, [source](#stmt-flat-inverse)): There is a number $\delta\in(0,1)$ such that, for every fixed $\alpha\in(0,1)$, the operator <a href="#flat:operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flat:operator">[flat:operator]</a> has a bounded real linear right inverse $$\widehat D:Y\longrightarrow X,\qquad \widehat{\mathcal P}\widehat D f=f,\qquad \\|\widehat D f\\|_X\le C\\|f\\|_Y, \qquad p_0=-2+2\delta.$$ Here $Y$ has weight $p_0-4$ and parabolic exponent $\alpha$, and $X$ has weight $p_0$ and includes the parabolic $\alpha$ norms of all spatial derivatives through order four and of the first time derivative. The solution is radial and classical on $t<0$. Its initial values are selected by the construction. The constant depends only on the fixed profile, $\delta$, $\alpha$, and the fixed choices in the local norms.
  - Explanation: A spectral splitting in similarity coordinates constructs a bounded ancient right inverse on radial weighted spaces. Its initial values are selected using the forcing, so this is not a forward solution operator for arbitrary prescribed initial data.
- **claim-compact-positive-realization** (construction, [source](#stmt-glue-solution)): Let $G$ be the profile of Theorem <a href="#prof:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:profile">6</a>, put $$h(y)=\frac{G(y)-1}{y},\qquad F(t,x)=\rho^{-1}h(x/\rho),$$ and fix $0<\alpha<\alpha'<1$. Choose $\delta\in(0,1)$ and the ancient right inverse supplied by Theorem <a href="#flat:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flat:inverse">18</a>. For every sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, there are $T>0$ and a real invariant function $V\in X_\alpha$ on $\Sigma\times[-T,0)$ such that $\lVert V\rVert_{X'_\alpha}\le\varepsilon$ and $H=F+V$ satisfies $$\label{glue:equation} H_t+(1+v_*H)^2\mathcal AH=0,\qquad v_*=x(1-x).$$ The function $H$ is smooth on $\Sigma\times(-T,0)$, and there are constants $0<c_1<c_2<\infty$, independent of $t$, such that $$\label{glue:positive} c_1\le 1+v_*H\le c_2.$$ In particular, $H(t,\cdot)$ is smooth as a function of $x\in[0,1]$, and $$\label{glue:center} V(t,0)=O(\rho^{-1+\delta})\qquad(t\uparrow0).$$
  - Explanation: The flat shrinker admits a small invariant correction on the compact auxiliary sphere. The corrected solution stays positive, is smooth before the singular time, and preserves the lower-order origin correction required for curvature divergence.
- **claim-scaled-background-and-geometric-mismatch** (bound, [source](#stmt-glue-background)): For $0<T\le1$, on the compact domain one has $$\label{glue:background-bounds} F=O_{\mathrm{sc}}(b^{-2}),\qquad \mathcal AF=O_{\mathrm{sc}}(b^{-6}),\qquad A_c=O_{\mathrm{sc}}(1),\qquad B_c=O_{\mathrm{sc}}(b^{-4}),\qquad v_*=O_{\mathrm{sc}}(b^2).$$ The analogous flat bounds hold in a fixed neighborhood of $r=0$. In the common coordinates there, write $$\mathcal P-\widehat\mathcal P=\sum_{\|\nu\|\le4}d_\nu(z,t)\partial_z^\nu.$$ For each $j,k\ge0$, the rescaled coefficients satisfy $$\label{glue:mismatch-coeff} b^{4-\|\nu\|+j+4k} \lvert \partial_z^j\partial_t^k d_\nu\rvert\le C_{j,k}b^2.$$ Finally, near $r=0$, $$\label{glue:error-order} E_c=O_{\mathrm{sc}}(b^{-4}).$$ Away from a fixed neighborhood of $r=0$, all these background quantities extend smoothly to $t=0$ with bounds of every finite order.
  - Explanation: The profile and linearized coefficients have controlled scaled derivatives. The compact-to-flat mismatch gains a positive spatial-scale factor, while the compact residual has a weaker singular order and extends smoothly away from the core.
- **claim-bounded-ancient-source-extension** (construction, [source](#stmt-glue-extension)): There is a real linear extension $\mathcal E_i:Y_\alpha\to Y_\alpha(\mathbb{R}^m\times(-\infty,0))$, uniformly bounded for $T\le\beta^4$, which equals $\chi_i f$ in flat coordinates for $-T\le t<0$, and is supported spatially in $r\le2\beta$.
  - Explanation: A localized compact source extends to the ancient flat cylinder with uniform weighted Hölder control. The extension agrees across the lower time face and preserves spatial support, reality, and radial symmetry.
- **claim-uniform-compact-right-inverse** (construction, [source](#stmt-glue-inverse)): There are $\beta_0,\eta_0,C_D>0$ such that, for $0<\beta\le\beta_0$ and $0<T\le\eta_0\beta^4$, the operator $\mathcal P$ has a real invariant right inverse $D:Y_\alpha\to X_\alpha$ with $\lVert D\rVert\le C_D$. No initial value is prescribed for $Df$.
  - Explanation: The localized ancient inverse and outer zero-data solution combine into a compact parametrix. Exact cancellation of the inner cutoff commutator leaves small errors that a Neumann series removes.
- **claim-small-inverse-applied-to-compact-residual** (bound, [source](#stmt-glue-special-error)): For the right inverses of Proposition <a href="#glue:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:inverse">22</a>, $$\label{glue:DE-small} \lVert DE_c\rVert_{X'_\alpha} \le C\left(\beta^{2-2\delta} +\eta^{(\alpha'-\alpha)/4} +\eta^{(1-\alpha)/4}\right).$$ In particular this norm becomes arbitrarily small by choosing $\beta$ and then $\eta=T/\beta^4$ sufficiently small.
  - Explanation: Although the compact inverse need not have small operator norm, its value on the specific background residual is small in the spatial parabolic norm. Core localization and a higher source Hölder exponent supply the separate small factors.
- **claim-uniform-certified-finite-shooting** (bound, [source](#stmt-cert-shoot)): For every parameter $z_*\in[-R,R]^2$, where $R=5\cdot10^{-10}$, let $(k,d)$ be given by <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a>, and let $p$ be the origin solution of <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> supplied by Lemma <a href="#prof:origin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:origin">7</a>. This solution extends to $0\le s\le S_0=56$ and satisfies $$1+p(s)>0.$$ For the coordinates of its jet relative to $p_{\rm a}$, defined in <a href="#prof:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:coordinates">[prof:coordinates]</a>, one has $$\label{cert:conclusion} \|q\|\le .25R,\qquad \big\\|(\Re\xi,\Im\xi)-z_*\big\\|_\infty\le .13R.$$
  - Explanation: Validated finite integration and nonlinear parameter estimates control the entire shooting square. Every shot reaches the matching point with positivity and with stable and growing coordinates within the tolerances needed for matching.

        "anchor": "geo:potential-flow",
        "exact_quote": "Let $I$ be an open time interval, and let $H\\in C^\\infty(I\\times[0,1])$ satisfy $$\\label{geo:positivity}\n b(t,x):=1+v_*(x)H(t,x)>0.$$ Define $$\\label{geo:momentum}\n v=v_*+v_*^2H=v_*b,\\qquad\n K(H)=w^{-1}(wv_*^2H)'',\\qquad\n \\mathcal AH=\\big[w^{-1}(wv_*^2H)''\\big]''.$$ At each time these data, together with an affine choice in the Legendre potential, determine a smooth $U(n)$-invariant Kähler form in $[\\omega_*]$. Its scalar curvature and average scalar curvature are $$\\label{geo:scalar}\n S(t,x)=n(n+1)-K(H)(t,x),\\qquad \\bar S=n(n+1).$$ If, in addition, $$\\label{geo:equation}\n H_t+(1+v_*H)^2\\mathcal AH=0,$$ then for any $t_0\\in I$ and any initial affine choice there is a smooth family of these forms satisfying the Calabi flow exactly. More precisely, its global potential relative to $\\omega(t_0)$ satisfies $$\\label{geo:potential-flow}\n \\phi(t_0)=0,\\qquad\n \\omega(t)=\\omega(t_0)+i\\partial\\bar\\partial\\phi(t),\\qquad\n \\phi_t=S(\\omega(t))-\\bar S.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-352.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-geo-reduction",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-geo-reduction</summary>\n\n\n\n*Proof.* We first verify smoothness and positivity at both endpoints. Suppress time for this part of the argument. Since $$\\label{geo:smooth-legendre}\n \\frac1v-\\frac1{v_*}=-\\frac{H}{1+v_*H},$$ we can choose $\\psi\\in C^\\infty[0,1]$ with second derivative equal to the right-hand side, and put $u=u_*+\\psi$. The two integration constants are precisely its free affine part. Write $a=\\psi'$. The function $u'$ is strictly increasing from $-\\infty$ to $+\\infty$, so it has a smooth inverse on the open interval. In terms of $q=\\exp\\ell$, the inverse relation is $$\\label{geo:center-inverse}\n q=\\frac{x}{1-x}\\exp a(x).$$ At $x=0$ its right-hand side vanishes and has derivative $\\exp a(0)>0$. The inverse therefore extends smoothly to $q=0$, with $$x(q)=\\exp(-a(0))q+O(q^2).$$ Here and below a smooth remainder is meant: for example the last error is $q^2$ times a smooth function of $q$. Since $\\mathrm{d}\\Phi/\\mathrm{d}q=x(q)/q$, integration shows that the Legendre dual extends smoothly as a function of $q$, with expansion $$\\label{geo:center-expansion}\n \\Phi(\\log q)=C_0+\\exp(-a(0))q+O(q^2).$$ Division by $q$ here preserves smoothness because $x(0)=0$, as is also clear from $x(q)/q=\\int_0^1x'(sq)\\,\\mathrm{d}s$. Its complex Hessian at $Z=0$ is $\\exp(-a(0))$ times the Euclidean Hermitian form, and is positive.\n\n\nAt the other endpoint set $\\eta=q^{-1}$ and $b_1=1-x$. The same inverse relation becomes $$\\eta=\\frac{b_1}{1-b_1}\\exp\\big(-a(1-b_1)\\big),\\qquad\n b_1(\\eta)=\\exp a(1)\\eta+O(\\eta^2).$$ If $\\Psi(\\eta)=\\Phi(\\log q)-\\log q$, then $\\Psi'(\\eta)=b_1(\\eta)/\\eta$. Consequently $$\\label{geo:infinity-expansion}\n \\Psi(\\eta)=C_1+\\exp a(1)\\eta+O(\\eta^2)$$ is smooth at $\\eta=0$. This expansion gives smoothness on the projective manifold, not merely along radial rays. Indeed, on the projective chart meeting the divisor at infinity given by $$W_0=1/Z_j,\\qquad W_i=Z_i/Z_j\\quad(i\\ne j),\\qquad\n B=1+\\sum_{i\\ne j}|W_i|^2,$$ we have $q=B/|W_0|^2$. After removing the pluriharmonic term $-\\log|W_0|^2$ from $\\Phi$, a local potential is $$\\label{geo:divisor-potential}\n \\log B+\\Psi\\!\\left(\\frac{|W_0|^2}{B}\\right).$$ On $W_0=0$ its tangential complex Hessian is the Fubini–Study metric of the divisor, its normal coefficient is $\\exp a(1)/B>0$, and its mixed coefficients vanish. This proves positivity there; for $n=1$ only the normal coefficient is present. On $0<x<1$, direct differentiation gives the tangential and radial eigenvalues $x/q$ and $v/q$, respectively, so positivity holds everywhere.\n\nThe difference $\\Phi(\\log|Z|^2)-\\log(1+|Z|^2)$ is a globally smooth function. At the origin this follows from <a href=\"#geo:center-expansion\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:center-expansion\">[geo:center-expansion]</a>; in the chart at infinity it equals $\\Psi(\\eta)-\\log(1+\\eta)$, which is smooth by <a href=\"#geo:infinity-expansion\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:infinity-expansion\">[geo:infinity-expansion]</a>. Thus its complex Hessian is a global exact form and the Kähler class is $[\\omega_*]$. All inversions and integrations above depend smoothly on time on compact subintervals of $I$, by the positive endpoint derivatives and the inverse function theorem with parameters.\n\nWe next compute scalar curvature. The eigenvalues already found give $$\\det(g_{i\\bar j})=q^{-n}x^{n-1}v=q^{-n}wv.$$ For any radial function $f$, the complex Laplacian is $$\\label{geo:complex-laplacian}\n \\Delta_\\omega f\n =\\frac{f_{\\ell\\ell}}{v}+\\frac{n-1}{x}f_\\ell\n =w^{-1}\\partial_x(wf_\\ell),$$ where $\\partial_\\ell=v\\partial_x$ in the last expression. For $f=-\\log\\det g$, this yields $$f_\\ell=n-\\frac{(wv)'}w,\\qquad\n S=\\frac{n(n-1)}x-w^{-1}(wv)''.$$ Substituting $v=v_*+v_*^2H$ gives <a href=\"#geo:scalar\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:scalar\">[geo:scalar]</a>. The expression $K(H)$ is smooth at zero: $wv_*^2H=x^{n+1}(1-x)^2H$, whose second derivative is divisible by $x^{n-1}$ as a smooth function. It is also smooth at one.\n\nFor completeness, the average can be read directly from this formula. The radial volume density is a constant multiple of $w(x)\\,\\mathrm{d}x$: the Euclidean polar factor $q^{n-1}\\mathrm{d}q$, multiplied by the determinant, becomes $wv\\,\\mathrm{d}\\ell=w\\,\\mathrm{d}x$. Moreover, $$\\int_0^1 K(H)w\\,\\mathrm{d}x\n =\\big[(wv_*^2H)'\\big]_{x=0}^{x=1}=0,$$ because $wv_*^2H$ vanishes to order at least two at both endpoints. This proves the asserted average, including when $n=1$.\n\n\n\nFinally, fix $t_0\\in I$, choose $u(t_0)$ as above, and define at fixed momentum coordinate $$\\label{geo:legendre-flow}\n u(t,x)=u(t_0,x)+\\int_{t_0}^{t}K(H)(s,x)\\,\\mathrm{d}s.$$ Equation <a href=\"#geo:equation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:equation\">[geo:equation]</a> implies $$\\partial_t(1/v)=-\\frac{v_*^2H_t}{v^2}\n =\\mathcal AH=\\partial_x^2K(H)=\\partial_tu''.$$ Their initial values agree, so $u''=1/v$ for all time. In particular the preceding metric construction applies throughout $I$. Differentiating the Legendre dual $\\Phi(t,\\ell)=x(t,\\ell)\\ell-u(t,x(t,\\ell))$ at fixed $\\ell$ cancels the terms containing $x_t$, and gives $$\\label{geo:legendre-sign}\n \\Phi_t=-u_t=-K(H)=S-\\bar S.$$ The difference between $\\Phi(t)$ and $\\Phi(t_0)$ is globally smooth by the endpoint argument and therefore supplies $\\phi$ in <a href=\"#geo:potential-flow\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:potential-flow\">[geo:potential-flow]</a>.\n\nThis also specifies the affine part of $u$ during evolution. Replacing a fixed $u$ by $u+\\alpha x+\\beta$ changes its dual to $\\Phi(\\ell-\\alpha)-\\beta$, corresponding to the projective dilation $Z\\mapsto\\exp(-\\alpha/2)Z$ and a potential constant. An arbitrary time-dependent choice of these terms would affect <a href=\"#geo:legendre-sign\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:legendre-sign\">[geo:legendre-sign]</a>; the prescription <a href=\"#geo:legendre-flow\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:legendre-flow\">[geo:legendre-flow]</a> fixes them and establishes that equation without an additional affine term. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "Let $I$ be an open time interval, and let $H\\in C^\\infty(I\\times[0,1])$ satisfy $$\\label{geo:positivity}\n b(t,x):=1+v_*(x)H(t,x)>0.$$ Define $$\\label{geo:momentum}\n v=v_*+v_*^2H=v_*b,\\qquad\n K(H)=w^{-1}(wv_*^2H)'',\\qquad\n \\mathcal AH=\\big[w^{-1}(wv_*^2H)''\\big]''.$$ At each time these data, together with an affine choice in the Legendre potential, determine a smooth $U(n)$-invariant Kähler form in $[\\omega_*]$. Its scalar curvature and average scalar curvature are $$\\label{geo:scalar}\n S(t,x)=n(n+1)-K(H)(t,x),\\qquad \\bar S=n(n+1).$$ If, in addition, $$\\label{geo:equation}\n H_t+(1+v_*H)^2\\mathcal AH=0,$$ then for any $t_0\\in I$ and any initial affine choice there is a smooth family of these forms satisfying the Calabi flow exactly. More precisely, its global potential relative to $\\omega(t_0)$ satisfies $$\\label{geo:potential-flow}\n \\phi(t_0)=0,\\qquad\n \\omega(t)=\\omega(t_0)+i\\partial\\bar\\partial\\phi(t),\\qquad\n \\phi_t=S(\\omega(t))-\\bar S.$$",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-geo-operators",
        "exact_quote": "This identification is a bijection between $C^\\infty[0,1]$ and the smooth invariant functions on $\\Sigma$. The pushforward of spherical volume under $x$ is a positive constant multiple of $wv_*^2\\,\\mathrm{d}x$. With the nonpositive Laplacian convention, put $L=\\frac14\\Delta_\\Sigma$. On invariant functions, $$\\label{geo:sphere-operator}\n L=x(1-x)\\partial_x^2+\\big(n+2-(n+5)x\\big)\\partial_x,\n \\qquad\n \\mathcal A=(-L+n+3)(-L+2(n+2)).$$ The right-hand expression defines a smooth elliptic operator of order four on all of $\\Sigma$.*\n\n\n\n*In the core coordinates $(z,\\theta)\\mapsto(z,\\sqrt{1-|z|^2}\\,\\theta)$, with $\\theta\\in S^5$, its Laplacian on functions independent of $\\theta$ is $$\\label{geo:local-laplacian}\n \\Delta_\\Sigma\n =\\Delta_z-\\sum_{i,j=1}^m z_i z_j\\partial_{z_i z_j}\n             -(m+5)\\sum_{i=1}^m z_i\\partial_{z_i}.$$ The flat momentum operator obtained by replacing $v_*$ by $x$ in <a href=\"#geo:momentum\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:momentum\">[geo:momentum]</a> satisfies $$\\label{geo:flat-operator}\n \\widehat\\mathcal AH\n :=\\big[x^{1-n}(x^{n+1}H)''\\big]''\n =\\big(x\\partial_x^2+(n+2)\\partial_x\\big)^2H\n =\\frac1{16}\\Delta_{\\mathbb{R}^m}^2H,\\qquad x=|z|^2.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-352.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-geo-operators",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-geo-operators</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The only regularity issue in the identification occurs at the two singular orbits. Here is an elementary smoothness argument. If $f(r)$ is smooth and even near zero, then the operator $$\\mathcal T f(r)=\\frac{f'(r)}r\n              =\\int_0^1f''(sr)\\,\\mathrm{d}s$$ defines another smooth even function. Thus, for $g(x)=f(\\sqrt x)$, each derivative on $x>0$ has the form $g^{(j)}(x)=2^{-j}(\\mathcal T^jf)(\\sqrt x)$, and extends continuously to $x=0$. Induction gives smoothness up to zero of $g$ and all its derivatives. The same argument holds with smooth parameters. Restricting a smooth invariant function on $\\Sigma$ to $(re_1,\\sqrt{1-r^2}\\,\\theta_0)$ gives such an even $f$, and invariance makes its value depend only on $x=r^2$. Interchanging the two factors proves smoothness in $1-x$ at the other endpoint. Conversely a smooth function of $x$ is smooth in these coordinates at both orbits, as well as on their complement.\n\nWrite $|z|=\\sin\\vartheta$, with $0\\leq\\vartheta\\leq\\pi/2$. The spherical metric and volume density are $$\\mathrm{d}\\vartheta^2+\\sin^2\\vartheta\\,g_{S^{m-1}}\n                 +\\cos^2\\vartheta\\,g_{S^5},\\qquad\n \\sin^{m-1}\\vartheta\\cos^5\\vartheta\\,\\mathrm{d}\\vartheta\\,\n       \\mathrm{d}\\omega_{m-1}\\,\\mathrm{d}\\omega_5.$$ Changing to $x=\\sin^2\\vartheta$ gives density proportional to $$M(x)\\,\\mathrm{d}x:=x^{m/2-1}(1-x)^2\\,\\mathrm{d}x\n              =wv_*^2\\,\\mathrm{d}x.$$ The radial Laplacian computed from this metric gives the displayed formula for $L$, equivalently $L=M^{-1}\\partial_x(v_*M\\partial_x)$.\n\nWe verify the factorization by a polynomial argument that also checks the endpoint terms. Both $L$ and $\\mathcal A$ preserve polynomial degree and are symmetric for the inner product with density $M$. For $\\mathcal A$, twice integrating by parts gives $$\\label{geo:symmetry}\n \\int_0^1 a(\\mathcal Ab)M\\,\\mathrm{d}x\n =\\int_0^1\\frac{(Ma)''(Mb)''}{w}\\,\\mathrm{d}x.$$ There are no boundary terms: $Ma$ and its first derivative vanish at each endpoint, and $w^{-1}(Mb)''$ is smooth there. For $L$ symmetry follows from its divergence expression, since $v_*M$ vanishes at both ends. Orthogonalizing the monomials therefore gives common polynomial eigenfunctions. On the polynomial of degree $j$, the leading coefficients give respectively the eigenvalues $$-j(j+n+4),\\qquad\n (j+1)(j+2)(j+n+2)(j+n+3).$$ Indeed a symmetric degree-preserving operator maps the degree-$j$ orthogonal polynomial to a multiple of itself, because its inner product with every lower-degree polynomial vanishes. Finally, $$\\big(j(j+n+4)+n+3\\big)\n \\big(j(j+n+4)+2(n+2)\\big)\n =(j+1)(j+n+3)(j+2)(j+n+2).$$ This proves equality on every polynomial, hence equality of the differential expressions: at an interior point polynomials realize arbitrary jets through order four. The sphere expression has leading term $\\Delta_\\Sigma^2/16$, proving the claimed ellipticity.\n\nFor <a href=\"#geo:local-laplacian\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:local-laplacian\">[geo:local-laplacian]</a>, the inverse metric in the $z$ coordinates is $(\\delta_{ij}-z_i z_j)$ and the volume density is $(1-|z|^2)^2\\,\\mathrm{d}z\\,\\mathrm{d}\\omega_5$. Applying the divergence formula therefore gives $$(1-|z|^2)^{-2}\\partial_{z_i}\n \\big((1-|z|^2)^2(\\delta_{ij}-z_i z_j)\\partial_{z_j}\\big)\n =(\\delta_{ij}-z_i z_j)\\partial_{z_i z_j}\n   -(m+5)z_i\\partial_{z_i},$$ with repeated indices summed. This computation only requires independence of $\\theta$. Finally both one-dimensional expressions for $\\widehat\\mathcal A$ in <a href=\"#geo:flat-operator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:flat-operator\">[geo:flat-operator]</a> expand to $$x^2H^{(4)}+2(n+3)xH^{(3)}+(n+2)(n+3)H'',$$ and the radial Euclidean identity $\\frac14\\Delta_{\\mathbb{R}^m}=x\\partial_x^2+\\frac m2\\partial_x$ proves the last equality. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "This identification is a bijection between $C^\\infty[0,1]$ and the smooth invariant functions on $\\Sigma$. The pushforward of spherical volume under $x$ is a positive constant multiple of $wv_*^2\\,\\mathrm{d}x$. With the nonpositive Laplacian convention, put $L=\\frac14\\Delta_\\Sigma$. On invariant functions, $$\\label{geo:sphere-operator}\n L=x(1-x)\\partial_x^2+\\big(n+2-(n+5)x\\big)\\partial_x,\n \\qquad\n \\mathcal A=(-L+n+3)(-L+2(n+2)).$$ The right-hand expression defines a smooth elliptic operator of order four on all of $\\Sigma$.*\n\n\n\n*In the core coordinates $(z,\\theta)\\mapsto(z,\\sqrt{1-|z|^2}\\,\\theta)$, with $\\theta\\in S^5$, its Laplacian on functions independent of $\\theta$ is $$\\label{geo:local-laplacian}\n \\Delta_\\Sigma\n =\\Delta_z-\\sum_{i,j=1}^m z_i z_j\\partial_{z_i z_j}\n             -(m+5)\\sum_{i=1}^m z_i\\partial_{z_i}.$$ The flat momentum operator obtained by replacing $v_*$ by $x$ in <a href=\"#geo:momentum\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:momentum\">[geo:momentum]</a> satisfies $$\\label{geo:flat-operator}\n \\widehat\\mathcal AH\n :=\\big[x^{1-n}(x^{n+1}H)''\\big]''\n =\\big(x\\partial_x^2+(n+2)\\partial_x\\big)^2H\n =\\frac1{16}\\Delta_{\\mathbb{R}^m}^2H,\\qquad x=|z|^2.$$",
      "explanation": "The auxiliary sphere converts endpoint regularity into ordinary invariant smoothness. Its factored elliptic operator agrees with the compact momentum operator, while the flat radial operator is a multiple of the Euclidean biharmonic operator.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-geo-operators",
          "exact_quote": "With the nonpositive Laplacian convention, put $L=\\frac14\\Delta_\\Sigma$.",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "geo:sphere",
          "exact_quote": "$$\\label{geo:sphere}\n m=2n+4,\\qquad\n \\Sigma=S^{m+5}\\subset\\mathbb{R}^m\\times\\mathbb{R}^6,\\qquad x=|z|^2.$$ We identify functions of $x$ with $O(m)\\times O(6)$-invariant functions on $\\Sigma$.",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "claim-sphere-and-flat-operator-identities",
      "kind": "theorem",
      "quantifiers": [],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-geo-operators"
    },
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "geo:curvature-rate",
        "exact_quote": "Let $G\\in C^\\infty[0,\\infty)$ satisfy $G(0)=1$ and $G'(0)\\ne0$, and define $F$ by <a href=\"#geo:selfsimilar\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:selfsimilar\">[geo:selfsimilar]</a>. Suppose that for some $T>0$ and $\\delta>0$ there is a smooth invariant solution $H=F+V$ of <a href=\"#geo:equation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:equation\">[geo:equation]</a> on $(-T,0)\\times\\Sigma$ such that $1+v_*H>0$ and $$\\label{geo:correction-bound}\n V(t,0)=O(\\rho^{-1+\\delta})\\qquad(t\\uparrow0).$$ Then a smooth $U(10)$-invariant metric $\\omega_0\\in[\\omega_*]$ has maximal smooth Calabi-flow existence interval $[0,T_*)$, where $T_*=T/2<\\infty$. At the fixed affine origin $p$, the scalar curvature of this flow satisfies $$\\label{geo:curvature-rate}\n S(\\tau,p)=a(T_*-\\tau)^{-1/2}\n       +O\\big(1+(T_*-\\tau)^{(-1+\\delta)/2}\\big),\\qquad\n a=-110G'(0)\\ne0.$$ In particular $S(\\tau,p)\\sim a(T_*-\\tau)^{-1/2}$, and $a>0$ when $G'(0)<0$.",
        "source_id": "PM-352.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-geo-obstruction",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-geo-obstruction</summary>\n\n*Proof.* By Lemma <a href=\"#geo:operators\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geo:operators\">4</a>, $H$ is smooth in $x$ at both endpoints for each time. Proposition <a href=\"#geo:reduction\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geo:reduction\">3</a>, with $t_0=-T/2$, gives a smooth family of Kähler forms, and its slice $\\omega_0=\\omega(-T/2)$ is a smooth positive form in the Fubini–Study class. Put $\\tau=t+T/2$. The corresponding global potential satisfies exactly the normalization and evolution in <a href=\"#geo:potential-flow\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:potential-flow\">[geo:potential-flow]</a> on $0\\leq\\tau<T_*=T/2$.\n\n\nAt $x=0$, the coefficient of $x^{n+1}$ in $wv_*^2H$ is $H(t,0)$. Thus <a href=\"#geo:scalar\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:scalar\">[geo:scalar]</a> gives the exact identity $$\\label{geo:center-scalar}\n S(t,p)=n(n+1)\\big(1-H(t,0)\\big).$$ This evaluates scalar curvature at the same point $p$ at every time: <a href=\"#geo:center-inverse\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:center-inverse\">[geo:center-inverse]</a> sends $q=0$ to $x=0$ for every affine choice. Since $$H(t,0)=\\rho^{-1}G'(0)+O(\\rho^{-1+\\delta}),$$ Equation <a href=\"#geo:center-scalar\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:center-scalar\">[geo:center-scalar]</a> proves <a href=\"#geo:curvature-rate\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:curvature-rate\">[geo:curvature-rate]</a> and the asserted divergence.\n\nWe recall why uniqueness applies to comparison with an unrestricted smooth flow; no symmetry hypothesis is needed for this step. Smooth positive initial potentials have a unique local Calabi flow by (Chen and He 2008, Theorem 3.2) [[ChenHe2008](#ref-ChenHe2008)]. The uniqueness mechanism can also be seen directly. For two smooth solutions with the same initial potential, let $\\chi$ be their difference and interpolate their potentials by $\\varphi_s$, $0\\leq s\\leq1$. Their interpolated Kähler forms remain positive, and variation of scalar curvature gives $$\\chi_t=-\\int_0^1\n \\left(\\Delta_{\\varphi_s}^2\\chi\n       +\\operatorname{Ric}_{\\varphi_s}^{i\\bar j}\n                            \\partial_i\\partial_{\\bar j}\\chi\\right)\n \\,\\mathrm{d}s.$$ On a compact time interval where both solutions are smooth, the coefficients are smooth and uniformly elliptic. Relative to a fixed smooth volume form, integration twice by parts and interpolation give $$\\frac{\\mathrm{d}}{\\mathrm{d}t}\\|\\chi\\|_{L^2}^2\n \\leq-c\\|\\chi\\|_{H^2}^2+C\\|\\chi\\|_{L^2}^2\n \\leq C\\|\\chi\\|_{L^2}^2.$$ For this estimate, the principal quadratic form is the integral of $|\\Delta_{\\varphi_s}\\chi|^2$; differentiating the smooth volume density introduces terms bounded by $C\\|\\chi\\|_{H^1}\\|\\chi\\|_{H^2}$, and the Ricci term has the same or lower order. The elliptic estimate for each uniformly elliptic Laplacian and $\\|\\chi\\|_{H^1}\\leq\\varepsilon\\|\\chi\\|_{H^2}+C_\\varepsilon\\|\\chi\\|_{L^2}$ absorb those terms. Gronwall’s inequality proves uniqueness.\n\nIt follows that any smooth flow from $\\omega_0$ agrees with the one constructed above on their common interval. Hence its maximal interval contains $[0,T_*)$. If it extended beyond $T_*$, smooth positivity on the compact manifold across that time would bound its scalar curvature, contradicting <a href=\"#geo:curvature-rate\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:curvature-rate\">[geo:curvature-rate]</a>. Its maximal smooth existence time is therefore exactly $T_*$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $G\\in C^\\infty[0,\\infty)$ satisfy $G(0)=1$ and $G'(0)\\ne0$, and define $F$ by <a href=\"#geo:selfsimilar\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:selfsimilar\">[geo:selfsimilar]</a>. Suppose that for some $T>0$ and $\\delta>0$ there is a smooth invariant solution $H=F+V$ of <a href=\"#geo:equation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:equation\">[geo:equation]</a> on $(-T,0)\\times\\Sigma$ such that $1+v_*H>0$ and $$\\label{geo:correction-bound}\n V(t,0)=O(\\rho^{-1+\\delta})\\qquad(t\\uparrow0).$$ Then a smooth $U(10)$-invariant metric $\\omega_0\\in[\\omega_*]$ has maximal smooth Calabi-flow existence interval $[0,T_*)$, where $T_*=T/2<\\infty$. At the fixed affine origin $p$, the scalar curvature of this flow satisfies $$\\label{geo:curvature-rate}\n S(\\tau,p)=a(T_*-\\tau)^{-1/2}\n       +O\\big(1+(T_*-\\tau)^{(-1+\\delta)/2}\\big),\\qquad\n a=-110G'(0)\\ne0.$$ In particular $S(\\tau,p)\\sim a(T_*-\\tau)^{-1/2}$, and $a>0$ when $G'(0)<0$.",
      "explanation": "A positive compact solution whose correction is lower order at the origin yields a genuine smooth Calabi flow with finite maximal lifetime. The origin derivative of the profile determines the sign and leading coefficient of curvature divergence.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prof-profile",
        "exact_quote": "There exist a constant $c>0$ and a function $G\\in C^\\infty([0,\\infty))$ such that $$\\label{prof:profile-equation}\n Q(y\\partial_y)(G-1)=\\frac{y^2}{2}\\left(\\frac1G-\\frac1c\\right),\n \\qquad Q(D)=(D-1)(D+9)(D+10).$$ The function $G$ is bounded above and bounded away from zero, satisfies $G(0)=1$ and $G'(0)<0$, and is analytic near zero. There are real coefficients $g_j$, $j\\ge1$, such that, for every $J,\\ell\\ge0$, $$\\label{prof:expansion}\n \\frac{\\mathrm{d}^\\ell}{\\mathrm{d}y^\\ell}\n \\left(G(y)-c-\\sum_{j=1}^{J}g_jy^{-2j}\\right)\n =O\\bigl(y^{-2(J+1)-\\ell}\\bigr)\\qquad(y\\longrightarrow\\infty).$$ In particular, $u\\mapsto G(u^{-1/2})$ extends smoothly to $u=0$ from the right, with value $c$.*\n\n\n*Set $h(y)=(G(y)-1)/y$, with its smooth value at zero. For $t<0$, the function $$\\label{prof:flat-shrinker}\n F(t,x)=\\rho^{-1}h(x/\\rho),\\qquad \\rho=\\sqrt{-t},$$ solves $$F_t+(1+xF)^2\\widehat{\\mathcal A}F=0,\n \\qquad \\widehat{\\mathcal A}=(x\\partial_x^2+12\\partial_x)^2.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-352.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-13",
          "exact_quote": "#### Matching the two solutions\n\nFor a parameter $z_*\\in\\mathcal Z$, apply Lemma <a href=\"#prof:tail\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prof:tail\">10</a> using the value $d(z_*)$ and the coordinate $q_{\\rm shot}(z_*)$ supplied by the finite shot. This is allowed by $|q_{\\rm shot}|\\le .25R<.26R$. Define a map on the closed square by $$\\mathcal B(z_*)=z_*\n -\\bigl(\\Re(\\xi_{\\rm shot}-\\xi_{\\rm tail}),\n        \\Im(\\xi_{\\rm shot}-\\xi_{\\rm tail})\\bigr).$$ It is continuous by the continuous dependence established above and satisfies $$\\lVert \\mathcal B(z_*)\\rVert_\\infty\\le(.13+.25)R=.38R<R.$$ Brouwer’s fixed point theorem gives $z_*\\in\\mathcal Z$ with $\\xi_{\\rm shot}=\\xi_{\\rm tail}$. The real coordinate and the parameter $d$ already agree. The three jet entries in <a href=\"#prof:coordinates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:coordinates\">[prof:coordinates]</a> therefore agree, and ordinary uniqueness for <a href=\"#prof:ode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:ode\">[prof:ode]</a> joins the shot and tail to one solution on $[0,\\infty)$.\n\nThe finite shot has $1+p>0$, and the tail has $|p|<.001$. Compactness of the finite interval and the uniform tail bound imply that $1+p$ is bounded and bounded away from zero globally. Since $1+d>0$, the function $G$ in <a href=\"#prof:change-variables\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:change-variables\">[prof:change-variables]</a> has the same properties. Near zero, $$s^{3/2}=\\frac{\\sqrt{27}}{4c}y,\n \\qquad G(y)=1+\\frac{\\sqrt{27}}4ky+O(y^2).$$ It is therefore analytic there, with $G'(0)=k\\sqrt{27}/4<0$ by <a href=\"#prof:parameter-displacement\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:parameter-displacement\">[prof:parameter-displacement]</a>. This proves all assertions of Theorem <a href=\"#prof:profile\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prof:profile\">6</a> except for the differentiable expansion, which we now establish independently of the finite shooting estimates.",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prof-weighted-remainder-2",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prof-weighted-remainder</summary>\n\n*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href=\"#prof:profile\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prof:profile\">6</a>.* Finally $s^{-3}=(16/27)c^2y^{-2}$, so <a href=\"#prof:s-expansion\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:s-expansion\">[prof:s-expansion]</a> and the chain rule give <a href=\"#prof:expansion\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:expansion\">[prof:expansion]</a>, with $g_j=c\\,d_j((16/27)c^2)^j$. Repeated differentiation of $u\\mapsto G(u^{-1/2})$ expresses each derivative as a finite sum of powers of $y$ times derivatives of $G$. Subtracting sufficiently many terms in <a href=\"#prof:expansion\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:expansion\">[prof:expansion]</a> shows that these derivatives have their Taylor limits at $u=0$. The resulting extension is $C^\\infty$. The origin expansion gives the smooth extension of $h$, and the calculation in Section <a href=\"#geo:core-section\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geo:core-section\">2.2</a> proves the flat equation in Theorem <a href=\"#prof:profile\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prof:profile\">6</a>, also at $x=0$ by smoothness. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There exist a constant $c>0$ and a function $G\\in C^\\infty([0,\\infty))$ such that $$\\label{prof:profile-equation}\n Q(y\\partial_y)(G-1)=\\frac{y^2}{2}\\left(\\frac1G-\\frac1c\\right),\n \\qquad Q(D)=(D-1)(D+9)(D+10).$$ The function $G$ is bounded above and bounded away from zero, satisfies $G(0)=1$ and $G'(0)<0$, and is analytic near zero. There are real coefficients $g_j$, $j\\ge1$, such that, for every $J,\\ell\\ge0$, $$\\label{prof:expansion}\n \\frac{\\mathrm{d}^\\ell}{\\mathrm{d}y^\\ell}\n \\left(G(y)-c-\\sum_{j=1}^{J}g_jy^{-2j}\\right)\n =O\\bigl(y^{-2(J+1)-\\ell}\\bigr)\\qquad(y\\longrightarrow\\infty).$$ In particular, $u\\mapsto G(u^{-1/2})$ extends smoothly to $u=0$ from the right, with value $c$.*\n\n\n*Set $h(y)=(G(y)-1)/y$, with its smooth value at zero. For $t<0$, the function $$\\label{prof:flat-shrinker}\n F(t,x)=\\rho^{-1}h(x/\\rho),\\qquad \\rho=\\sqrt{-t},$$ solves $$F_t+(1+xF)^2\\widehat{\\mathcal A}F=0,\n \\qquad \\widehat{\\mathcal A}=(x\\partial_x^2+12\\partial_x)^2.$$",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prof-origin",
        "exact_quote": "For $|k-k_0|\\le .1$ and $|d-d_0|\\le .1$ in $\\mathbb{C}$, there is a unique solution germ of <a href=\"#prof:ode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:ode\">[prof:ode]</a>, analytic in $r=s^{3/2}$, of the form $$\\label{prof:origin-series}\n p=d+kr+\\sum_{i\\ge2}a_ir^i,\\qquad r=s^{3/2}.$$ The series is jointly holomorphic in $r,k,d$ for $|r|<3$ and satisfies $$\\label{prof:origin-majorant}\n 3|k|+\\sum_{i\\ge2}|a_i|3^i<.8.$$ Each of $p(1),p'(1),p''(1)$ has modulus at most $2$ on this parameter polydisc. For the real parameters in <a href=\"#prof:parameters\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:parameters\">[prof:parameters]</a>, $1+p>0$ on $0\\le s\\le1$.",
        "source_id": "PM-352.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prof-origin",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prof-origin</summary>\n\n\n*Proof.* In the variable $r=s^{3/2}$, <a href=\"#prof:ode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:ode\">[prof:ode]</a> is equivalent to $$Q(r\\partial_r)(p-d)=\\frac8{27}r^2\\bigl((1+p)^{-1}-1\\bigr).$$ Consequently the coefficients of an analytic solution must satisfy $$\\label{prof:origin-recurrence}\n a_i=\n \\frac{[r^{i-2}]\\bigl((1+p)^{-1}-1\\bigr)}\n {(27/8)(i-1)(i+9)(i+10)},\\qquad i\\ge2.$$ The constant and linear equations leave $d,k$ free, and this recurrence determines every subsequent coefficient uniquely.\n\nFor convergence, use the Banach algebra of power series with norm $\\sum_i|b_i|3^i$. On the closed ball of series $a$ with no constant or linear term and norm at most $.2$, one has $$\\lVert kr+a\\rVert<.585+.2<.8,\\qquad |1+d|>1.30.$$ Thus inversion of $1+d+kr+a$ is given by a convergent geometric series in that Banach algebra and has norm at most $2$. The map specified by <a href=\"#prof:origin-recurrence\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:origin-recurrence\">[prof:origin-recurrence]</a> sends this ball into the ball of radius $9(2+1)/440<.062$, since every denominator in that formula exceeds $440$. Its Lipschitz constant is at most $9\\cdot 2^2/440<1$. Iteration therefore gives a unique fixed point, jointly holomorphic in the parameters. The strict inequalities also hold on a neighborhood of the closed parameter polydisc. Moreover $$3|k|+\\lVert a\\rVert\n \\le 3(.094617116666041+.1)+\\frac{27}{440}<.8,$$ which proves <a href=\"#prof:origin-majorant\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:origin-majorant\">[prof:origin-majorant]</a>.\n\nFor $0\\le r\\le1$, the nonconstant sum is at most $.8r/3$. In particular the real parameters in <a href=\"#prof:parameters\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:parameters\">[prof:parameters]</a> give $1+p>1.4-.8/3>0$. At $s=1$, a term $a_is^{3i/2}$ acquires factors $3i/2$ and $(3i/2)(3i/2-1)$ under one and two derivatives. The elementary bounds $$\\sup_{i\\ge1}\\frac{3i/2}{3^i}=\\frac12,\n \\qquad\n \\sup_{i\\ge1}\\frac{(3i/2)(3i/2-1)}{3^i}=\\frac23$$ together with <a href=\"#prof:origin-majorant\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:origin-majorant\">[prof:origin-majorant]</a> and $|d|<.501$ give the stated jet bounds. Termwise differentiation is valid since $1<3$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For $|k-k_0|\\le .1$ and $|d-d_0|\\le .1$ in $\\mathbb{C}$, there is a unique solution germ of <a href=\"#prof:ode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:ode\">[prof:ode]</a>, analytic in $r=s^{3/2}$, of the form $$\\label{prof:origin-series}\n p=d+kr+\\sum_{i\\ge2}a_ir^i,\\qquad r=s^{3/2}.$$ The series is jointly holomorphic in $r,k,d$ for $|r|<3$ and satisfies $$\\label{prof:origin-majorant}\n 3|k|+\\sum_{i\\ge2}|a_i|3^i<.8.$$ Each of $p(1),p'(1),p''(1)$ has modulus at most $2$ on this parameter polydisc. For the real parameters in <a href=\"#prof:parameters\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:parameters\">[prof:parameters]</a>, $1+p>0$ on $0\\le s\\le1$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prof-root-bounds",
        "exact_quote": "The roots in <a href=\"#prof:coordinates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:coordinates\">[prof:coordinates]</a> are distinct and satisfy $$\\label{prof:root-boxes}\n \\begin{split}\n -1.08659&<\\lambda_0<-1.08655,\\\\\n .40933&<\\Re\\lambda_1<.40938,\\qquad\n .8671<\\Im\\lambda_1<.86721.\n \\end{split}$$ Every entry of $\\Gamma-\\Lambda$ has modulus less than $.001$. The absolute row sums of $\\Gamma$ are less than $.92,.68,.68$, respectively. With $$\\label{prof:convolution-constants}\n M_i=\\sum_{j=0}^2\n \\frac{|\\lambda_j|^i}{|\\Re\\lambda_j|\\,|D_0'(\\lambda_j)|},\\qquad 0\\le i\\le2,$$ one has $$M_0<1.96,\\qquad M_1<1.92,\\qquad M_2<1.88,\n \\qquad \\frac1{\\Re\\lambda_1|D_0'(\\lambda_1)|}<.82.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-352.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prof-root-bounds",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prof-root-bounds</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Write $\\alpha=15/56$, $\\beta=B/(2\\cdot56^2)$ and $H_0=1+C/(2\\cdot56^3)$. The exact calculations $$\\alpha^2-3\\beta=-\\frac{453}{25088}<0,\\qquad\n D_0(-1.08659)=-\\frac{17566501197647}{343000000000000000}<0,$$ $$D_0(-1.08655)=\\frac{187622583137}{2744000000000000}>0$$ show that $D_0$ is strictly increasing on the real line and has exactly one real root in the indicated interval. Write $\\lambda_0=-r_0$ and $\\lambda_1=u+iv$ with $v>0$. Vieta’s identities give $$\\label{prof:root-arithmetic}\n u=\\frac{r_0-\\alpha}{2},\\qquad\n v^2=\\frac{H_0}{r_0}-u^2,\\qquad |\\lambda_1|^2=\\frac{H_0}{r_0}.$$ In particular, if $u_-=114617/280000$ and $u_+=573113/1400000$, then $u_-<u<u_+$, with $.40933<u_-$ and $u_+<.40938$. The rational inequalities $$.8671^2<\\frac{H_0}{1.08659}-u_+^2\n \\le v^2\\le\\frac{H_0}{1.08655}-u_-^2<.86721^2,\n \\qquad \\frac{H_0}{1.08655}<.959^2$$ give the other root bounds and $|\\lambda_1|<.959$.\n\nFor each root, the corresponding complex projection row is $$\\label{prof:projection-row}\n \\frac{(\\lambda_j\\lambda_k,-\\lambda_j-\\lambda_k,1)}\n {D_0'(\\lambda_i)},\\qquad \\{i,j,k\\}=\\{0,1,2\\}.$$ Taking the real row for $i=0$ and the real and imaginary parts for $i=1$ gives $\\Gamma$. Put $E=(r_0+u)^2+v^2$. The three real rows are $$\\begin{gathered}\n \\left(\\frac{u^2+v^2}{E},\\frac{-2u}{E},\\frac1E\\right),\\\\\n \\left(\\frac{r_0(r_0+2u)}{2E},\\frac uE,\\frac{-1}{2E}\\right),\\\\\n \\left(\\frac{r_0[u(r_0+u)-v^2]}{2vE},\n       \\frac{-(v^2+r_0^2-u^2)}{2vE},\\frac{-(r_0+u)}{2vE}\\right).\n \\end{gathered}$$ Substituting $r_0\\in[1.08655,1.08659]$ and the intervals just obtained for $u,v,v^2$ gives the following rational enclosures. For each entry, products are enclosed by the minimum and maximum of the four endpoint products, and positive reciprocals by reversing the reciprocal endpoints.\n\n<div class=\"center\">\n\n|          |    First entry    |   Second entry    |    Third entry    |\n|:--------:|:-----------------:|:-----------------:|:-----------------:|\n|   $q$    |  $(.3074,.3078)$  | $(-.2740,-.2737)$ |  $(.3344,.3346)$  |\n| $\\Re\\xi$ |  $(.3461,.3464)$  |  $(.1368,.1370)$  | $(-.1673,-.1672)$ |\n| $\\Im\\xi$ | $(-.0294,-.0291)$ | $(-.3406,-.3402)$ | $(-.2887,-.2883)$ |\n\n</div>\n\nThe entrywise error from $\\Lambda$ is therefore less than $.001$, and the row sums are less than $.9164,.6507,.6587$, respectively.\n\nFinally, $$E>(1.08655+u_-)^2+.8671^2\n   =\\frac{46876434629}{15680000000}>2.9895>1.729^2.$$ Since $D_0'(-r_0)=E$ and $|D_0'(u+iv)|=2v\\sqrt E$, for $i=0,1,2$ one has $$M_i<\\frac{1.08659^i}{1.08655\\cdot2.9895}\n      +\\frac{.959^i}{.40933\\cdot.8671\\cdot1.729}.$$ These three rational expressions are less than $1.96,1.92,1.88$, respectively, by multiplication by their positive denominators. Similarly, $$\\frac1{\\Re\\lambda_1|D_0'(\\lambda_1)|}\n <\\frac1{2\\cdot.40933\\cdot.8671\\cdot1.729}\n =\\frac{500000000000}{613674044347}<.82.$$ ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "The roots in <a href=\"#prof:coordinates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:coordinates\">[prof:coordinates]</a> are distinct and satisfy $$\\label{prof:root-boxes}\n \\begin{split}\n -1.08659&<\\lambda_0<-1.08655,\\\\\n .40933&<\\Re\\lambda_1<.40938,\\qquad\n .8671<\\Im\\lambda_1<.86721.\n \\end{split}$$ Every entry of $\\Gamma-\\Lambda$ has modulus less than $.001$. The absolute row sums of $\\Gamma$ are less than $.92,.68,.68$, respectively. With $$\\label{prof:convolution-constants}\n M_i=\\sum_{j=0}^2\n \\frac{|\\lambda_j|^i}{|\\Re\\lambda_j|\\,|D_0'(\\lambda_j)|},\\qquad 0\\le i\\le2,$$ one has $$M_0<1.96,\\qquad M_1<1.92,\\qquad M_2<1.88,\n \\qquad \\frac1{\\Re\\lambda_1|D_0'(\\lambda_1)|}<.82.$$",
      "explanation": "Exact root isolation establishes the stable and growing directions used in the tail construction. Projection and convolution bounds then control the Green representation quantitatively.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [
        {
          "anchor": "prof:coordinates",
          "exact_quote": "Define the four-term approximation $$\\label{prof:asymptotic-polynomial}\n \\begin{gathered}\n p_{\\rm a}(s;d)=\\sum_{j=1}^4d_js^{-3j},\\qquad\n a=d_1=Cd,\\qquad b=d_2=a^2+567a,\\\\\n d_3=2ab-a^3+\\frac{2025}{4}b,\\qquad\n d_4=b^2+2ad_3-3a^2b+a^4+\\frac{567}{2}d_3.\n \\end{gathered}$$ We choose the constant coefficients in $D_0$ at the midpoints of the corresponding linearized tail ranges on $[S_0,\\infty)$, giving the uniform difference bounds used in Lemma <a href=\"#prof:tail\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prof:tail\">10</a>. Let $$\\label{prof:coordinates}\n \\begin{gathered}\n D_0(\\lambda)=\\lambda^3+\\frac{15}{S_0}\\lambda^2\n       +\\frac{B}{2S_0^2}\\lambda+1+\\frac{C}{2S_0^3},\\\\\n v_i=(1,\\lambda_i,\\lambda_i^2)^t,\\qquad\n \\begin{pmatrix}p-p_{\\rm a}\\\\p'-p_{\\rm a}'\\\\p''-p_{\\rm a}''\\end{pmatrix}_{s=S_0}\n       =qv_0+\\xi v_1+\\bar\\xi v_2.\n \\end{gathered}$$ The roots are ordered so that $\\lambda_0$ is real, $\\Im\\lambda_1>0$, and $\\lambda_2=\\bar\\lambda_1$. The exact real projection matrix $\\Gamma$ takes a real jet to $(q,\\Re\\xi,\\Im\\xi)^t$. Its rational approximation used in the finite certificate is $$\\label{prof:projection-approximation}\n \\Lambda=10^{-5}\n \\begin{pmatrix}\n 30756&-27384&33447\\\\\n 34622&13692&-16724\\\\\n -2925&-34040&-28850\n \\end{pmatrix}.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }
      ],

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prof-tail-residual",
        "exact_quote": "Uniformly for the parameters in <a href=\"#prof:parameters\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:parameters\">[prof:parameters]</a>, $$\\sup_{s\\ge56}|\\mathcal E_d(p_{\\rm a})(s)|<3\\cdot10^{-13}.$$ Each derivative of this residual tends to zero at infinity. If $P_{\\rm a}(d)=(p_{\\rm a},p_{\\rm a}',p_{\\rm a}'')(56;d)$, then $$\\label{prof:tail-parameter-bound}\n \\lVert P_{\\rm a}(d)-P_{\\rm a}(d_0)\\rVert_\\infty\\le .01|d-d_0|.$$",

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prof-tail-residual",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prof-tail-residual</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Put $t=s^{-3}$. For a monomial $d_js^{-3j}$, the differential part $p'''+30s^{-1}p''+Bs^{-2}p'+Cs^{-3}p$ contributes $L(-3j)d_jt^{j+1}$, where $$L(\\nu)=\\nu(\\nu-1)(\\nu-2)+30\\nu(\\nu-1)+B\\nu+C.$$ Its first four relevant values are $$L(-3)=-567,\\quad L(-6)=-2025/4,\\quad\n L(-9)=-567/2,\\quad L(-12)=-243/4.$$ Using $p/(1+p)=p-p^2/(1+p)$ shows that the coefficients through $t^4$ cancel exactly for <a href=\"#prof:asymptotic-polynomial\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:asymptotic-polynomial\">[prof:asymptotic-polynomial]</a>.\n\n\nThe parameter interval <a href=\"#prof:parameter-displacement\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:parameter-displacement\">[prof:parameter-displacement]</a> is contained in $[.40068272185,.40068272187]$. Substituting this rational interval in <a href=\"#prof:asymptotic-polynomial\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:asymptotic-polynomial\">[prof:asymptotic-polynomial]</a> and its $d$-derivative gives $$\\begin{array}{c|rrrr}\n j&1&2&3&4\\\\ \\hline\n |d_j|<&122&54200&12500000&5100000000\\\\\n |\\partial_d d_j|<&304&100000&65000000&100000000000.\n\\end{array}$$ These interval bounds use just the endpoint operations described in Lemma <a href=\"#prof:root-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prof:root-bounds\">8</a>; the coefficient formulas are polynomials. The first row in particular implies $$\\label{prof:tail-term-bounds}\n |d_1|56^{-3}<.00070,\\quad |d_2|56^{-6}<1.8\\cdot10^{-6},\\quad\n |d_3|56^{-9}<8.1\\cdot10^{-9},\\quad |d_4|56^{-12}<3.2\\cdot10^{-11}.$$ Here is a finite positive-coefficient way to check the residual bound. Let $b=(.00070,1.8\\cdot10^{-6},8.1\\cdot10^{-9},3.2\\cdot10^{-11})$ and $B_*(z)=\\sum_{i=1}^4b_iz^i$. If $T_*=\\sum b_i$, the sums of the coefficients of degrees greater than four in its second, third, and fourth powers are, respectively, $$T_*^2-b_1^2-2b_1b_2-2b_1b_3-b_2^2,\\qquad\n T_*^3-b_1^3-3b_1^2b_2,\\qquad T_*^4-b_1^4.$$ Substitution of the displayed four rational numbers gives $$\\label{prof:residual-arithmetic}\n \\sum_{j>4}[z^j]B_*(z)^2<8\\cdot10^{-14},\\qquad\n \\sum_{j>4}[z^j]B_*(z)^3<2\\cdot10^{-14},\\qquad\n \\sum_{j>4}[z^j]B_*(z)^4<3\\cdot10^{-15}.$$ Since $B_*(1)<.001$, all powers at least five contribute at most $.001^5/(1-.001)$. The remaining linear term contributes at most $(243/4)(3.2\\cdot10^{-11})/56^3$. Thus the residual is bounded by $$8\\cdot10^{-14}+2\\cdot10^{-14}+3\\cdot10^{-15}\n +\\frac{.001^5}{1-.001}\n +\\frac{(243/4)(3.2\\cdot10^{-11})}{56^3}\n <3\\cdot10^{-13}.$$ These bounds hold for all $s\\ge56$ because every remaining absolute monomial decreases there. The residual is a rational function of $s^{-3}$, analytic at zero with vanishing constant term, which also proves the assertion about its derivatives. For the parameter estimate, the factors $3j/56$ and $3j(3j+1)/56^2$ are at most one for $1\\le j\\le4$. Thus the second row of the coefficient table gives $$\\lVert \\partial_dP_{\\rm a}\\rVert_\\infty\n \\le\\frac{304}{56^3}+\\frac{100000}{56^6}\n       +\\frac{65000000}{56^9}+\\frac{100000000000}{56^{12}}\n <.001735<.01.$$ The mean value theorem yields <a href=\"#prof:tail-parameter-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:tail-parameter-bound\">[prof:tail-parameter-bound]</a>. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Uniformly for the parameters in <a href=\"#prof:parameters\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:parameters\">[prof:parameters]</a>, $$\\sup_{s\\ge56}|\\mathcal E_d(p_{\\rm a})(s)|<3\\cdot10^{-13}.$$ Each derivative of this residual tends to zero at infinity. If $P_{\\rm a}(d)=(p_{\\rm a},p_{\\rm a}',p_{\\rm a}'')(56;d)$, then $$\\label{prof:tail-parameter-bound}\n \\lVert P_{\\rm a}(d)-P_{\\rm a}(d_0)\\rVert_\\infty\\le .01|d-d_0|.$$",

          "exact_quote": "The affine square below makes the shot’s two remaining coordinates close to its parameter $z_*$. Define $$\\label{prof:parameters}\n \\begin{gathered}\n R=5\\cdot10^{-10},\\qquad\n \\mathcal Z=\\{z_*\\in\\mathbb{R}^2:\\lVert z_*\\rVert_\\infty\\le R\\},\\qquad S_0=56,\\\\\n \\begin{pmatrix}k\\\\d\\end{pmatrix}\n =\\begin{pmatrix}-.094617116666041\\\\ .400682721856639\\end{pmatrix}\n +10^{-8}\n \\begin{pmatrix}25190&-63731\\\\721855&359157\\end{pmatrix}z_*.\n \\end{gathered}$$ Write $k_0,d_0$ for the central values. The square implies $$\\label{prof:parameter-displacement}\n |k-k_0|\\le4.44605\\cdot10^{-13},\\qquad\n |d-d_0|\\le5.40506\\cdot10^{-12}<.011R.$$",

        "anchor": "stmt-prof-tail",
        "exact_quote": "For each $d$ arising in <a href=\"#prof:parameters\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:parameters\">[prof:parameters]</a> and each real $|q|\\le .26R$, there is a solution $p_{\\rm tail}$ of <a href=\"#prof:ode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:ode\">[prof:ode]</a> on $[56,\\infty)$ such that $$|p_{\\rm tail}|<.001,\n \\qquad q_{\\rm tail}=q,\\qquad |\\xi_{\\rm tail}|<.25R.$$ Its first three jet entries tend to zero at infinity. The solution, as a bounded jet, and its initial coordinates depend continuously on $q,d$.",
        "source_id": "PM-352.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prof-tail",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prof-tail</summary>\n\n\n*Proof.* Let $b_i=D_0'(\\lambda_i)^{-1}$ and let $j$ be a bounded continuous real function on $[S_0,\\infty)$. Define a vector $\\mathcal V=(V,V',V'')^t$ by the convergent integrals $$\\label{prof:green-formula}\n \\begin{split}\n \\mathcal V(s)={}&q\\mathrm{e}^{\\lambda_0(s-S_0)}v_0\n  +b_0v_0\\int_{S_0}^s\\mathrm{e}^{\\lambda_0(s-u)}j(u)\\,\\mathrm{d}u\\\\\n &-\\sum_{i=1}^2 b_iv_i\\int_s^\\infty\\mathrm{e}^{\\lambda_i(s-u)}j(u)\\,\\mathrm{d}u.\n \\end{split}$$ Indeed this vector solves the companion system for $D_0(\\partial_s)V=j$: the identities $\\sum b_i=\\sum\\lambda_ib_i=0$ and $\\sum\\lambda_i^2b_i=1$ follow by partial fractions of $1/D_0$. They show both the derivative relations between its entries and the source in the last component. The vector is real by complex conjugation. It is the unique bounded solution with the prescribed real-mode coordinate at $S_0$, since a bounded homogeneous solution has no growing nonreal modes.\n\n\nFor $0\\le i\\le2$, Lemma <a href=\"#prof:root-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prof:root-bounds\">8</a> gives $$\\label{prof:green-bound}\n \\lVert V^{(i)}\\rVert_\\infty\\le |\\lambda_0|^i|q|+M_i\\lVert j\\rVert_\\infty.$$ In particular, on the closed ball $\\lVert j\\rVert_\\infty\\le .3R$, the first entry satisfies $\\lVert V\\rVert_\\infty\\le(.26+1.96\\cdot.3)R=.848R$. Together with <a href=\"#prof:tail-term-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:tail-term-bounds\">[prof:tail-term-bounds]</a>, this ensures $|p_{\\rm a}+V|<.001$ throughout the half-line.\n\n\nWrite $N(p)=p^2/(1+p)$ and $e_{\\rm a}=\\mathcal E_d(p_{\\rm a})$. Then $p=p_{\\rm a}+V$ solves <a href=\"#prof:ode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:ode\">[prof:ode]</a> exactly when $$\\label{prof:tail-map}\n \\begin{split}\n j=\\mathcal T_{q,d}j:={}&-e_{\\rm a}\n -\\left(\\frac{30}{s}-\\frac{15}{S_0}\\right)V''\n -\\left(\\frac B{s^2}-\\frac B{2S_0^2}\\right)V'\\\\\n &-\\left(\\frac C{s^3}-\\frac C{2S_0^3}\\right)V\n +N(p_{\\rm a}+V)-N(p_{\\rm a}).\n \\end{split}$$ For $|p|\\le.001$, $$|N'(p)|=|1-(1+p)^{-2}|<.0021.$$ The three coefficient differences in <a href=\"#prof:tail-map\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:tail-map\">[prof:tail-map]</a> have absolute bounds $15/S_0$, $B/(2S_0^2)$ and $|C|/(2S_0^3)$ on the entire half-line. It follows from <a href=\"#prof:green-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:green-bound\">[prof:green-bound]</a> that the Lipschitz constant of $\\mathcal T_{q,d}$ on the ball is at most $$\\label{prof:tail-contraction-constant}\n \\frac{15}{56}(1.88)+\\frac{B}{2\\cdot56^2}(1.92)\n +\\left(\\frac{|C|}{2\\cdot56^3}+.0021\\right)(1.96)<.57.$$ The corresponding coefficient of the homogeneous term is bounded by $$\\label{prof:tail-homogeneous-constant}\n \\frac{15}{56}(1.08659)^2+\\frac{B}{2\\cdot56^2}(1.08659)\n +\\frac{|C|}{2\\cdot56^3}+.0021<.36.$$ Consequently $$\\lVert \\mathcal T_{q,d}j\\rVert_\\infty\n \\le3\\cdot10^{-13}+.36|q|+.57\\lVert j\\rVert_\\infty\n \\le(.0006+.36\\cdot.26+.57\\cdot.3)R=.2652R<.3R.$$ The contraction theorem gives a unique fixed point in this ball. Equation <a href=\"#prof:green-formula\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:green-formula\">[prof:green-formula]</a> then gives a $C^3$ solution, and <a href=\"#prof:ode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:ode\">[prof:ode]</a> bootstraps it to a smooth solution for $s>0$. Its nonreal initial coordinate satisfies $$\\xi_{\\rm tail}=-b_1\\int_{S_0}^\\infty\n                  \\mathrm{e}^{\\lambda_1(S_0-u)}j(u)\\,\\mathrm{d}u,\n \\qquad |\\xi_{\\rm tail}|<.82(.3R)=.246R<.25R.$$\n\nAll coefficients in <a href=\"#prof:tail-map\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:tail-map\">[prof:tail-map]</a> depend continuously on $d$ in the relevant uniform norms, and the homogeneous vector depends continuously on $q$. The common contraction constant therefore gives continuous dependence of the fixed point, the bounded jet, and its initial coordinates. Finally, exponential convolutions of continuous functions tending to zero again tend to zero. Thus <a href=\"#prof:tail-map\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:tail-map\">[prof:tail-map]</a> preserves the closed subspace of such functions; the residual tends to zero and all its other coefficients are bounded. Iteration from $j=0$ shows that the fixed point belongs to this subspace. Formula <a href=\"#prof:green-formula\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:green-formula\">[prof:green-formula]</a> proves $p,p',p''\\to0$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-352.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For each $d$ arising in <a href=\"#prof:parameters\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:parameters\">[prof:parameters]</a> and each real $|q|\\le .26R$, there is a solution $p_{\\rm tail}$ of <a href=\"#prof:ode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:ode\">[prof:ode]</a> on $[56,\\infty)$ such that $$|p_{\\rm tail}|<.001,\n \\qquad q_{\\rm tail}=q,\\qquad |\\xi_{\\rm tail}|<.25R.$$ Its first three jet entries tend to zero at infinity. The solution, as a bounded jet, and its initial coordinates depend continuously on $q,d$.",
      "explanation": "A contraction constructs bounded tail solutions with a prescribed stable coordinate and controlled growing coordinates. Continuous dependence makes this family suitable for topological matching.",

Together with the tail bound, the certified estimate yields a continuous self-map of the square; Brouwer’s theorem supplies a matching point. All terminating decimals in this section denote exact rational numbers. Define $$\\label{prof:parameters}\n \\begin{gathered}\n R=5\\cdot10^{-10},\\qquad\n \\mathcal Z=\\{z_*\\in\\mathbb{R}^2:\\lVert z_*\\rVert_\\infty\\le R\\},\\qquad S_0=56,\\\\\n \\begin{pmatrix}k\\\\d\\end{pmatrix}\n =\\begin{pmatrix}-.094617116666041\\\\ .400682721856639\\end{pmatrix}\n +10^{-8}\n \\begin{pmatrix}25190&-63731\\\\721855&359157\\end{pmatrix}z_*.\n \\end{gathered}$$ Write $k_0,d_0$ for the central values. The square implies $$\\label{prof:parameter-displacement}\n |k-k_0|\\le4.44605\\cdot10^{-13},\\qquad\n |d-d_0|\\le5.40506\\cdot10^{-12}<.011R.$$",
