Define a map on the closed square by $$\\mathcal B(z_*)=z_*\n -\\bigl(\\Re(\\xi_{\\rm shot}-\\xi_{\\rm tail}),\n        \\Im(\\xi_{\\rm shot}-\\xi_{\\rm tail})\\bigr).$$ It is continuous by the continuous dependence established above and satisfies $$\\lVert \\mathcal B(z_*)\\rVert_\\infty\\le(.13+.25)R=.38R<R.$$ Brouwer’s fixed point theorem gives $z_*\\in\\mathcal Z$ with $\\xi_{\\rm shot}=\\xi_{\\rm tail}$. The real coordinate and the parameter $d$ already agree. The three jet entries in <a href=\"#prof:coordinates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:coordinates\">[prof:coordinates]</a> therefore agree, and ordinary uniqueness for <a href=\"#prof:ode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:ode\">[prof:ode]</a> joins the shot and tail to one solution on $[0,\\infty)$.\n\nThe finite shot has $1+p>0$, and the tail has $|p|<.001$. Compactness of the finite interval and the uniform tail bound imply that $1+p$ is bounded and bounded away from zero globally. Since $1+d>0$, the function $G$ in <a href=\"#prof:change-variables\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"prof:change-variables\">[prof:change-variables]</a> has the same properties.           "anchor": "stmt-glue-inverse",
          "quote": "There are $\\beta_0,\\eta_0,C_D>0$ such that, for $0<\\beta\\le\\beta_0$ and $0<T\\le\\eta_0\\beta^4$, the operator $\\mathcal P$ has a real invariant right inverse $D:Y_\\alpha\\to X_\\alpha$ with $\\lVert D\\rVert\\le C_D$. No initial value is prescribed for $Df$.",

          "anchor": "stmt-flat-inverse",
          "quote": "There is a number $\\delta\\in(0,1)$ such that, for every fixed $\\alpha\\in(0,1)$, the operator <a href=\"#flat:operator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"flat:operator\">[flat:operator]</a> has a bounded real linear right inverse $$\\widehat D:Y\\longrightarrow X,\\qquad\n \\widehat{\\mathcal P}\\widehat D f=f,\\qquad\n \\|\\widehat D f\\|_X\\le C\\|f\\|_Y,\n \\qquad p_0=-2+2\\delta.$$ Here $Y$ has weight $p_0-4$ and parabolic exponent $\\alpha$, and $X$ has weight $p_0$ and includes the parabolic $\\alpha$ norms of all spatial derivatives through order four and of the first time derivative. The solution is radial and classical on $t<0$. Its initial values are selected by the construction. The constant depends only on the fixed profile, $\\delta$, $\\alpha$, and the fixed choices in the local norms.",
          "quote_sha256": "2a47e464ee44089aa93b6fa0b494988dfb224b49c4c7ef9ee66339299bb4c933"

        {
          "anchor": "flat:recursion-solution",
          "quote": "Let the one-step source increments be $$l_j=\\int_0^1S(1-s)f^\\#(j+s)\\,\\mathrm{d}s,\\qquad j\\in\\mathbb{Z}.$$ Then $\\|l_j\\|_E\\le CMq^j$. Define $$\\label{flat:recursion-solution}\n v_j=\\sum_{k=0}^\\infty P_s^k\\Pi_s l_{j-1-k}\n       -\\sum_{k=0}^\\infty P_u^{-k-1}\\Pi_u l_{j+k}.$$ Both series converge absolutely for every integer $j$. More explicitly, their norms are at most $$CMq^j\\left[\n   q^{-1}\\sum_{k=0}^\\infty(r_s/q)^k\n       +r_u^{-1}\\sum_{k=0}^\\infty(q/r_u)^k\\right]\n \\le C'Mq^j.$$ Shifting the summation indices proves $v_{j+1}=Pv_j+l_j$. On $[j,j+1]$ define $v$ by <a href=\"#flat:duhamel\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"flat:duhamel\">[flat:duhamel]</a> with initial value $v_j$. The definitions agree at the endpoints and give $$\\label{flat:preliminary-bound}\n \\|v(\\tau)\\|_E\\le CM e^{-\\gamma\\tau},\\qquad\n |V(z,t)|\\le CM\\rho^{-1+\\delta}.$$ The continuous joining creates no distributional time-boundary term, so <a href=\"#flat:similarity-equation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"flat:similarity-equation\">[flat:similarity-equation]</a> holds across every joined face. Interior regularity makes it a classical equation there. The construction is linear and real on real sources. The second sum in <a href=\"#flat:recursion-solution\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"flat:recursion-solution\">[flat:recursion-solution]</a> selects the outside spectral components using future forcing; its use is permitted precisely because no initial values have been imposed.",

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct a smooth Kähler metric in the Fubini–Study class of $`\mathbb{CP}^{10}`$ whose Calabi flow develops unbounded scalar curvature in finite time. This disproves Chen's smooth long-time existence conjecture, even in a class containing a constant-scalar-curvature metric.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:calabi-flow"></a>
Let $\omega_0$ be a smooth Kähler form on a compact complex manifold $M$. We use the scalar-curvature convention $S(\omega)=\operatorname{tr}_\omega\operatorname{Ric}(\omega)$. The Calabi flow is $$\label{eq:calabi-flow}
 \omega(t)=\omega_0+i\partial\bar\partial\phi(t)>0,
 \qquad \phi(0)=0,\qquad
 \partial_t\phi=S(\omega(t))-\bar S,$$ where $\bar S$ is the average scalar curvature, determined by the Kähler class. Calabi introduced this flow to seek canonical metrics within a Kähler class (Calabi 1982) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Calabi1982](#ref-Calabi1982)]. Chen’s smooth long-time existence conjecture asserts that the flow exists for all positive times from arbitrary smooth Kähler initial data; see (He and Zeng 2021, Conjecture 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[HeZeng2021](#ref-HeZeng2021)]. We give a counterexample on projective space. Write $\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}$ for the Fubini–Study form whose potential on the standard affine chart is $\log(1+|Z|^2)$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a smooth $U(10)$-invariant Kähler form $\omega_0$ on $\mathbb{CP}^{10}$, in the class $[\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}]$, whose maximal smooth Calabi flow has existence interval $[0,T_*)$ with $0<T_*<\infty$. There are a point $p\in\mathbb{CP}^{10}$ and a constant $a>0$ such that $$S(\omega(t))(p)\sim a(T_*-t)^{-1/2}\qquad\text{as }t\uparrow T_*.$$*

</div>

The Fubini–Study metric has constant scalar curvature. Thus the example also answers negatively the smooth global-existence question for classes containing a constant-scalar-curvature Kähler metric, emphasized by Chen and Cheng (Chen and Cheng 2021, 912, following Conjecture 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[ChenCheng2021](#ref-ChenCheng2021)]. In particular, no assertion of smooth global existence and subsequent smooth convergence modulo holomorphic automorphisms can hold for arbitrary smooth initial metrics in such a class: the obstruction already occurs at a finite time.

The same singularity persists on products with a stationary factor.

<a id="stmt-cor-product-singularities"></a>
<div id="cor:product-singularities" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Products with stationary factors). *Let $\omega(t)$, $T_*$, $p$, and $a$ be as in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, and let $(Y,\eta)$ be a compact connected Kähler manifold with $\eta$ of constant scalar curvature. With $\pi_X$ and $\pi_Y$ the projections from $\mathbb{CP}^{10}\times Y$, the maximal smooth Calabi flow from $\Omega_0=\pi_X^*\omega_0+\pi_Y^*\eta$ is $$\Omega(t)=\pi_X^*\omega(t)+\pi_Y^*\eta,
 \qquad 0\leq t<T_*.$$ For every $y\in Y$, $S(\Omega(t))(p,y)\sim a(T_*-t)^{-1/2}$. In particular, finite-time smooth Calabi-flow singularities occur in every complex dimension $n\geq10$, on $\mathbb{CP}^{10}\times\mathbb{CP}^{n-10}$, in a class containing a Kähler–Einstein metric.*

</div>

The corollary is proved after Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> in Section <a href="#sec:compact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compact">[sec:compact]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Background and significance

On compact Riemann surfaces, Chruściel proved global forward existence and convergence to a constant-curvature metric (Chruściel 1991, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Chrusciel1991](#ref-Chrusciel1991)]. Chen gave a different proof using decreasing functionals and concentration compactness (Chen 2001) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Chen2001](#ref-Chen2001)], and Struwe developed a unified treatment of Calabi and normalized Ricci flows on closed surfaces (Struwe 2002) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Struwe2002](#ref-Struwe2002)]. In higher dimensions, Chen and He proved local existence and uniqueness and established stability near a constant-scalar-curvature metric (Chen and He 2008, Theorems 1.2 and 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[ChenHe2008](#ref-ChenHe2008)]. He and Zeng developed short-time theory for rougher initial metrics (He and Zeng 2021) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[HeZeng2021](#ref-HeZeng2021)]. These higher-dimensional results concern either short times or restricted initial data.

Curvature estimates give continuation criteria for the smooth flow. Chen and He proved extension under a uniform Ricci-curvature bound (Chen and He 2008, Corollary 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[ChenHe2008](#ref-ChenHe2008)], and Chen and Cheng established extension under a uniform scalar-curvature bound (Chen and Cheng 2021, Theorem 1.8) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[ChenCheng2021](#ref-ChenCheng2021)]. Li, Zhang and Zheng state that a uniform spatial $L^p$ scalar-curvature bound, measured with respect to the evolving metric, suffices when $p>\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M$ (Li, Zhang, and Zheng 2024, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[LiZhangZheng2024](#ref-LiZhangZheng2024)]; Li and Zhang state a further criterion involving spacetime scalar and scalar-Laplacian integrability (Li and Zhang 2025, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[LiZhang2025](#ref-LiZhang2025)].

On a compact almost homogeneous manifold with two hypersurface ends, Guan proved exponential smooth convergence of the modified Calabi flow for initial metrics invariant under a maximal compact subgroup of the automorphism group (Guan 2007, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Guan2007](#ref-Guan2007)]. The two special orbits of the action used here are instead a point and the hyperplane at infinity. The point has complex codimension ten, so the two-hypersurface hypothesis fails.

Streets constructed global weak Calabi flows by minimizing movements (Streets 2014) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Streets2014](#ref-Streets2014)]. Berman, Darvas and Lu identify these flows with trajectories in a space of finite-energy Kähler potentials and show that, when the class contains a constant-scalar-curvature metric, every weak trajectory converges to a smooth constant-scalar-curvature potential in the $d_1$ distance, the extension of the $L^1$ path-length distance on smooth Kähler potentials (Berman, Darvas, and Lu 2017, sec. 6 and Theorem 1.11) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BermanDarvasLu2017](#ref-BermanDarvasLu2017)]. The weak and classical flows agree for as long as the latter exists smoothly (Berman, Darvas, and Lu 2017, Proposition 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[BermanDarvasLu2017](#ref-BermanDarvasLu2017)]. Neither this weak continuation nor the large-time $d_1$ convergence implies smooth continuation through every finite time.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Structure of the construction

The construction first reduces the flow to a scalar equation, then builds an exact shrinking solution of its flat limit, and finally corrects that solution on the compact manifold. The geometric starting point is the radial ansatz of Calabi (Calabi 1979) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Calabi1979](#ref-Calabi1979)] and the momentum formulation of Hwang and Singer (Hwang and Singer 2002) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[HwangSinger2002](#ref-HwangSinger2002)]. Abreu’s toric scalar-curvature formula (Abreu 1998) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Abreu1998](#ref-Abreu1998)] is expressed in Guillemin’s symplectic-potential framework (Guillemin 1994) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Guillemin1994](#ref-Guillemin1994)]. For a radial potential $\Phi(\ell)$, where $\ell=\log|Z|^2$, set $x=\Phi'(\ell)$ and $v=\Phi''(\ell)$. We use $x\in(0,1)$ as the momentum coordinate and write $$v=x(1-x)+x^2(1-x)^2H.$$ Smoothness and positivity at both special orbits are encoded by $H\in C^\infty[0,1]$ and $1+x(1-x)H>0$. Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">[sec:geometry]</a> derives the scalar fourth-order equation for $H$, including the affine choice in the Legendre potential needed to recover the Calabi flow with its prescribed normalization. It also realizes the fixed spatial operator on an auxiliary sphere, where the endpoint conditions become ordinary smooth invariance.

The singularity is detected by one value of $H$. At the fixed affine origin $p$, the scalar-curvature identity is $$S(t,p)=110\bigl(1-H(t,0)\bigr).$$ We construct a smooth positive profile $G$, bounded above and away from zero, with $G(0)=1$ and $G'(0)<0$, and set $$F(t,x)=\rho^{-1}h(x/\rho),\qquad
 h(y)=\frac{G(y)-1}{y},\qquad
 \rho=\sqrt{-t},\quad t<0.$$ Then $F(t,0)=\rho^{-1}G'(0)$. Our task is to find an exact compact solution $H=F+V$ for which positivity persists and $$V(t,0)=O(\rho^{-1+\delta})\qquad\text{for some }\delta>0.$$ The correction is therefore smaller than the leading term at the origin, preserving the desired scalar-curvature divergence. A smooth interior time slice of this solution will be the initial metric; no initial metric needs to be fixed before the construction.

Section <a href="#sec:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:profile">[sec:profile]</a> constructs $G$ so that $F$ solves the scale-invariant flat equation exactly. The profile is obtained by matching a two-parameter analytic solution at the origin to a half-line solution approaching a positive constant. A finite exact-arithmetic calculation, with analytic enclosure and nonlinear error bounds, certifies the matching inequalities. This uses the framework of validated Taylor integration surveyed in (Nedialkov, Jackson, and Corliss 1999) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[NedialkovJacksonCorliss1999](#ref-NedialkovJacksonCorliss1999)]. The finite argument and its complete executable certificate appear in the appendices. The resulting profile also has a differentiable expansion at infinity; this expansion controls the compact error away from the shrinking core.

The correction begins with a right inverse for the flat linearized equation. Section <a href="#sec:parabolic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:parabolic">4</a> introduces the weighted norms and local estimates, and Section <a href="#sec:flat" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flat">5</a> constructs this inverse on all negative times. In similarity variables its time-one evolution is a compact perturbation of a damped constant-coefficient evolution. A spectral split determines finitely many components from future forcing. This choice is available because the initial metric is still free; it requires no stability or nondegeneracy theorem for the profile. The compact-perturbation and spectral-projection tools are classical (Kato 1995) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Kato1995](#ref-Kato1995)]. After selecting a spectral weight, an outer annulus estimate upgrades the temporal supremum bound to the full spatially weighted parabolic Hölder estimate. The decisive input there is a scale-invariant $L^2$ bound that is integrable up to the singular time.

Section <a href="#sec:compact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compact">[sec:compact]</a> combines this ancient inverse near the core with a short-time forward solution outside it. The outer forcing cancels the inner cutoff commutator exactly; the remaining operator errors are small. For the nonlinear correction one further needs the compact residual to become small after applying the inverse. This holds in a norm controlling all four spatial derivatives and their temporal Hölder seminorms, with the time derivative omitted. The outer estimate uses a strictly higher Hölder exponent for the source, and the fixed-point equation then supplies the missing time derivative. Positivity and the pointwise correction bound above give the geometric hypotheses needed to finish Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="geo:section"></a>
### The geometric reduction and the finite-time obstruction

<span id="sec:geometry" label="sec:geometry"></span>

We use scalar curvature in the convention $S(\omega)=\operatorname{tr}_{\omega}\operatorname{Ric}(\omega)$. Let $\omega_*$ be the Fubini–Study form on $\mathbb{CP}^n$ with affine potential $\log(1+|Z|^2)$. The geometric identities below hold for every $n\geq1$; the construction will use $n=10$. The radial and momentum descriptions follow the framework of Calabi (Calabi 1979) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Calabi1979](#ref-Calabi1979)] and Hwang–Singer (Hwang and Singer 2002) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[HwangSinger2002](#ref-HwangSinger2002)]; the Legendre-coordinate viewpoint is also central to the toric formulas of Guillemin and Abreu (Guillemin 1994; Abreu 1998) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Guillemin1994](#ref-Guillemin1994), [Abreu1998](#ref-Abreu1998)]. We give the identities directly to fix the scalar convention and to verify smoothness at the point orbit as well as at the divisor. Set <a id="geo:reference"></a>$$\label{geo:reference}
 q=|Z|^2,\qquad \ell=\log q,\qquad
 v_*(x)=x(1-x),\qquad w(x)=x^{n-1}.$$ For a radial potential $\Phi(\ell)$, its momentum coordinate and momentum profile are $$x=\Phi'(\ell),\qquad v(x)=\Phi''(\ell).$$ The Legendre transform $u(x)=x\ell-\Phi(\ell)$ satisfies $u'=\ell$ and $u''=1/v$. The reference transform is $$u_*(x)=x\log x+(1-x)\log(1-x),\qquad u_*''=v_*^{-1}.$$ Primes on functions of $x$ will denote differentiation in $x$.

<a id="stmt-geo-reduction"></a>
<div id="geo:reduction" class="proposition">

<a id="geo:momentum"></a>
<a id="geo:scalar"></a>
<a id="geo:equation"></a>
<a id="geo:potential-flow"></a>
**Proposition 3** (Exact fixed-class reduction). *Let $I$ be an open time interval, and let $H\in C^\infty(I\times[0,1])$ satisfy <a id="geo:positivity"></a>$$\label{geo:positivity}
 b(t,x):=1+v_*(x)H(t,x)>0.$$ Define $$\label{geo:momentum}
 v=v_*+v_*^2H=v_*b,\qquad
 K(H)=w^{-1}(wv_*^2H)'',\qquad
 \mathcal AH=\big[w^{-1}(wv_*^2H)''\big]''.$$ At each time these data, together with an affine choice in the Legendre potential, determine a smooth $U(n)$-invariant Kähler form in $[\omega_*]$. Its scalar curvature and average scalar curvature are $$\label{geo:scalar}
 S(t,x)=n(n+1)-K(H)(t,x),\qquad \bar S=n(n+1).$$ If, in addition, $$\label{geo:equation}
 H_t+(1+v_*H)^2\mathcal AH=0,$$ then for any $t_0\in I$ and any initial affine choice there is a smooth family of these forms satisfying the Calabi flow exactly. More precisely, its global potential relative to $\omega(t_0)$ satisfies $$\label{geo:potential-flow}
 \phi(t_0)=0,\qquad
 \omega(t)=\omega(t_0)+i\partial\bar\partial\phi(t),\qquad
 \phi_t=S(\omega(t))-\bar S.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-reduction</summary>

<a id="geo:center-inverse"></a>
<a id="geo:center-expansion"></a>
*Proof.* We first verify smoothness and positivity at both endpoints. Suppress time for this part of the argument. Since <a id="geo:smooth-legendre"></a>$$\label{geo:smooth-legendre}
 \frac1v-\frac1{v_*}=-\frac{H}{1+v_*H},$$ we can choose $\psi\in C^\infty[0,1]$ with second derivative equal to the right-hand side, and put $u=u_*+\psi$. The two integration constants are precisely its free affine part. Write $a=\psi'$. The function $u'$ is strictly increasing from $-\infty$ to $+\infty$, so it has a smooth inverse on the open interval. In terms of $q=\exp\ell$, the inverse relation is $$\label{geo:center-inverse}
 q=\frac{x}{1-x}\exp a(x).$$ At $x=0$ its right-hand side vanishes and has derivative $\exp a(0)>0$. The inverse therefore extends smoothly to $q=0$, with $$x(q)=\exp(-a(0))q+O(q^2).$$ Here and below a smooth remainder is meant: for example the last error is $q^2$ times a smooth function of $q$. Since $\mathrm{d}\Phi/\mathrm{d}q=x(q)/q$, integration shows that the Legendre dual extends smoothly as a function of $q$, with expansion $$\label{geo:center-expansion}
 \Phi(\log q)=C_0+\exp(-a(0))q+O(q^2).$$ Division by $q$ here preserves smoothness because $x(0)=0$, as is also clear from $x(q)/q=\int_0^1x'(sq)\,\mathrm{d}s$. Its complex Hessian at $Z=0$ is $\exp(-a(0))$ times the Euclidean Hermitian form, and is positive.

<a id="geo:infinity-expansion"></a>
At the other endpoint set $\eta=q^{-1}$ and $b_1=1-x$. The same inverse relation becomes $$\eta=\frac{b_1}{1-b_1}\exp\big(-a(1-b_1)\big),\qquad
 b_1(\eta)=\exp a(1)\eta+O(\eta^2).$$ If $\Psi(\eta)=\Phi(\log q)-\log q$, then $\Psi'(\eta)=b_1(\eta)/\eta$. Consequently $$\label{geo:infinity-expansion}
 \Psi(\eta)=C_1+\exp a(1)\eta+O(\eta^2)$$ is smooth at $\eta=0$. This expansion gives smoothness on the projective manifold, not merely along radial rays. Indeed, on the projective chart meeting the divisor at infinity given by $$W_0=1/Z_j,\qquad W_i=Z_i/Z_j\quad(i\ne j),\qquad
 B=1+\sum_{i\ne j}|W_i|^2,$$ we have $q=B/|W_0|^2$. After removing the pluriharmonic term $-\log|W_0|^2$ from $\Phi$, a local potential is <a id="geo:divisor-potential"></a>$$\label{geo:divisor-potential}
 \log B+\Psi\!\left(\frac{|W_0|^2}{B}\right).$$ On $W_0=0$ its tangential complex Hessian is the Fubini–Study metric of the divisor, its normal coefficient is $\exp a(1)/B>0$, and its mixed coefficients vanish. This proves positivity there; for $n=1$ only the normal coefficient is present. On $0<x<1$, direct differentiation gives the tangential and radial eigenvalues $x/q$ and $v/q$, respectively, so positivity holds everywhere.

The difference $\Phi(\log|Z|^2)-\log(1+|Z|^2)$ is a globally smooth function. At the origin this follows from <a href="#geo:center-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:center-expansion">[geo:center-expansion]</a>; in the chart at infinity it equals $\Psi(\eta)-\log(1+\eta)$, which is smooth by <a href="#geo:infinity-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:infinity-expansion">[geo:infinity-expansion]</a>. Thus its complex Hessian is a global exact form and the Kähler class is $[\omega_*]$. All inversions and integrations above depend smoothly on time on compact subintervals of $I$, by the positive endpoint derivatives and the inverse function theorem with parameters.

We next compute scalar curvature. The eigenvalues already found give $$\det(g_{i\bar j})=q^{-n}x^{n-1}v=q^{-n}wv.$$ For any radial function $f$, the complex Laplacian is <a id="geo:complex-laplacian"></a>$$\label{geo:complex-laplacian}
 \Delta_\omega f
 =\frac{f_{\ell\ell}}{v}+\frac{n-1}{x}f_\ell
 =w^{-1}\partial_x(wf_\ell),$$ where $\partial_\ell=v\partial_x$ in the last expression. For $f=-\log\det g$, this yields $$f_\ell=n-\frac{(wv)'}w,\qquad
 S=\frac{n(n-1)}x-w^{-1}(wv)''.$$ Substituting $v=v_*+v_*^2H$ gives <a href="#geo:scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:scalar">[geo:scalar]</a>. The expression $K(H)$ is smooth at zero: $wv_*^2H=x^{n+1}(1-x)^2H$, whose second derivative is divisible by $x^{n-1}$ as a smooth function. It is also smooth at one.

For completeness, the average can be read directly from this formula. The radial volume density is a constant multiple of $w(x)\,\mathrm{d}x$: the Euclidean polar factor $q^{n-1}\mathrm{d}q$, multiplied by the determinant, becomes $wv\,\mathrm{d}\ell=w\,\mathrm{d}x$. Moreover, $$\int_0^1 K(H)w\,\mathrm{d}x
 =\big[(wv_*^2H)'\big]_{x=0}^{x=1}=0,$$ because $wv_*^2H$ vanishes to order at least two at both endpoints. This proves the asserted average, including when $n=1$.

<a id="geo:legendre-flow"></a>
<a id="geo:legendre-sign"></a>
Finally, fix $t_0\in I$, choose $u(t_0)$ as above, and define at fixed momentum coordinate $$\label{geo:legendre-flow}
 u(t,x)=u(t_0,x)+\int_{t_0}^{t}K(H)(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s.$$ Equation <a href="#geo:equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:equation">[geo:equation]</a> implies $$\partial_t(1/v)=-\frac{v_*^2H_t}{v^2}
 =\mathcal AH=\partial_x^2K(H)=\partial_tu''.$$ Their initial values agree, so $u''=1/v$ for all time. In particular the preceding metric construction applies throughout $I$. Differentiating the Legendre dual $\Phi(t,\ell)=x(t,\ell)\ell-u(t,x(t,\ell))$ at fixed $\ell$ cancels the terms containing $x_t$, and gives $$\label{geo:legendre-sign}
 \Phi_t=-u_t=-K(H)=S-\bar S.$$ The difference between $\Phi(t)$ and $\Phi(t_0)$ is globally smooth by the endpoint argument and therefore supplies $\phi$ in <a href="#geo:potential-flow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:potential-flow">[geo:potential-flow]</a>.

This also specifies the affine part of $u$ during evolution. Replacing a fixed $u$ by $u+\alpha x+\beta$ changes its dual to $\Phi(\ell-\alpha)-\beta$, corresponding to the projective dilation $Z\mapsto\exp(-\alpha/2)Z$ and a potential constant. An arbitrary time-dependent choice of these terms would affect <a href="#geo:legendre-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:legendre-sign">[geo:legendre-sign]</a>; the prescription <a href="#geo:legendre-flow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:legendre-flow">[geo:legendre-flow]</a> fixes them and establishes that equation without an additional affine term. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### A regular auxiliary sphere and its flat limit

The endpoints of the momentum interval can be handled by a smooth equation on a closed auxiliary manifold. Set <a id="geo:sphere"></a>$$\label{geo:sphere}
 m=2n+4,\qquad
 \Sigma=S^{m+5}\subset\mathbb{R}^m\times\mathbb{R}^6,\qquad x=|z|^2.$$ We identify functions of $x$ with $O(m)\times O(6)$-invariant functions on $\Sigma$.

<a id="stmt-geo-operators"></a>
<div id="geo:operators" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (The compact and flat operators). *This identification is a bijection between $C^\infty[0,1]$ and the smooth invariant functions on $\Sigma$. The pushforward of spherical volume under $x$ is a positive constant multiple of $wv_*^2\,\mathrm{d}x$. With the nonpositive Laplacian convention, put $L=\frac14\Delta_\Sigma$. On invariant functions, <a id="geo:sphere-operator"></a>$$\label{geo:sphere-operator}
 L=x(1-x)\partial_x^2+\big(n+2-(n+5)x\big)\partial_x,
 \qquad
 \mathcal A=(-L+n+3)(-L+2(n+2)).$$ The right-hand expression defines a smooth elliptic operator of order four on all of $\Sigma$.*

<a id="geo:local-laplacian"></a>
<a id="geo:flat-operator"></a>
*In the core coordinates $(z,\theta)\mapsto(z,\sqrt{1-|z|^2}\,\theta)$, with $\theta\in S^5$, its Laplacian on functions independent of $\theta$ is $$\label{geo:local-laplacian}
 \Delta_\Sigma
 =\Delta_z-\sum_{i,j=1}^m z_i z_j\partial_{z_i z_j}
             -(m+5)\sum_{i=1}^m z_i\partial_{z_i}.$$ The flat momentum operator obtained by replacing $v_*$ by $x$ in <a href="#geo:momentum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:momentum">[geo:momentum]</a> satisfies $$\label{geo:flat-operator}
 \widehat\mathcal AH
 :=\big[x^{1-n}(x^{n+1}H)''\big]''
 =\big(x\partial_x^2+(n+2)\partial_x\big)^2H
 =\frac1{16}\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^m}^2H,\qquad x=|z|^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-operators"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-operators</summary>

*Proof.* The only regularity issue in the identification occurs at the two singular orbits. Here is an elementary smoothness argument. If $f(r)$ is smooth and even near zero, then the operator $$\mathcal T f(r)=\frac{f'(r)}r
              =\int_0^1f''(sr)\,\mathrm{d}s$$ defines another smooth even function. Thus, for $g(x)=f(\sqrt x)$, each derivative on $x>0$ has the form $g^{(j)}(x)=2^{-j}(\mathcal T^jf)(\sqrt x)$, and extends continuously to $x=0$. Induction gives smoothness up to zero of $g$ and all its derivatives. The same argument holds with smooth parameters. Restricting a smooth invariant function on $\Sigma$ to $(re_1,\sqrt{1-r^2}\,\theta_0)$ gives such an even $f$, and invariance makes its value depend only on $x=r^2$. Interchanging the two factors proves smoothness in $1-x$ at the other endpoint. Conversely a smooth function of $x$ is smooth in these coordinates at both orbits, as well as on their complement.

Write $|z|=\sin\vartheta$, with $0\leq\vartheta\leq\pi/2$. The spherical metric and volume density are $$\mathrm{d}\vartheta^2+\sin^2\vartheta\,g_{S^{m-1}}
                 +\cos^2\vartheta\,g_{S^5},\qquad
 \sin^{m-1}\vartheta\cos^5\vartheta\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta\,
       \mathrm{d}\omega_{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_5.$$ Changing to $x=\sin^2\vartheta$ gives density proportional to $$M(x)\,\mathrm{d}x:=x^{m/2-1}(1-x)^2\,\mathrm{d}x
              =wv_*^2\,\mathrm{d}x.$$ The radial Laplacian computed from this metric gives the displayed formula for $L$, equivalently $L=M^{-1}\partial_x(v_*M\partial_x)$.

We verify the factorization by a polynomial argument that also checks the endpoint terms. Both $L$ and $\mathcal A$ preserve polynomial degree and are symmetric for the inner product with density $M$. For $\mathcal A$, twice integrating by parts gives <a id="geo:symmetry"></a>$$\label{geo:symmetry}
 \int_0^1 a(\mathcal Ab)M\,\mathrm{d}x
 =\int_0^1\frac{(Ma)''(Mb)''}{w}\,\mathrm{d}x.$$ There are no boundary terms: $Ma$ and its first derivative vanish at each endpoint, and $w^{-1}(Mb)''$ is smooth there. For $L$ symmetry follows from its divergence expression, since $v_*M$ vanishes at both ends. Orthogonalizing the monomials therefore gives common polynomial eigenfunctions. On the polynomial of degree $j$, the leading coefficients give respectively the eigenvalues $$-j(j+n+4),\qquad
 (j+1)(j+2)(j+n+2)(j+n+3).$$ Indeed a symmetric degree-preserving operator maps the degree-$j$ orthogonal polynomial to a multiple of itself, because its inner product with every lower-degree polynomial vanishes. Finally, $$\big(j(j+n+4)+n+3\big)
 \big(j(j+n+4)+2(n+2)\big)
 =(j+1)(j+n+3)(j+2)(j+n+2).$$ This proves equality on every polynomial, hence equality of the differential expressions: at an interior point polynomials realize arbitrary jets through order four. The sphere expression has leading term $\Delta_\Sigma^2/16$, proving the claimed ellipticity.

For <a href="#geo:local-laplacian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:local-laplacian">[geo:local-laplacian]</a>, the inverse metric in the $z$ coordinates is $(\delta_{ij}-z_i z_j)$ and the volume density is $(1-|z|^2)^2\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}\omega_5$. Applying the divergence formula therefore gives $$(1-|z|^2)^{-2}\partial_{z_i}
 \big((1-|z|^2)^2(\delta_{ij}-z_i z_j)\partial_{z_j}\big)
 =(\delta_{ij}-z_i z_j)\partial_{z_i z_j}
   -(m+5)z_i\partial_{z_i},$$ with repeated indices summed. This computation only requires independence of $\theta$. Finally both one-dimensional expressions for $\widehat\mathcal A$ in <a href="#geo:flat-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:flat-operator">[geo:flat-operator]</a> expand to $$x^2H^{(4)}+2(n+3)xH^{(3)}+(n+2)(n+3)H'',$$ and the radial Euclidean identity $\frac14\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^m}=x\partial_x^2+\frac m2\partial_x$ proves the last equality. ◻

</details>

<a id="geo:core-section"></a>
#### The self-similar core

<a id="geo:flat-flow"></a>
<a id="geo:euler-identity"></a>
<a id="geo:g-flow"></a>
From now on set $n=10$, so $m=24$ and $\Sigma=S^{29}$. In the flat model the equation for the coefficient is $$\label{geo:flat-flow}
 F_t+(1+xF)^2\widehat\mathcal AF=0.$$ Let $D=x\partial_x$. For the function $g=1+xF$, <a href="#geo:flat-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:flat-operator">[geo:flat-operator]</a> gives $$\label{geo:euler-identity}
 x\widehat\mathcal A\big(x^{-1}(g-1)\big)
 =x^{-2}P(D)(g-1),\qquad
 P(D)=(D-2)(D-1)(D+9)(D+10).$$ Here $P$ denotes a polynomial, as distinct from the evolution operator denoted by $P$ later. One can check <a href="#geo:euler-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:euler-identity">[geo:euler-identity]</a> on $g-1=x^j$: both sides equal $(j-2)(j-1)(j+9)(j+10)x^{j-2}$; equality of the differential expressions follows as in Lemma <a href="#geo:operators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:operators">4</a>. Consequently <a href="#geo:flat-flow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:flat-flow">[geo:flat-flow]</a> is equivalent, for $x>0$, to $$\label{geo:g-flow}
 g_t=-g^2x^{-2}P(D)(g-1).$$

<a id="geo:profile-equation"></a>
<a id="geo:selfsimilar"></a>
Define the cubic polynomial operator $Q(D)=(D-1)(D+9)(D+10)$. Suppose that $G$ is smooth and positive on $[0,\infty)$, with $G(0)=1$, and for some $c>0$ satisfies $$\label{geo:profile-equation}
 Q(y\partial_y)(G-1)=\frac{y^2}{2}\left(\frac1G-\frac1c\right).$$ Then set $$\label{geo:selfsimilar}
 \rho=\sqrt{-t},\qquad y=x/\rho,\qquad
 h(y)=\frac{G(y)-1}{y},\qquad
 F(t,x)=\rho^{-1}h(x/\rho),\qquad t<0.$$ The value $h(0)=G'(0)$ makes $h$ smooth at zero, since $h(y)=\int_0^1G'(sy)\,\mathrm{d}s$. Thus $F$ is smooth for $t<0$ including at the flat origin. Applying $D-2$, now with $D=y\partial_y$, to <a href="#geo:profile-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:profile-equation">[geo:profile-equation]</a> yields $$P(D)(G-1)=-\frac{y^2DG}{2G^2}.$$ On the other hand $\rho_t=-1/(2\rho)$, so $\partial_tG(x/\rho)=DG/(2\rho^2)$. These identities prove <a href="#geo:g-flow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:g-flow">[geo:g-flow]</a>, and hence <a href="#geo:flat-flow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:flat-flow">[geo:flat-flow]</a>, first for $x>0$ and then at zero by smoothness. The existence of the required nonconstant positive $G$ is the subject of Theorem <a href="#prof:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:profile">6</a>.

The same expression $F(t,x)$ is smooth on the compact sphere for every $t<0$. Its compact positivity multiplier obeys the useful identity <a id="geo:approximate-positivity"></a>$$\label{geo:approximate-positivity}
 1+v_*F=x+(1-x)G(x/\rho).$$ In particular, if $G$ is bounded above and below by positive constants, this multiplier has the same property uniformly in $t<0$ and $0\leq x\leq1$. This is a bound in the momentum coordinate $x$, whose relation to the fixed affine coordinate varies under the Legendre transform. It does not imply uniform equivalence to the Fubini–Study metric.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Curvature at the concentrating point

The final geometric implication uses only one value of the correction. It is convenient to state it before constructing that correction.

<a id="stmt-geo-obstruction"></a>
<div id="geo:obstruction" class="lemma">

<a id="geo:curvature-rate"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Finite-time obstruction). *Let $G\in C^\infty[0,\infty)$ satisfy $G(0)=1$ and $G'(0)\ne0$, and define $F$ by <a href="#geo:selfsimilar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:selfsimilar">[geo:selfsimilar]</a>. Suppose that for some $T>0$ and $\delta>0$ there is a smooth invariant solution $H=F+V$ of <a href="#geo:equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:equation">[geo:equation]</a> on $(-T,0)\times\Sigma$ such that $1+v_*H>0$ and <a id="geo:correction-bound"></a>$$\label{geo:correction-bound}
 V(t,0)=O(\rho^{-1+\delta})\qquad(t\uparrow0).$$ Then a smooth $U(10)$-invariant metric $\omega_0\in[\omega_*]$ has maximal smooth Calabi-flow existence interval $[0,T_*)$, where $T_*=T/2<\infty$. At the fixed affine origin $p$, the scalar curvature of this flow satisfies $$\label{geo:curvature-rate}
 S(\tau,p)=a(T_*-\tau)^{-1/2}
       +O\big(1+(T_*-\tau)^{(-1+\delta)/2}\big),\qquad
 a=-110G'(0)\ne0.$$ In particular $S(\tau,p)\sim a(T_*-\tau)^{-1/2}$, and $a>0$ when $G'(0)<0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-obstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-obstruction</summary>

*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#geo:operators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:operators">4</a>, $H$ is smooth in $x$ at both endpoints for each time. Proposition <a href="#geo:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:reduction">3</a>, with $t_0=-T/2$, gives a smooth family of Kähler forms, and its slice $\omega_0=\omega(-T/2)$ is a smooth positive form in the Fubini–Study class. Put $\tau=t+T/2$. The corresponding global potential satisfies exactly the normalization and evolution in <a href="#geo:potential-flow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:potential-flow">[geo:potential-flow]</a> on $0\leq\tau<T_*=T/2$.

<a id="geo:center-scalar"></a>
At $x=0$, the coefficient of $x^{n+1}$ in $wv_*^2H$ is $H(t,0)$. Thus <a href="#geo:scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:scalar">[geo:scalar]</a> gives the exact identity $$\label{geo:center-scalar}
 S(t,p)=n(n+1)\big(1-H(t,0)\big).$$ This evaluates scalar curvature at the same point $p$ at every time: <a href="#geo:center-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:center-inverse">[geo:center-inverse]</a> sends $q=0$ to $x=0$ for every affine choice. Since $$H(t,0)=\rho^{-1}G'(0)+O(\rho^{-1+\delta}),$$ Equation <a href="#geo:center-scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:center-scalar">[geo:center-scalar]</a> proves <a href="#geo:curvature-rate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:curvature-rate">[geo:curvature-rate]</a> and the asserted divergence.

We recall why uniqueness applies to comparison with an unrestricted smooth flow; no symmetry hypothesis is needed for this step. Smooth positive initial potentials have a unique local Calabi flow by (Chen and He 2008, Theorem 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[ChenHe2008](#ref-ChenHe2008)]. The uniqueness mechanism can also be seen directly. For two smooth solutions with the same initial potential, let $\chi$ be their difference and interpolate their potentials by $\varphi_s$, $0\leq s\leq1$. Their interpolated Kähler forms remain positive, and variation of scalar curvature gives $$\chi_t=-\int_0^1
 \left(\Delta_{\varphi_s}^2\chi
       +\operatorname{Ric}_{\varphi_s}^{i\bar j}
                            \partial_i\partial_{\bar j}\chi\right)
 \,\mathrm{d}s.$$ On a compact time interval where both solutions are smooth, the coefficients are smooth and uniformly elliptic. Relative to a fixed smooth volume form, integration twice by parts and interpolation give $$\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\chi\|_{L^2}^2
 \leq-c\|\chi\|_{H^2}^2+C\|\chi\|_{L^2}^2
 \leq C\|\chi\|_{L^2}^2.$$ For this estimate, the principal quadratic form is the integral of $|\Delta_{\varphi_s}\chi|^2$; differentiating the smooth volume density introduces terms bounded by $C\|\chi\|_{H^1}\|\chi\|_{H^2}$, and the Ricci term has the same or lower order. The elliptic estimate for each uniformly elliptic Laplacian and $\|\chi\|_{H^1}\leq\varepsilon\|\chi\|_{H^2}+C_\varepsilon\|\chi\|_{L^2}$ absorb those terms. Gronwall’s inequality proves uniqueness.

It follows that any smooth flow from $\omega_0$ agrees with the one constructed above on their common interval. Hence its maximal interval contains $[0,T_*)$. If it extended beyond $T_*$, smooth positivity on the compact manifold across that time would bound its scalar curvature, contradicting <a href="#geo:curvature-rate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:curvature-rate">[geo:curvature-rate]</a>. Its maximal smooth existence time is therefore exactly $T_*$. ◻

</details>

The Fubini–Study class contains the constant-scalar-curvature form $\omega_*$, so the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#geo:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:obstruction">5</a> suffice to refute the smooth global-existence assertion. Its requirement of smooth existence at every finite time has already failed. In addition, $\|S\|_{L^\infty}$ is unchanged by biholomorphic pullback, so the curvature divergence itself is unaffected by allowing automorphisms.

<a id="prof:section"></a>
### A positive shrinking profile

<span id="sec:profile" label="sec:profile"></span>

We construct the profile used in the concentration argument. Its construction has two parts: a finite shooting estimate, proved by rational enclosures in Appendix <a href="#cert:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:section">[cert:section]</a>, and an analytic construction on a half-line. The finite calculation belongs to the established framework of validated Taylor integration for ordinary differential equations (Nedialkov, Jackson, and Corliss 1999) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[NedialkovJacksonCorliss1999](#ref-NedialkovJacksonCorliss1999)]. Here the integer interval recurrences and complex-disk remainder bounds are given explicitly. The half-line solution is constructed by a contraction, whereas the final matching argument uses only continuity of the shooting map.

<a id="stmt-prof-profile"></a>
<div id="prof:profile" class="theorem">

<a id="prof:profile-equation"></a>
<a id="prof:expansion"></a>
**Theorem 6** (Shrinking profile). *There exist a constant $c>0$ and a function $G\in C^\infty([0,\infty))$ such that $$\label{prof:profile-equation}
 Q(y\partial_y)(G-1)=\frac{y^2}{2}\left(\frac1G-\frac1c\right),
 \qquad Q(D)=(D-1)(D+9)(D+10).$$ The function $G$ is bounded above and bounded away from zero, satisfies $G(0)=1$ and $G'(0)<0$, and is analytic near zero. There are real coefficients $g_j$, $j\ge1$, such that, for every $J,\ell\ge0$, $$\label{prof:expansion}
 \frac{\mathrm{d}^\ell}{\mathrm{d}y^\ell}
 \left(G(y)-c-\sum_{j=1}^{J}g_jy^{-2j}\right)
 =O\bigl(y^{-2(J+1)-\ell}\bigr)\qquad(y\longrightarrow\infty).$$ In particular, $u\mapsto G(u^{-1/2})$ extends smoothly to $u=0$ from the right, with value $c$.*

<a id="prof:flat-shrinker"></a>
*Set $h(y)=(G(y)-1)/y$, with its smooth value at zero. For $t<0$, the function $$\label{prof:flat-shrinker}
 F(t,x)=\rho^{-1}h(x/\rho),\qquad \rho=\sqrt{-t},$$ solves $$F_t+(1+xF)^2\widehat{\mathcal A}F=0,
 \qquad \widehat{\mathcal A}=(x\partial_x^2+12\partial_x)^2.$$*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Reduction and the analytic family at the origin

The calculation in Section <a href="#geo:core-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:core-section">2.2</a> shows that <a href="#prof:profile-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:profile-equation">[prof:profile-equation]</a> makes $F$ in <a href="#prof:flat-shrinker" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:flat-shrinker">[prof:flat-shrinker]</a> an exact solution of the flat equation. It remains to construct a positive $G$ with the stated behavior at zero and infinity.

<a id="prof:change-variables"></a>
<a id="prof:ode"></a>
Put $$\label{prof:change-variables}
 c=(1+d)^{-1},\qquad
 s=\left(\frac{27}{16}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac yc\right)^{2/3},
 \qquad G(y)=c(1+p(s)).$$ Since $y\partial_y=\frac23s\partial_s$ and $G-1=c(p-d)$, <a href="#prof:profile-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:profile-equation">[prof:profile-equation]</a> becomes $$\label{prof:ode}
 \mathcal E_d(p):=
 p'''+\frac{30}{s}p''+\frac B{s^2}p'
       +\frac C{s^3}(p-d)+\frac p{1+p}=0,
 \qquad B=\frac{751}{4},\quad C=-\frac{1215}{4}.$$ Primes in this section henceforth denote $s$-derivatives, except where another variable is explicitly displayed. For example, the identity $Q(D)=D^3+18D^2+71D-90$ verifies all the coefficients in <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a>.

We will construct origin solutions $p(s)=d+ks^{3/2}+O(s^3)$, with two real parameters: $d$ fixes $c=(1+d)^{-1}$, and $k$ fixes $G'(0)$. These parameters must be chosen to match $p$ to a tail tending to zero. The limiting linearized equation $p'''+p=0$ has roots $-1$ and $(1\pm i\sqrt3)/2$: one decaying direction and two real growing directions.

The tail argument uses a constant-coefficient comparison equation with the same splitting. After subtracting an explicit decaying approximation, we express the residual jet at $S_0=56$ in its eigenbasis. For each allowed $d$, a contraction constructs small bounded tails with one real coordinate free and the other two determined. Choosing the free coordinate from the origin solution leaves two real matching conditions to enforce by varying $(k,d)$.

<a id="prof:parameters"></a>
<a id="prof:parameter-displacement"></a>
The affine square below makes the shot’s two remaining coordinates close to its parameter $z_*$. Together with the tail bound, the certified estimate yields a continuous self-map of the square; Brouwer’s theorem supplies a matching point. All terminating decimals in this section denote exact rational numbers. Define $$\label{prof:parameters}
 \begin{gathered}
 R=5\cdot10^{-10},\qquad
 \mathcal Z=\{z_*\in\mathbb{R}^2:\lVert z_*\rVert_\infty\le R\},\qquad S_0=56,\\
 \begin{pmatrix}k\\d\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}-.094617116666041\\ .400682721856639\end{pmatrix}
 +10^{-8}
 \begin{pmatrix}25190&-63731\\721855&359157\end{pmatrix}z_*.
 \end{gathered}$$ Write $k_0,d_0$ for the central values. The square implies $$\label{prof:parameter-displacement}
 |k-k_0|\le4.44605\cdot10^{-13},\qquad
 |d-d_0|\le5.40506\cdot10^{-12}<.011R.$$

<a id="stmt-prof-origin"></a>
<div id="prof:origin" class="lemma">

<a id="prof:origin-majorant"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Origin family). *For $|k-k_0|\le .1$ and $|d-d_0|\le .1$ in $\mathbb{C}$, there is a unique solution germ of <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a>, analytic in $r=s^{3/2}$, of the form <a id="prof:origin-series"></a>$$\label{prof:origin-series}
 p=d+kr+\sum_{i\ge2}a_ir^i,\qquad r=s^{3/2}.$$ The series is jointly holomorphic in $r,k,d$ for $|r|<3$ and satisfies $$\label{prof:origin-majorant}
 3|k|+\sum_{i\ge2}|a_i|3^i<.8.$$ Each of $p(1),p'(1),p''(1)$ has modulus at most $2$ on this parameter polydisc. For the real parameters in <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a>, $1+p>0$ on $0\le s\le1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prof-origin"><summary>Proof of stmt-prof-origin</summary>

<a id="prof:origin-recurrence"></a>
*Proof.* In the variable $r=s^{3/2}$, <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> is equivalent to $$Q(r\partial_r)(p-d)=\frac8{27}r^2\bigl((1+p)^{-1}-1\bigr).$$ Consequently the coefficients of an analytic solution must satisfy $$\label{prof:origin-recurrence}
 a_i=
 \frac{[r^{i-2}]\bigl((1+p)^{-1}-1\bigr)}
 {(27/8)(i-1)(i+9)(i+10)},\qquad i\ge2.$$ The constant and linear equations leave $d,k$ free, and this recurrence determines every subsequent coefficient uniquely.

For convergence, use the Banach algebra of power series with norm $\sum_i|b_i|3^i$. On the closed ball of series $a$ with no constant or linear term and norm at most $.2$, one has $$\lVert kr+a\rVert<.585+.2<.8,\qquad |1+d|>1.30.$$ Thus inversion of $1+d+kr+a$ is given by a convergent geometric series in that Banach algebra and has norm at most $2$. The map specified by <a href="#prof:origin-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:origin-recurrence">[prof:origin-recurrence]</a> sends this ball into the ball of radius $9(2+1)/440<.062$, since every denominator in that formula exceeds $440$. Its Lipschitz constant is at most $9\cdot 2^2/440<1$. Iteration therefore gives a unique fixed point, jointly holomorphic in the parameters. The strict inequalities also hold on a neighborhood of the closed parameter polydisc. Moreover $$3|k|+\lVert a\rVert
 \le 3(.094617116666041+.1)+\frac{27}{440}<.8,$$ which proves <a href="#prof:origin-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:origin-majorant">[prof:origin-majorant]</a>.

For $0\le r\le1$, the nonconstant sum is at most $.8r/3$. In particular the real parameters in <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a> give $1+p>1.4-.8/3>0$. At $s=1$, a term $a_is^{3i/2}$ acquires factors $3i/2$ and $(3i/2)(3i/2-1)$ under one and two derivatives. The elementary bounds $$\sup_{i\ge1}\frac{3i/2}{3^i}=\frac12,
 \qquad
 \sup_{i\ge1}\frac{(3i/2)(3i/2-1)}{3^i}=\frac23$$ together with <a href="#prof:origin-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:origin-majorant">[prof:origin-majorant]</a> and $|d|<.501$ give the stated jet bounds. Termwise differentiation is valid since $1<3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The matching coordinates

<a id="prof:asymptotic-polynomial"></a>
<a id="prof:coordinates"></a>
Define the four-term approximation $$\label{prof:asymptotic-polynomial}
 \begin{gathered}
 p_{\rm a}(s;d)=\sum_{j=1}^4d_js^{-3j},\qquad
 a=d_1=Cd,\qquad b=d_2=a^2+567a,\\
 d_3=2ab-a^3+\frac{2025}{4}b,\qquad
 d_4=b^2+2ad_3-3a^2b+a^4+\frac{567}{2}d_3.
 \end{gathered}$$ We choose the constant coefficients in $D_0$ at the midpoints of the corresponding linearized tail ranges on $[S_0,\infty)$, giving the uniform difference bounds used in Lemma <a href="#prof:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:tail">10</a>. Let $$\label{prof:coordinates}
 \begin{gathered}
 D_0(\lambda)=\lambda^3+\frac{15}{S_0}\lambda^2
       +\frac{B}{2S_0^2}\lambda+1+\frac{C}{2S_0^3},\\
 v_i=(1,\lambda_i,\lambda_i^2)^t,\qquad
 \begin{pmatrix}p-p_{\rm a}\\p'-p_{\rm a}'\\p''-p_{\rm a}''\end{pmatrix}_{s=S_0}
       =qv_0+\xi v_1+\bar\xi v_2.
 \end{gathered}$$ The roots are ordered so that $\lambda_0$ is real, $\Im\lambda_1>0$, and $\lambda_2=\bar\lambda_1$. The exact real projection matrix $\Gamma$ takes a real jet to $(q,\Re\xi,\Im\xi)^t$. Its rational approximation used in the finite certificate is <a id="prof:projection-approximation"></a>$$\label{prof:projection-approximation}
 \Lambda=10^{-5}
 \begin{pmatrix}
 30756&-27384&33447\\
 34622&13692&-16724\\
 -2925&-34040&-28850
 \end{pmatrix}.$$

<a id="stmt-prof-root-bounds"></a>
<div id="prof:root-bounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Root and convolution bounds). *The roots in <a href="#prof:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:coordinates">[prof:coordinates]</a> are distinct and satisfy <a id="prof:root-boxes"></a>$$\label{prof:root-boxes}
 \begin{split}
 -1.08659&<\lambda_0<-1.08655,\\
 .40933&<\Re\lambda_1<.40938,\qquad
 .8671<\Im\lambda_1<.86721.
 \end{split}$$ Every entry of $\Gamma-\Lambda$ has modulus less than $.001$. The absolute row sums of $\Gamma$ are less than $.92,.68,.68$, respectively. With <a id="prof:convolution-constants"></a>$$\label{prof:convolution-constants}
 M_i=\sum_{j=0}^2
 \frac{|\lambda_j|^i}{|\Re\lambda_j|\,|D_0'(\lambda_j)|},\qquad 0\le i\le2,$$ one has $$M_0<1.96,\qquad M_1<1.92,\qquad M_2<1.88,
 \qquad \frac1{\Re\lambda_1|D_0'(\lambda_1)|}<.82.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prof-root-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-prof-root-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\alpha=15/56$, $\beta=B/(2\cdot56^2)$ and $H_0=1+C/(2\cdot56^3)$. The exact calculations $$\alpha^2-3\beta=-\frac{453}{25088}<0,\qquad
 D_0(-1.08659)=-\frac{17566501197647}{343000000000000000}<0,$$ $$D_0(-1.08655)=\frac{187622583137}{2744000000000000}>0$$ show that $D_0$ is strictly increasing on the real line and has exactly one real root in the indicated interval. Write $\lambda_0=-r_0$ and $\lambda_1=u+iv$ with $v>0$. Vieta’s identities give <a id="prof:root-arithmetic"></a>$$\label{prof:root-arithmetic}
 u=\frac{r_0-\alpha}{2},\qquad
 v^2=\frac{H_0}{r_0}-u^2,\qquad |\lambda_1|^2=\frac{H_0}{r_0}.$$ In particular, if $u_-=114617/280000$ and $u_+=573113/1400000$, then $u_-<u<u_+$, with $.40933<u_-$ and $u_+<.40938$. The rational inequalities $$.8671^2<\frac{H_0}{1.08659}-u_+^2
 \le v^2\le\frac{H_0}{1.08655}-u_-^2<.86721^2,
 \qquad \frac{H_0}{1.08655}<.959^2$$ give the other root bounds and $|\lambda_1|<.959$.

For each root, the corresponding complex projection row is <a id="prof:projection-row"></a>$$\label{prof:projection-row}
 \frac{(\lambda_j\lambda_k,-\lambda_j-\lambda_k,1)}
 {D_0'(\lambda_i)},\qquad \{i,j,k\}=\{0,1,2\}.$$ Taking the real row for $i=0$ and the real and imaginary parts for $i=1$ gives $\Gamma$. Put $E=(r_0+u)^2+v^2$. The three real rows are $$\begin{gathered}
 \left(\frac{u^2+v^2}{E},\frac{-2u}{E},\frac1E\right),\\
 \left(\frac{r_0(r_0+2u)}{2E},\frac uE,\frac{-1}{2E}\right),\\
 \left(\frac{r_0[u(r_0+u)-v^2]}{2vE},
       \frac{-(v^2+r_0^2-u^2)}{2vE},\frac{-(r_0+u)}{2vE}\right).
 \end{gathered}$$ Substituting $r_0\in[1.08655,1.08659]$ and the intervals just obtained for $u,v,v^2$ gives the following rational enclosures. For each entry, products are enclosed by the minimum and maximum of the four endpoint products, and positive reciprocals by reversing the reciprocal endpoints.

<div class="center">

|          |    First entry    |   Second entry    |    Third entry    |
|:--------:|:-----------------:|:-----------------:|:-----------------:|
|   $q$    |  $(.3074,.3078)$  | $(-.2740,-.2737)$ |  $(.3344,.3346)$  |
| $\Re\xi$ |  $(.3461,.3464)$  |  $(.1368,.1370)$  | $(-.1673,-.1672)$ |
| $\Im\xi$ | $(-.0294,-.0291)$ | $(-.3406,-.3402)$ | $(-.2887,-.2883)$ |

</div>

The entrywise error from $\Lambda$ is therefore less than $.001$, and the row sums are less than $.9164,.6507,.6587$, respectively.

Finally, $$E>(1.08655+u_-)^2+.8671^2
   =\frac{46876434629}{15680000000}>2.9895>1.729^2.$$ Since $D_0'(-r_0)=E$ and $|D_0'(u+iv)|=2v\sqrt E$, for $i=0,1,2$ one has $$M_i<\frac{1.08659^i}{1.08655\cdot2.9895}
      +\frac{.959^i}{.40933\cdot.8671\cdot1.729}.$$ These three rational expressions are less than $1.96,1.92,1.88$, respectively, by multiplication by their positive denominators. Similarly, $$\frac1{\Re\lambda_1|D_0'(\lambda_1)|}
 <\frac1{2\cdot.40933\cdot.8671\cdot1.729}
 =\frac{500000000000}{613674044347}<.82.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="prof:forward-bounds"></a>
The finite part of the construction is the following consequence of Proposition <a href="#cert:shoot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:shoot">24</a>. The origin solution of Lemma <a href="#prof:origin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:origin">7</a>, for every $z_*\in\mathcal Z$, continues to $s=S_0$ and satisfies $$\label{prof:forward-bounds}
 1+p(s)>0\quad(0\le s\le S_0),\qquad
 |q_{\rm shot}(z_*)|\le .25R,
 \qquad
 \lVert (\Re\xi_{\rm shot},\Im\xi_{\rm shot})-z_*\rVert_\infty\le .13R.$$ The proof in the Appendix includes the analytic interpretation of its rational Taylor enclosures, the propagation of all rounding and truncation errors, and the uniform nonlinear continuation estimate. In particular, <a href="#prof:forward-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:forward-bounds">[prof:forward-bounds]</a> is a statement about the exact solutions. Their endpoint jets depend continuously on $z_*$: this follows first from Lemma <a href="#prof:origin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:origin">7</a> at $s=1$, and then from ordinary continuous dependence for <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> where $s>0$ and $1+p>0$. The estimates from this section used in Appendix <a href="#sec:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate">[sec:certificate]</a> are the origin family and the explicit root and tail-polynomial estimates; all are independent of the matching step.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Construction on the half-line

<a id="stmt-prof-tail-residual"></a>
<div id="prof:tail-residual" class="lemma">

<a id="prof:tail-parameter-bound"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Tail residual). *Uniformly for the parameters in <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a>, $$\sup_{s\ge56}|\mathcal E_d(p_{\rm a})(s)|<3\cdot10^{-13}.$$ Each derivative of this residual tends to zero at infinity. If $P_{\rm a}(d)=(p_{\rm a},p_{\rm a}',p_{\rm a}'')(56;d)$, then $$\label{prof:tail-parameter-bound}
 \lVert P_{\rm a}(d)-P_{\rm a}(d_0)\rVert_\infty\le .01|d-d_0|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prof-tail-residual"><summary>Proof of stmt-prof-tail-residual</summary>

*Proof.* Put $t=s^{-3}$. For a monomial $d_js^{-3j}$, the differential part $p'''+30s^{-1}p''+Bs^{-2}p'+Cs^{-3}p$ contributes $L(-3j)d_jt^{j+1}$, where $$L(\nu)=\nu(\nu-1)(\nu-2)+30\nu(\nu-1)+B\nu+C.$$ Its first four relevant values are $$L(-3)=-567,\quad L(-6)=-2025/4,\quad
 L(-9)=-567/2,\quad L(-12)=-243/4.$$ Using $p/(1+p)=p-p^2/(1+p)$ shows that the coefficients through $t^4$ cancel exactly for <a href="#prof:asymptotic-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:asymptotic-polynomial">[prof:asymptotic-polynomial]</a>.

<a id="prof:tail-term-bounds"></a>
The parameter interval <a href="#prof:parameter-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameter-displacement">[prof:parameter-displacement]</a> is contained in $[.40068272185,.40068272187]$. Substituting this rational interval in <a href="#prof:asymptotic-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:asymptotic-polynomial">[prof:asymptotic-polynomial]</a> and its $d$-derivative gives $$\begin{array}{c|rrrr}
 j&1&2&3&4\\ \hline
 |d_j|<&122&54200&12500000&5100000000\\
 |\partial_d d_j|<&304&100000&65000000&100000000000.
\end{array}$$ These interval bounds use just the endpoint operations described in Lemma <a href="#prof:root-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:root-bounds">8</a>; the coefficient formulas are polynomials. The first row in particular implies $$\label{prof:tail-term-bounds}
 |d_1|56^{-3}<.00070,\quad |d_2|56^{-6}<1.8\cdot10^{-6},\quad
 |d_3|56^{-9}<8.1\cdot10^{-9},\quad |d_4|56^{-12}<3.2\cdot10^{-11}.$$ Here is a finite positive-coefficient way to check the residual bound. Let $b=(.00070,1.8\cdot10^{-6},8.1\cdot10^{-9},3.2\cdot10^{-11})$ and $B_*(z)=\sum_{i=1}^4b_iz^i$. If $T_*=\sum b_i$, the sums of the coefficients of degrees greater than four in its second, third, and fourth powers are, respectively, $$T_*^2-b_1^2-2b_1b_2-2b_1b_3-b_2^2,\qquad
 T_*^3-b_1^3-3b_1^2b_2,\qquad T_*^4-b_1^4.$$ Substitution of the displayed four rational numbers gives <a id="prof:residual-arithmetic"></a>$$\label{prof:residual-arithmetic}
 \sum_{j>4}[z^j]B_*(z)^2<8\cdot10^{-14},\qquad
 \sum_{j>4}[z^j]B_*(z)^3<2\cdot10^{-14},\qquad
 \sum_{j>4}[z^j]B_*(z)^4<3\cdot10^{-15}.$$ Since $B_*(1)<.001$, all powers at least five contribute at most $.001^5/(1-.001)$. The remaining linear term contributes at most $(243/4)(3.2\cdot10^{-11})/56^3$. Thus the residual is bounded by $$8\cdot10^{-14}+2\cdot10^{-14}+3\cdot10^{-15}
 +\frac{.001^5}{1-.001}
 +\frac{(243/4)(3.2\cdot10^{-11})}{56^3}
 <3\cdot10^{-13}.$$ These bounds hold for all $s\ge56$ because every remaining absolute monomial decreases there. The residual is a rational function of $s^{-3}$, analytic at zero with vanishing constant term, which also proves the assertion about its derivatives. For the parameter estimate, the factors $3j/56$ and $3j(3j+1)/56^2$ are at most one for $1\le j\le4$. Thus the second row of the coefficient table gives $$\lVert \partial_dP_{\rm a}\rVert_\infty
 \le\frac{304}{56^3}+\frac{100000}{56^6}
       +\frac{65000000}{56^9}+\frac{100000000000}{56^{12}}
 <.001735<.01.$$ The mean value theorem yields <a href="#prof:tail-parameter-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:tail-parameter-bound">[prof:tail-parameter-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prof-tail"></a>
<div id="prof:tail" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Tail solutions). *For each $d$ arising in <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a> and each real $|q|\le .26R$, there is a solution $p_{\rm tail}$ of <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> on $[56,\infty)$ such that $$|p_{\rm tail}|<.001,
 \qquad q_{\rm tail}=q,\qquad |\xi_{\rm tail}|<.25R.$$ Its first three jet entries tend to zero at infinity. The solution, as a bounded jet, and its initial coordinates depend continuously on $q,d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prof-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-prof-tail</summary>

<a id="prof:green-formula"></a>
*Proof.* Let $b_i=D_0'(\lambda_i)^{-1}$ and let $j$ be a bounded continuous real function on $[S_0,\infty)$. Define a vector $\mathcal V=(V,V',V'')^t$ by the convergent integrals $$\label{prof:green-formula}
 \begin{split}
 \mathcal V(s)={}&q\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_0(s-S_0)}v_0
  +b_0v_0\int_{S_0}^s\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_0(s-u)}j(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\\
 &-\sum_{i=1}^2 b_iv_i\int_s^\infty\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_i(s-u)}j(u)\,\mathrm{d}u.
 \end{split}$$ Indeed this vector solves the companion system for $D_0(\partial_s)V=j$: the identities $\sum b_i=\sum\lambda_ib_i=0$ and $\sum\lambda_i^2b_i=1$ follow by partial fractions of $1/D_0$. They show both the derivative relations between its entries and the source in the last component. The vector is real by complex conjugation. It is the unique bounded solution with the prescribed real-mode coordinate at $S_0$, since a bounded homogeneous solution has no growing nonreal modes.

<a id="prof:green-bound"></a>
For $0\le i\le2$, Lemma <a href="#prof:root-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:root-bounds">8</a> gives $$\label{prof:green-bound}
 \lVert V^{(i)}\rVert_\infty\le |\lambda_0|^i|q|+M_i\lVert j\rVert_\infty.$$ In particular, on the closed ball $\lVert j\rVert_\infty\le .3R$, the first entry satisfies $\lVert V\rVert_\infty\le(.26+1.96\cdot.3)R=.848R$. Together with <a href="#prof:tail-term-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:tail-term-bounds">[prof:tail-term-bounds]</a>, this ensures $|p_{\rm a}+V|<.001$ throughout the half-line.

<a id="prof:tail-map"></a>
Write $N(p)=p^2/(1+p)$ and $e_{\rm a}=\mathcal E_d(p_{\rm a})$. Then $p=p_{\rm a}+V$ solves <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> exactly when $$\label{prof:tail-map}
 \begin{split}
 j=\mathcal T_{q,d}j:={}&-e_{\rm a}
 -\left(\frac{30}{s}-\frac{15}{S_0}\right)V''
 -\left(\frac B{s^2}-\frac B{2S_0^2}\right)V'\\
 &-\left(\frac C{s^3}-\frac C{2S_0^3}\right)V
 +N(p_{\rm a}+V)-N(p_{\rm a}).
 \end{split}$$ For $|p|\le.001$, $$|N'(p)|=|1-(1+p)^{-2}|<.0021.$$ The three coefficient differences in <a href="#prof:tail-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:tail-map">[prof:tail-map]</a> have absolute bounds $15/S_0$, $B/(2S_0^2)$ and $|C|/(2S_0^3)$ on the entire half-line. It follows from <a href="#prof:green-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:green-bound">[prof:green-bound]</a> that the Lipschitz constant of $\mathcal T_{q,d}$ on the ball is at most <a id="prof:tail-contraction-constant"></a>$$\label{prof:tail-contraction-constant}
 \frac{15}{56}(1.88)+\frac{B}{2\cdot56^2}(1.92)
 +\left(\frac{|C|}{2\cdot56^3}+.0021\right)(1.96)<.57.$$ The corresponding coefficient of the homogeneous term is bounded by <a id="prof:tail-homogeneous-constant"></a>$$\label{prof:tail-homogeneous-constant}
 \frac{15}{56}(1.08659)^2+\frac{B}{2\cdot56^2}(1.08659)
 +\frac{|C|}{2\cdot56^3}+.0021<.36.$$ Consequently $$\lVert \mathcal T_{q,d}j\rVert_\infty
 \le3\cdot10^{-13}+.36|q|+.57\lVert j\rVert_\infty
 \le(.0006+.36\cdot.26+.57\cdot.3)R=.2652R<.3R.$$ The contraction theorem gives a unique fixed point in this ball. Equation <a href="#prof:green-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:green-formula">[prof:green-formula]</a> then gives a $C^3$ solution, and <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> bootstraps it to a smooth solution for $s>0$. Its nonreal initial coordinate satisfies $$\xi_{\rm tail}=-b_1\int_{S_0}^\infty
                  \mathrm{e}^{\lambda_1(S_0-u)}j(u)\,\mathrm{d}u,
 \qquad |\xi_{\rm tail}|<.82(.3R)=.246R<.25R.$$

All coefficients in <a href="#prof:tail-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:tail-map">[prof:tail-map]</a> depend continuously on $d$ in the relevant uniform norms, and the homogeneous vector depends continuously on $q$. The common contraction constant therefore gives continuous dependence of the fixed point, the bounded jet, and its initial coordinates. Finally, exponential convolutions of continuous functions tending to zero again tend to zero. Thus <a href="#prof:tail-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:tail-map">[prof:tail-map]</a> preserves the closed subspace of such functions; the residual tends to zero and all its other coefficients are bounded. Iteration from $j=0$ shows that the fixed point belongs to this subspace. Formula <a href="#prof:green-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:green-formula">[prof:green-formula]</a> proves $p,p',p''\to0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Matching the two solutions

For a parameter $z_*\in\mathcal Z$, apply Lemma <a href="#prof:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:tail">10</a> using the value $d(z_*)$ and the coordinate $q_{\rm shot}(z_*)$ supplied by the finite shot. This is allowed by $|q_{\rm shot}|\le .25R<.26R$. Define a map on the closed square by $$\mathcal B(z_*)=z_*
 -\bigl(\Re(\xi_{\rm shot}-\xi_{\rm tail}),
        \Im(\xi_{\rm shot}-\xi_{\rm tail})\bigr).$$ It is continuous by the continuous dependence established above and satisfies $$\lVert \mathcal B(z_*)\rVert_\infty\le(.13+.25)R=.38R<R.$$ Brouwer’s fixed point theorem gives $z_*\in\mathcal Z$ with $\xi_{\rm shot}=\xi_{\rm tail}$. The real coordinate and the parameter $d$ already agree. The three jet entries in <a href="#prof:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:coordinates">[prof:coordinates]</a> therefore agree, and ordinary uniqueness for <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> joins the shot and tail to one solution on $[0,\infty)$.

The finite shot has $1+p>0$, and the tail has $|p|<.001$. Compactness of the finite interval and the uniform tail bound imply that $1+p$ is bounded and bounded away from zero globally. Since $1+d>0$, the function $G$ in <a href="#prof:change-variables" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:change-variables">[prof:change-variables]</a> has the same properties. Near zero, $$s^{3/2}=\frac{\sqrt{27}}{4c}y,
 \qquad G(y)=1+\frac{\sqrt{27}}4ky+O(y^2).$$ It is therefore analytic there, with $G'(0)=k\sqrt{27}/4<0$ by <a href="#prof:parameter-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameter-displacement">[prof:parameter-displacement]</a>. This proves all assertions of Theorem <a href="#prof:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:profile">6</a> except for the differentiable expansion, which we now establish independently of the finite shooting estimates.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Differentiable asymptotics at every order

The distinction between a bounded remainder and a remainder in a prescribed polynomial weight matters here. The following argument first proves uniqueness among bounded solutions and then identifies the actual remainder with the weighted solution.

<a id="stmt-prof-weighted-remainder"></a>
<div id="prof:weighted-remainder" class="lemma">

<a id="prof:s-expansion"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Weighted remainder). *Let $p$ be the matched solution above. There are uniquely determined coefficients $d_j$, extending <a href="#prof:asymptotic-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:asymptotic-polynomial">[prof:asymptotic-polynomial]</a>, such that for every $J,\ell\ge0$, $$\label{prof:s-expansion}
 \frac{\mathrm{d}^\ell}{\mathrm{d}s^\ell}
 \left(p(s)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}d_js^{-3j}\right)
 =O\bigl(s^{-3(J+1)-\ell}\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prof-weighted-remainder"><summary>Proof of stmt-prof-weighted-remainder</summary>

*Proof.* The limits $p,p',p''\to0$ and <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> imply that every derivative of $p$ is bounded on a sufficiently late half-line. To see this without assuming any asymptotic expansion, solve <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> for $p'''$. Its right-hand side is bounded, since $p$ is separated from $-1$. Successively differentiating this formula proves the claim by induction: the coefficient functions $s^{-j}$ have bounded derivatives and all derivatives of $p\mapsto p/(1+p)$ are bounded on the range in question.

<a id="prof:formal-residual"></a>
Formal substitution of $p=\sum_{j\ge1}d_jt^j$, $t=s^{-3}$, in <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> determines the coefficients successively. Indeed the coefficient of $t^j$ contains the new coefficient $d_j$ with coefficient one from $p/(1+p)$; every other term involves only previous coefficients. The source $-Cd/s^3$ determines $d_1=Cd$. For $p_J=\sum_{j=1}^Jd_js^{-3j}$, including $p_0=0$, one consequently has $$\label{prof:formal-residual}
 e_J:=\mathcal E_d(p_J),\qquad
 e_J^{(\ell)}(s)=O\bigl(s^{-N-\ell}\bigr),
 \quad N=3(J+1),\quad \ell\ge0.$$ This follows directly from analyticity of the finite expression in $s^{-3}$ near zero; no convergence of the infinite formal series is asserted.

<a id="prof:remainder-equation"></a>
Let $W=p-p_J$. The exact divided-difference identity $$\frac p{1+p}-\frac{p_J}{1+p_J}
 =\frac{W}{(1+p)(1+p_J)}$$ gives the linear equation $$\label{prof:remainder-equation}
 W'''+W=-e_J-a_2W''-a_1W'-a_0W,$$ where $$a_2=\frac{30}{s},\qquad a_1=\frac B{s^2},\qquad
 a_0=\frac C{s^3}+\frac1{(1+p)(1+p_J)}-1.$$ These are now treated as given coefficient functions. Their absolute sum tends to zero because $p,p_J\to0$, and all their derivatives are bounded on a sufficiently late half-line.

<a id="prof:weighted-green"></a>
<a id="prof:weighted-green-2"></a>
The polynomial $\lambda^3+1$ has roots $-1$ and $(1\pm i\sqrt3)/2$. The analogue of <a href="#prof:green-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:green-formula">[prof:green-formula]</a> on $[S,\infty)$, with these roots, writes the bounded solution of $W'''+W=j$ as a stable homogeneous vector plus a convolution vector $\mathcal K_Sj$. Every root has modulus one and $|(\lambda^3+1)'|=3$ at the roots. Hence $$\label{prof:weighted-green}
 \max_{0\le i\le2}\lVert (\mathcal K_Sj)_i\rVert_\infty
 \le\frac53\lVert j\rVert_\infty.$$ It also acts in the Banach space with norm $\lVert j\rVert_N=\sup_{s\ge S}s^N|j(s)|$. For $S\le u\le s$, $$(s/u)^N\le\exp\bigl(N(s-u)/S\bigr),$$ while for $u\ge s$ this ratio is at most one. If $S\ge2N$, the forward kernel is therefore bounded by an exponential with decay rate $1/2$, and the two backward kernels already have decay rate $1/2$. It follows that $$\label{prof:weighted-green-2}
 \max_{0\le i\le2}\sup_{s\ge S}s^N| (\mathcal K_Sj)_i(s)|
 \le2\lVert j\rVert_N.$$ The stable homogeneous vector $H(s)=q_S\mathrm{e}^{-(s-S)}(1,-1,1)^t$ belongs to both spaces. Here $q_S$ is chosen to be the stable coordinate of the actual jet $(W,W',W'')(S)$.

<a id="prof:weighted-source"></a>
Choose $S\ge2N$ so late that $2\sup_{s\ge S}(|a_0|+|a_1|+|a_2|)<1$. The affine equation for the source, $$\label{prof:weighted-source}
 j=-e_J-\sum_{i=0}^2a_i\bigl(H_i+(\mathcal K_Sj)_i\bigr),$$ is a contraction both in the bounded norm and in the norm $\lVert \cdot\rVert_N$, by <a href="#prof:weighted-green" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:weighted-green">[prof:weighted-green]</a>–<a href="#prof:weighted-green-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:weighted-green-2">[prof:weighted-green-2]</a>. It has a unique solution in each space. The weighted solution is bounded, so uniqueness in the bounded space identifies the two solutions. On the other hand, the actual remainder has a bounded jet and bounded source $j=W'''+W$. The growing-mode integrals from infinity are forced by boundedness, so its jet has the representation $H+\mathcal K_Sj$ with the chosen $q_S$. It satisfies <a href="#prof:weighted-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:weighted-source">[prof:weighted-source]</a> and hence equals the weighted solution. We have proved <a id="prof:preliminary-remainder"></a>$$\label{prof:preliminary-remainder}
 W,W',W''=O(s^{-N}).$$

Equation <a href="#prof:remainder-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:remainder-equation">[prof:remainder-equation]</a> gives $W'''=O(s^{-N})$. Differentiating it repeatedly, using the bounded coefficient derivatives and <a href="#prof:formal-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:formal-residual">[prof:formal-residual]</a>, proves $W^{(\ell)}=O(s^{-N})$ for every fixed $\ell$. To obtain the extra decay for each derivative in <a href="#prof:s-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:s-expansion">[prof:s-expansion]</a>, fix $J,\ell$ and apply this last conclusion to the deeper truncation $p_K$, where $K\ge J+\ell$. Then $$(p-p_J)^{(\ell)}
 =(p-p_K)^{(\ell)}+
   \sum_{j=J+1}^{K}d_j(s^{-3j})^{(\ell)}
 =O\bigl(s^{-3(J+1)-\ell}\bigr).$$ The starting point $S$ and constants may depend on the finite orders $J,\ell$, which is exactly what the asserted asymptotic statement requires. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prof-weighted-remainder-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prof-weighted-remainder</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#prof:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:profile">6</a>.* Finally $s^{-3}=(16/27)c^2y^{-2}$, so <a href="#prof:s-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:s-expansion">[prof:s-expansion]</a> and the chain rule give <a href="#prof:expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:expansion">[prof:expansion]</a>, with $g_j=c\,d_j((16/27)c^2)^j$. Repeated differentiation of $u\mapsto G(u^{-1/2})$ expresses each derivative as a finite sum of powers of $y$ times derivatives of $G$. Subtracting sufficiently many terms in <a href="#prof:expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:expansion">[prof:expansion]</a> shows that these derivatives have their Taylor limits at $u=0$. The resulting extension is $C^\infty$. The origin expansion gives the smooth extension of $h$, and the calculation in Section <a href="#geo:core-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:core-section">2.2</a> proves the flat equation in Theorem <a href="#prof:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:profile">6</a>, also at $x=0$ by smoothness. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:parabolic"></a>
### Parabolic norms and uniform local estimates

<span id="ana:section" label="ana:section"></span>

The correction to the profile must satisfy the lower-order bound in Lemma <a href="#geo:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:obstruction">5</a>. To encode that bound together with its spatial derivatives, put $$\rho=\sqrt{-t},\qquad r=\sqrt{x},\qquad
 \sigma=(r^2+\rho)^{1/2},\qquad p_0=-2+2\delta,
 \qquad 0<\delta<1.$$ At the concentrating point, a bound $|V|\le\varepsilon\sigma^{p_0}$ gives $|V(t,0)|\le\varepsilon\rho^{-1+\delta}$, smaller than the profile’s $\rho^{-1}$ term. On the sphere, $v_*\le r^2\le\sigma^2$, so it also gives $|v_*V|\le\varepsilon\sigma^{2\delta}$. Since $\sigma$ is bounded on $\Sigma\times[-T,0)$ for fixed $T$, a small weighted norm will preserve positivity of the multiplier $1+v_*(F+V)$. Outside the shrinking core, $r^2\ge\rho$, the scale $\sigma$ is comparable to $r$ and the same weight records spatial decay. The particular value of $\delta$ will be chosen in Theorem <a href="#flat:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flat:inverse">18</a>.

The norms below measure four spatial derivatives and one time derivative in units $(\sigma,\sigma^4)$. We also need estimates for an outer forward problem with zero initial data, uniform as the elapsed time tends to zero. For this purpose we distinguish the full parabolic norm from the norm that omits the first time derivative.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Norms and scales

<a id="ana:local-norm"></a>
On a product $D=U\times I\subset\mathbb{R}^d\times\mathbb{R}$, put $$d_{\rm p}((z,t),(w,s))=|z-w|+|t-s|^{1/4}.$$ Write $$[g]_{\alpha;D}=\sup_{P\ne Q\in D}
       \frac{|g(P)-g(Q)|}{d_{\rm p}(P,Q)^\alpha},
 \quad |g|_{\alpha;D}=\|g\|_{\infty;D}+[g]_{\alpha;D}.$$ The joint seminorm is equivalent, with constants depending only on $\alpha$, to the sum of the spatial $\alpha$ and temporal $\alpha/4$ seminorms. We use the following explicit version of the fourth-order parabolic norm: $$\label{ana:local-norm}
 \mathcal N_\alpha(u;D)
   =\sum_{j=0}^4|D_z^ju|_{\alpha;D}+|u_t|_{\alpha;D},\qquad
 \mathcal N'_\alpha(u;D)=\sum_{j=0}^4|D_z^ju|_{\alpha;D}.$$ Here and below norms of arrays mean sums of component norms. Thus $\mathcal N'_\alpha$ still includes temporal Hölder seminorms of all four spatial derivatives.

<a id="stmt-ana-spaces"></a>
<div id="ana:spaces" class="definition">

<a id="ana:X"></a>
<a id="ana:Y"></a>
**Definition 12** (Weighted local spaces). Let $M$ be either $\mathbb{R}^m$ or the fixed sphere $\Sigma$, let $I$ be a time interval, and let $s(P)>0$ be a scale on $M\times I$. Fix a sufficiently small number $a_0>0$. In Euclidean coordinates, or normal coordinates on $\Sigma$, centered at $P=(z_0,t_0)$, write $s_0=s(P)$ and set $$\Psi_P(y,\theta)=(\exp_{z_0}(s_0y),t_0+s_0^4\theta),\qquad
 D_P=B_{a_0}\times
       \bigl((-a_0^4,a_0^4)\cap s_0^{-4}(I-t_0)\bigr).$$ In the Euclidean case the exponential map means translation. The one-sided endpoints belonging to $I$ are included. For a fixed weight $p$, define <a id="ana:Xprime"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|u\|_{X_{\alpha,p}(s)}
   &=\sup_P\mathcal N_\alpha(s_0^{-p}u\circ\Psi_P;D_P),\label{ana:X}\\
 \|u\|_{X'_{\alpha,p}(s)}
   &=\sup_P\mathcal N'_\alpha(s_0^{-p}u\circ\Psi_P;D_P),\label{ana:Xprime}\\
 \|f\|_{Y_{\alpha,p}(s)}
   &=\sup_P|s_0^{4-p}f\circ\Psi_P|_{\alpha;D_P}.
                                                        \label{ana:Y}
\end{aligned}$$ The derivatives in these formulas are derivatives of the displayed pullbacks. On the sphere $s$ is bounded above so that these charts, and their fixed enlargements used below, lie within the injectivity radius. Restriction to radial functions on $\mathbb{R}^m$, or to invariant functions on $\Sigma$, gives closed subspaces with these same norms.

Unless specified otherwise, $X,X',Y$ mean the spaces <a href="#ana:X" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:X">[ana:X]</a>–<a href="#ana:Y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:Y">[ana:Y]</a> with $s=\sigma$, $p=p_0$, and the fixed exponent $0<\alpha<1$. Subscripts $\alpha'$ indicate replacement of $\alpha$ by $\alpha'$, with $\alpha<\alpha'<1$.

</div>

These are local weighted norms; no Hölder quotient between distant boxes is imposed. Their completeness follows by convergence of the functions and indicated derivatives on every compact subset, followed by taking the supremum in the defining inequalities. The derivative relations pass to the limit distributionally. In particular $X$ and $X'$ are Banach spaces and $X\hookrightarrow X'$ has norm at most one.

The radius function $r$ is Lipschitz on both spaces. The elementary inequalities for Euclidean norms and fourth roots give $$|\sigma(z,t)-\sigma(z_0,t_0)|
 \le |r(z)-r(z_0)|+|t-t_0|^{1/4}.$$ Consequently $\sigma$ is comparable to its center value on every box in Definition <a href="#ana:spaces" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:spaces">12</a>, including boxes truncated at $t=0$. Changing $a_0$ to another fixed sufficiently small radius gives equivalent norms: cover the enlarged box by finitely many smaller comparable-scale boxes, and split a Hölder quotient whose points are not in a common small box using the supremum norm. The same argument applies to $s_\beta=\max(\beta,r)$, whose lack of smoothness at $r=\beta$ is harmless: the scale is frozen at each center, never differentiated. Products satisfy the usual local Hölder inequality $|fg|_\alpha\le 2|f|_\alpha|g|_\alpha$, with their weights added.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Interpolation without a lower bound for the time length

<a id="ana:interp-basic"></a>
<a id="ana:fourth-sup-interp"></a>
We first isolate the interpolation used in localization. For fixed nested balls $B_1\Subset B_2$ and any interval $I$ of length at most one, let $$U=\|u\|_{\infty;B_2\times I},\qquad
