<a id="stmt-glue-extension"></a>
<div id="glue:extension" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Extension of the inner source). *There is a real linear extension $\mathcal E_i:Y_\alpha\to
Y_\alpha(\mathbb{R}^m\times(-\infty,0))$, uniformly bounded for $T\le\beta^4$, which equals $\chi_i f$ in flat coordinates for $-T\le t<0$, and is supported spatially in $r\le2\beta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-glue-extension"><summary>Proof of stmt-glue-extension</summary>

*Proof.* Put $f_i=\chi_i f$, view it in the flat coordinates, and extend it by zero beyond their neighborhood. The spatial extension is bounded because the cutoff vanishes before the boundary of that neighborhood. The $Y_\alpha$ norm gives a spatial $C^\alpha$ trace $f_i(-T,z)$ at the lower time face. Choose a smooth $\psi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]$ equal to one near zero and zero for arguments at least one. For $t<-T$ set <a id="glue:extension-formula"></a>$$\label{glue:extension-formula}
 (\mathcal E_i f)(t,z)
 =f_i(-T,z)\,\psi\left(\frac{-t-T}{(|z|^2+\sqrt T)^2}\right).$$ Let $b_0=(|z|^2+\sqrt T)^{1/2}$. Wherever this expression is nonzero, $$T\le-t\le T+b_0^4,
 \qquad b_0^2\le |z|^2+\sqrt{-t}\le(1+\sqrt2)b_0^2.$$ The ancient scale is therefore comparable to $b_0$. The argument of $\psi$ has bounded derivatives in spatial units $b_0$ and temporal units $b_0^4$ on its transition region. The trace norm, Hölder multiplication, and scale comparability prove the required estimate below $-T$. Across that face the values agree. Splitting a temporal increment at $-T$ bounds its $\alpha/4$ Hölder quotient by the sum of the two one-sided bounds; a spatial increment is handled by the trace bound on comparable boxes. This proves the full parabolic $C^\alpha$ bound. No time derivative of the source is required. The construction is real, linear, and radial. Its continuation below $-T$ is in fact supported in $-26\beta^4\le t<-T$, though a temporal support restriction is unnecessary for the ancient inverse. ◻

</details>

<a id="glue:inner-identity"></a>
Apply Theorem <a href="#flat:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flat:inverse">18</a> to obtain $$W_f=\widehat D\mathcal E_i f,\qquad V_i=\chi_b W_f,$$ where $V_i$ is regarded as a function on the sphere and extended by zero. Invariant norm equivalence and the cutoff bounds give $\lVert V_i\rVert_{X_\alpha}\le C\lVert f\rVert_{Y_\alpha}$. Define <a id="glue:inner-errors"></a>$$\label{glue:inner-errors}
 R_i f=(\mathcal P-\widehat\mathcal P)(\chi_bW_f),\qquad
 J_i f=[\widehat\mathcal P,\chi_b]W_f.$$ Both expressions are taken in the common coordinates and extended by zero. Since $\chi_b f_i=f_i$, the exact identity is $$\label{glue:inner-identity}
 \mathcal PV_i=f_i+R_i f+J_i f.$$ Lemma <a href="#glue:background" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:background">20</a> and $b\le C\beta$ on the relevant boxes give <a id="glue:inner-error-bounds"></a>$$\label{glue:inner-error-bounds}
 \lVert R_i f\rVert_{Y_\alpha}\le C\beta^2\lVert f\rVert_{Y_\alpha},
 \qquad
 \lVert J_i f\rVert_{Y_\alpha}\le C\lVert f\rVert_{Y_\alpha}.$$ Here $R_i f$ is supported in $r\le4\beta$, and $J_i f$ in $2\beta\le r\le4\beta$. The commutator has order at most three; on its support $b$ is comparable to $\beta$, so every derivative of $\chi_b$ is uniformly bounded in scale units. The definition of $R_i$ includes the mismatch applied to the cutoff itself, and hence accounts for all mixed cutoff and mismatch terms.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The outer Cauchy problem and the exact right inverse

Set $$s_\beta=\max(\beta,r),\qquad \eta=T/\beta^4,$$ and use the nonsingular weighted spaces with scale $s_\beta$. Equivalently one can use the smooth comparable scale $(\beta^2+r^2)^{1/2}$; the norms and all following estimates are uniformly equivalent. Define a modified operator on the entire sphere by <a id="glue:modified-operator"></a>$$\label{glue:modified-operator}
 \mathcal P_\beta=\partial_t+A_\beta\mathcal A+B_\beta,
 \qquad
 A_\beta=\kappa A_c+(1-\kappa),\qquad B_\beta=\kappa B_c.$$ Its principal multiplier is bounded above and below by positive constants, and $\mathcal P_\beta=\mathcal P$ for $r\ge\beta/2$. Lemma <a href="#glue:background" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:background">20</a> shows, for every $j,k$, uniform bounds for the derivatives of $A_\beta$ and $s_\beta^4B_\beta$ in units $(s_\beta,s_\beta^4)$, with the scale fixed at the center of each box. On the transition annulus $r\asymp\beta$ these are exactly its scaled background bounds and the cutoff bounds. Inside $r\le\beta/4$ the coefficients are $1$ and $0$; outside the transition they are the original coefficients with $\sigma\asymp s_\beta$. The sphere coefficients of $\mathcal A$ also have uniform bounds in these units. These facts verify all coefficient and ellipticity hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#ana:short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:short">15</a>, uniformly for $\eta\le1$.

<a id="glue:outer-source"></a>
The outer source is $$\label{glue:outer-source}
 f_o=f-f_i-J_i f.$$ It vanishes for $r<\beta$ and has <a id="glue:outer-source-bound"></a>$$\label{glue:outer-source-bound}
 \lVert f_o\rVert_{Y_\alpha(s_\beta)}\le C\lVert f\rVert_{Y_\alpha}.$$ Indeed $s_\beta\asymp\sigma$ on its support and on every sufficiently small box meeting that support. The latter assertion follows from the Lipschitz bound for $r$ and the box radius $a_0\sigma$; it also handles boxes crossing a support boundary. The same comparison holds for outputs supported in $r\ge\beta/2$.

For each fixed $\beta,T$, solve the closed-manifold Cauchy problem <a id="glue:outer-cauchy"></a>$$\label{glue:outer-cauchy}
 \mathcal P_\beta Z_o=f_o,\qquad Z_o(-T)=0.$$ The modified coefficients extend smoothly to $t=0$ by <a href="#glue:outer-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:outer-expansion">[glue:outer-expansion]</a>, and $f_o$ extends Hölder continuously there: for fixed $\beta$ its nonsingular weighted norm is an ordinary Hölder bound on a compact cylinder. Thus the solvability assertion in Lemma <a href="#ana:short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:short">15</a> applies, or one may solve on truncated cylinders and use its uniform bounds. Uniqueness makes $Z_o$ invariant and the solution operator real linear.

<a id="glue:outer-bound"></a>
<a id="glue:outer-sup"></a>
Choose a fixed $\eta_0>0$ small enough for Lemma <a href="#ana:short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:short">15</a> at both exponents $\alpha$ and $\alpha'$. That Lemma gives, for $0<\eta\le\eta_0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \lVert Z_o\rVert_{X_\alpha(s_\beta)}
   &\le C\lVert f_o\rVert_{Y_\alpha(s_\beta)},\label{glue:outer-bound}\\
 \sup s_\beta^{-p_0}\lvert Z_o\rvert
   &\le C\eta\lVert f_o\rVert_{Y_\alpha(s_\beta)}.\label{glue:outer-sup}
\end{aligned}$$ The constants are uniform in $\beta,T$ by the coefficient and scale bounds verified above. The small parameter in Lemma <a href="#ana:short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:short">15</a> is precisely $\eta=T/\beta^4$, since $\inf s_\beta=\beta$. That lemma applies to zero initial data even when $f_o(-T)$ is nonzero.

<a id="glue:outer-identity"></a>
<a id="glue:outer-error-small"></a>
Put $V_o=\chi_o Z_o$. On the support of this product the coefficients have already become those of $\mathcal P$, and $\chi_o f_o=f_o$. Consequently $$\label{glue:outer-identity}
 \mathcal PV_o=f_o+J_o f,\qquad
 J_o f=[\mathcal P_\beta,\chi_o]Z_o.$$ This commutator is supported in $\beta/2\le r\le\beta$ and involves spatial derivatives of $Z_o$ of order at most three. For these derivatives, Lemma <a href="#ana:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:interpolation">16</a> and <a href="#glue:outer-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:outer-bound">[glue:outer-bound]</a>–<a href="#glue:outer-sup" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:outer-sup">[glue:outer-sup]</a> give the scaled parabolic $C^\alpha$ bound $C\eta^{(1-\alpha)/4}\lVert f_o\rVert_{Y_\alpha(s_\beta)}$. Since $s_\beta\asymp\sigma\asymp\beta$ on the commutator annulus, $$\label{glue:outer-error-small}
 \lVert J_o f\rVert_{Y_\alpha}
 \le C\eta^{(1-\alpha)/4}\lVert f\rVert_{Y_\alpha}.$$ In particular, no fourth derivative needs to be made small for this commutator estimate.

<a id="stmt-glue-inverse"></a>
<div id="glue:inverse" class="proposition">

**Proposition 22** (Uniform compact right inverse). *There are $\beta_0,\eta_0,C_D>0$ such that, for $0<\beta\le\beta_0$ and $0<T\le\eta_0\beta^4$, the operator $\mathcal P$ has a real invariant right inverse $D:Y_\alpha\to X_\alpha$ with $\lVert D\rVert\le C_D$. No initial value is prescribed for $Df$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-glue-inverse"><summary>Proof of stmt-glue-inverse</summary>

<a id="glue:neumann"></a>
*Proof.* Define $$D_{\mathrm{app}}f=V_i+V_o,\qquad \mathcal R=R_i+J_o.$$ Equations <a href="#glue:inner-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:inner-identity">[glue:inner-identity]</a>, <a href="#glue:outer-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:outer-source">[glue:outer-source]</a>, and <a href="#glue:outer-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:outer-identity">[glue:outer-identity]</a> give the exact operator identity <a id="glue:parametrix"></a>$$\label{glue:parametrix}
 \mathcal PD_{\mathrm{app}}=I+\mathcal R,\qquad
 \lVert D_{\mathrm{app}}\rVert_{Y_\alpha\to X_\alpha}\le C,\qquad
 \lVert \mathcal R\rVert_{Y_\alpha\to Y_\alpha}
 \le C\bigl(\beta^2+\eta^{(1-\alpha)/4}\bigr).$$ Choose $\beta_0$ and then $\eta_0$ small enough that the last bound is at most $1/2$ throughout the asserted range, decreasing the earlier $\eta_0$ if necessary. The convergent Neumann series defines $$\label{glue:neumann}
 D=D_{\mathrm{app}}(I+\mathcal R)^{-1}.$$ It satisfies $\mathcal PD=I$, is real and invariant, and has uniform norm at most twice the bound for $D_{\mathrm{app}}$. This is a right inverse construction and requires no assertion of injectivity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Smallness of the corrected error

The right inverse need not have small norm. What is needed for the nonlinear problem is its small value on the particular error $E_c$ in the weaker space $X'_\alpha$.

<a id="stmt-glue-special-error"></a>
<div id="glue:special-error" class="lemma">

<a id="glue:DE-small"></a>
**Lemma 23**. *For the right inverses of Proposition <a href="#glue:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:inverse">22</a>, $$\label{glue:DE-small}
 \lVert DE_c\rVert_{X'_\alpha}
 \le C\left(\beta^{2-2\delta}
       +\eta^{(\alpha'-\alpha)/4}
       +\eta^{(1-\alpha)/4}\right).$$ In particular this norm becomes arbitrarily small by choosing $\beta$ and then $\eta=T/\beta^4$ sufficiently small.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-glue-special-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-glue-special-error</summary>

*Proof.* Use the inner output cutoff, rather than the inner input cutoff, to split $$E_c=f_c+f_e,\qquad f_c=\chi_bE_c,\qquad f_e=(1-\chi_b)E_c.$$ Equation <a href="#glue:core-error-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:core-error-small">[glue:core-error-small]</a> gives $\lVert Df_c\rVert_{X_\alpha}\le C\beta^{2-2\delta}$. The remaining source vanishes for $r\le2\beta$, so $\chi_i f_e=0$ exactly. Its inner extension, flat correction, $R_i f_e$, and $J_i f_e$ all vanish. Thus $D_{\mathrm{app}}f_e$ consists solely of the outer zero-data solution for source $f_e$, followed by multiplication by $\chi_o$, and $\mathcal R f_e=J_o f_e$.

<a id="glue:app-special-small"></a>
The extra regularity in Lemma <a href="#glue:background" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:background">20</a> gives <a id="glue:outer-error-high"></a>$$\label{glue:outer-error-high}
 \lVert f_e\rVert_{Y_{\alpha'}(s_\beta)}\le C.$$ The constant is uniform in $\beta,T$: in the transition annulus all cutoff derivatives have bounded scaled size, and elsewhere this is <a href="#glue:error-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:error-weight">[glue:error-weight]</a> or the fixed exterior smooth bound. Apply Lemma <a href="#ana:short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:short">15</a> at exponent $\alpha'$ to the outer zero-data problem with source $f_e$. The coefficient bounds hold at that exponent, so its solution has uniformly bounded $X_{\alpha'}(s_\beta)$ norm. Equation <a href="#ana:fourth-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:fourth-small">[ana:fourth-small]</a> of Lemma <a href="#ana:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:interpolation">16</a> gives an $X'_\alpha(s_\beta)$ bound of $C\eta^{(\alpha'-\alpha)/4}$. Multiplication by $\chi_o$ is bounded in these scaled spaces, and $s_\beta$ is comparable to $\sigma$ on the output support. This proves the first estimate below; the commutator bound <a href="#glue:outer-error-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:outer-error-small">[glue:outer-error-small]</a> proves the second: $$\label{glue:app-special-small}
 \lVert D_{\mathrm{app}}f_e\rVert_{X'_\alpha}
 \le C\eta^{(\alpha'-\alpha)/4},\qquad
 \lVert \mathcal Rf_e\rVert_{Y_\alpha}
 \le C\eta^{(1-\alpha)/4}.$$ The first estimate includes the temporal Hölder seminorms of all spatial derivatives through order four, as supplied by <a href="#ana:fourth-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ana:fourth-small">[ana:fourth-small]</a>. It makes no claim that the first time derivative is small: the outer zero-data solution has time derivative $f_e$ at the initial face.

Finally, <a href="#glue:neumann" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:neumann">[glue:neumann]</a> gives $D(I+\mathcal R)=D_{\mathrm{app}}$, so <a id="glue:special-neumann-identity"></a>$$\label{glue:special-neumann-identity}
 Df_e=D_{\mathrm{app}}f_e-D\mathcal Rf_e.$$ The uniform bound for $D$ and <a href="#glue:app-special-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:app-special-small">[glue:app-special-small]</a> prove the desired estimate for $f_e$. Combining it with the estimate for $f_c$ proves <a href="#glue:DE-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:DE-small">[glue:DE-small]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### The nonlinear correction and finite-time obstruction

<details id="proof-stmt-glue-special-error-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-glue-special-error</summary>

<a id="glue:nonlinear-equation"></a>
<a id="glue:nonlinearity"></a>
<a id="glue:nonlinear-size"></a>
<a id="glue:nonlinear-lipschitz"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#glue:solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:solution">19</a>.* Expanding <a href="#glue:equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:equation">[glue:equation]</a> at $F$ gives $$\label{glue:nonlinear-equation}
 \mathcal PV+E_c+N(V)=0,$$ where $$\label{glue:nonlinearity}
 N(V)=v_*^2V^2\mathcal AF+
       \bigl(2v_*g_cV+v_*^2V^2\bigr)\mathcal AV.$$ This expression contains only spatial derivatives of $V$, so is defined on $X'_\alpha$. For clarity, on a box of scale $b$, a function with $\lVert V\rVert_{X'_\alpha}\le\varepsilon$ has scaled sizes $$V=O(\varepsilon b^{-2+2\delta}),\qquad
 \mathcal AV=O(\varepsilon b^{-6+2\delta}),$$ where these statements include the scaled parabolic $C^\alpha$ norms. By Lemma <a href="#glue:background" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:background">20</a>, the two quadratic terms in <a href="#glue:nonlinearity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:nonlinearity">[glue:nonlinearity]</a> therefore have size $C\varepsilon^2b^{-6+4\delta}$, and the cubic term has size $C\varepsilon^3b^{-6+6\delta}$. Relative to the $Y$ weight these gain $b^{2\delta}$ and $b^{4\delta}$, respectively. Since $b$ is bounded above on the compact domain, Hölder multiplication and the factorization of differences of products give uniform bounds $$\begin{aligned}
 \lVert N(V)\rVert_{Y_\alpha}&\le C_N(\varepsilon^2+\varepsilon^3),
 \label{glue:nonlinear-size}\\
 \lVert N(V)-N(W)\rVert_{Y_\alpha}
 &\le C_N(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^2)\lVert V-W\rVert_{X'_\alpha}
 \label{glue:nonlinear-lipschitz}
\end{aligned}$$ on the closed ball of radius $\varepsilon$ in $X'_\alpha$. The fixed sphere coefficients in $\mathcal A$ have bounded rescaled norms, so these estimates apply on the whole sphere, including the other endpoint $x=1$.

We specify the parameter choices to avoid a dependence on an inverse norm that is still being chosen. First fix the profile, exponents, flat inverse, and the constants $\beta_0,\eta_0,C_D$ of Proposition <a href="#glue:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:inverse">22</a>. The nonlinear constant $C_N$ is uniform throughout this parameter range. Choose the requested ball radius $\varepsilon>0$ so small that $$C_D C_N(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^2)\le\tfrac14,\qquad
 C_*\varepsilon\le\tfrac12\mu,$$ where $C_*$ bounds $\lvert v_*V\rvert/\lVert V\rVert_{X'_\alpha}$ uniformly. Such a $C_*$ exists because $v_*V$ has scaled size $C\varepsilon b^{2\delta}$ and $b\le\sqrt2$. Now take $\beta\le\beta_0$ sufficiently small, and next $\eta\le\min(\eta_0,a_0^4)$ sufficiently small, that Lemma <a href="#glue:special-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:special-error">23</a> gives $\lVert DE_c\rVert_{X'_\alpha}\le\varepsilon/2$; put $T=\eta\beta^4$.

The map $$\mathcal T(V)=-DE_c-DN(V)$$ takes the closed $X'_\alpha$ ball of radius $\varepsilon$ into itself by <a href="#glue:nonlinear-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:nonlinear-size">[glue:nonlinear-size]</a>, and is a contraction by <a href="#glue:nonlinear-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:nonlinear-lipschitz">[glue:nonlinear-lipschitz]</a>. The real invariant subspace is closed, so the Banach fixed point theorem gives a real invariant fixed point there. Its fixed point identity also shows that this same $V$ belongs to $X_\alpha$, because $E_c+N(V)\in Y_\alpha$ and $D:Y_\alpha\to X_\alpha$. Applying $\mathcal PD=I$ therefore yields <a href="#glue:nonlinear-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:nonlinear-equation">[glue:nonlinear-equation]</a> in the classical Hölder sense and proves <a href="#glue:equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:equation">[glue:equation]</a>. This recovers the time derivative after the contraction has been performed.

By <a href="#glue:background-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:background-positive">[glue:background-positive]</a> and the radius choice, $$\tfrac12\mu\le 1+v_*(F+V)\le M+\tfrac12\mu,$$ which proves <a href="#glue:positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:positive">[glue:positive]</a>. On each compact time subinterval of $(-T,0)$, the weight is bounded above and below, and the equation for $H$ is a uniformly parabolic scalar equation with principal multiplier $(1+v_*H)^2$. Its current regularity is $C^{4+\alpha,1+\alpha/4}$. Interior spatial difference quotients in this equation have the same principal operator; their sources are bounded in $C^\alpha$ because the coefficient is a smooth function of $x,H$ and $H$ already has four spatial derivatives. The interior estimate of Lemma <a href="#ana:schauder" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:schauder">13</a>, followed by passage to the difference-quotient limit on a smaller cylinder, gains one spatial derivative. Repetition gains every spatial derivative. The equation then gives corresponding spatial bounds for $H_t$; differentiating the equation in time and repeating the interior estimates gives all mixed derivatives. Thus $H$ is smooth on $\Sigma\times(-T,0)$. Invariant smoothness gives smoothness in $x\in[0,1]$ by Lemma <a href="#geo:operators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:operators">4</a>. At $x=0$, the $X'$ weight reads $$\lvert V(t,0)\rvert\le\varepsilon\sigma(t,0)^{-2+2\delta}
                =\varepsilon\rho^{-1+\delta},$$ up to the fixed equivalent-norm constant. This proves <a href="#glue:center" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="glue:center">[glue:center]</a> and finishes the construction. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-glue-special-error-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-glue-special-error</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Apply Theorem <a href="#glue:solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="glue:solution">19</a> to the nonconstant profile of Theorem <a href="#prof:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:profile">6</a>. Proposition <a href="#geo:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:reduction">3</a> turns the smooth positive coefficient $H$ into a Calabi flow in the Fubini–Study class on $\mathbb{CP}^{10}$. Choose its slice at $t=-T/2$ as the initial metric and translate time so that this slice has time zero. It is a smooth Kähler metric in a class containing the Fubini–Study metric. At the distinguished point, $$H(t,0)=\rho^{-1}G'(0)+O(\rho^{-1+\delta}),\qquad G'(0)<0.$$ Lemma <a href="#geo:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:obstruction">5</a> shows that the maximal smooth existence time is exactly $T_*=T/2$ and that, in the elapsed time $\tau$, $$S(\omega(\tau))(p)\sim a(T_*-\tau)^{-1/2},\qquad
 a=-110G'(0)>0.$$ The metric is $U(10)$ invariant by the radial reconstruction. These are all the assertions of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-glue-special-error-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-glue-special-error</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:product-singularities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:product-singularities">2</a>.* For a product metric the determinant factors and the inverse is block diagonal, so scalar curvatures add. The product volume form also shows that their averages add. Writing $c_\eta=S(\eta)$, we obtain $$S(\Omega(t))=\pi_X^*S(\omega(t))+c_\eta,
 \qquad \bar S_\Omega=\bar S_\omega+c_\eta.$$ Thus $\pi_X^*\phi(t)$, initially zero, satisfies <a href="#eq:calabi-flow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calabi-flow">[eq:calabi-flow]</a> for $\Omega_0$. Smooth local uniqueness (Chen and He 2008, Theorem 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[ChenHe2008](#ref-ChenHe2008)] identifies this explicit solution with the unrestricted flow on $[0,T_*)$. The displayed scalar-curvature identity gives the stated asymptotic. A smooth extension across $T_*$ would have bounded scalar curvature on the compact product, so the maximal time is exactly $T_*$.

For the final assertion put $m=n-10$. When $m>0$, choose $Y=\mathbb{CP}^m$ and $\eta=\frac{m+1}{11}\omega_{\mathrm{FS},m}$. In our normalization, $\operatorname{Ric}(\omega_{\mathrm{FS},j})
=(j+1)\omega_{\mathrm{FS},j}$. Constant scaling leaves the Ricci form unchanged, so $\omega_{\mathrm{FS},10}$ and $\eta$ both have Einstein constant $11$. Their product form is therefore Kähler–Einstein and lies in $[\Omega_0]$. For $m=0$, use Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> itself, with the second factor a point. ◻

</details>

<a id="cert:appendix"></a>
### Certification of the finite shooting calculation

<span id="sec:certificate" label="sec:certificate"></span> <span id="cert:section" label="cert:section"></span>

This Appendix proves the finite part of the shooting argument. The calculation uses integer interval arithmetic, Taylor expansion on complex disks, and a separate estimate for the nonlinear dependence on the shooting parameters. Every terminating decimal in this Appendix denotes its exact rational value.

<a id="stmt-cert-shoot"></a>
<div id="cert:shoot" class="proposition">

<a id="cert:conclusion"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Finite shooting bounds). *For every parameter $z_*\in[-R,R]^2$, where $R=5\cdot10^{-10}$, let $(k,d)$ be given by <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a>, and let $p$ be the origin solution of <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> supplied by Lemma <a href="#prof:origin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:origin">7</a>. This solution extends to $0\le s\le S_0=56$ and satisfies $$1+p(s)>0.$$ For the coordinates of its jet relative to $p_{\rm a}$, defined in <a href="#prof:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:coordinates">[prof:coordinates]</a>, one has $$\label{cert:conclusion}
 |q|\le .25R,\qquad
 \big\|(\Re\xi,\Im\xi)-z_*\big\|_\infty\le .13R.$$*

</div>

The proof compares the entire family of shots with a sequence of reference trajectories at the central parameter $d=d_0$. The state is $(p,p',p'',d)$, with $d'=0$. We divide $[1,56]$ into finitely many blocks. On each block, the reference trajectory is the exact solution starting from a rounded three-component jet at its left endpoint. At the right endpoint, an interval enclosure supplies the rounded starting jet for the next block. Thus successive reference trajectories may have small jumps at block boundaries; their size is included in the error estimate.

The interval calculation encloses the reference endpoint jets and the first-variation matrices of the four-component state across each block. Products of these matrices give a linear prediction for the effect of the two shooting parameters through all blocks. Rounding the reference jets and variation matrices introduces separate errors. A perturbation estimate compares products of the rounded variation matrices with the exact transfers and bounds the propagation of those errors. The origin series supplies the initial central jet, its two parameter variations, and a bound for the nonlinear parameter remainder.

It remains to compare these linear predictions with the actual solutions for every $z_*\in[-R,R]^2$. The only nonlinear term in the state equation depends on $p$; its remainder after linearization is quadratic in the difference from the reference value. A uniform bootstrap bounds this remainder using the certified positive lower bounds for $1+p$ along the reference trajectories and the transfer estimates. It proves continuation and positivity for the whole shooting family and bounds its endpoint error. Projecting the endpoint jets into the matching coordinates then gives <a href="#cert:conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:conclusion">[cert:conclusion]</a>.

We use the maximum norm on vectors and the induced maximum row-sum norm on matrices, including rectangular matrices. For a $4\times4$ matrix, its superscript $(3)$ denotes its leading $3\times3$ block. We first describe the interval arithmetic and the series recurrences, then justify their analytic enclosures and the separate nonlinear comparison.

<a id="cert:arithmetic"></a>
#### Integer intervals and the two series recurrences

Put $Q_{\rm int}=2^{256}$. An integer pair $(a,b)$ represents the closed interval $[a/Q_{\rm int},b/Q_{\rm int}]$. All arithmetic in `verification/certificate.py` is integer arithmetic. Addition and multiplication by an integer are exact, with endpoint reversal for a negative multiplier. For multiplication, let $m$ and $M$ be the minimum and maximum of the four products of integer endpoints. The output pair is $$\left(\left\lfloor\frac{m}{Q_{\rm int}}\right\rfloor,
       \left\lceil\frac{M}{Q_{\rm int}}\right\rceil\right).$$ For a same-sign interval $(a,b)$ excluding zero, the reciprocal pair is $$\left(\left\lfloor\frac{Q_{\rm int}^2}{b}\right\rfloor,
       \left\lceil\frac{Q_{\rm int}^2}{a}\right\rceil\right).$$ These formulas are valid also for negative intervals. Division by a positive integer rounds each endpoint outward in the same way. Python’s integer division is floor division, so the identity $\lceil x\rceil=-\lfloor-x\rfloor$ implements the required upper roundings. In particular, `rat(a,b)` encloses the exact rational $a/b$. The function `mag` bounds absolute value; the function `norm` bounds the matrix norm specified above.

The operation `widen` enlarges an interval at each end by the upper endpoint of a nonnegative error allowance. The operation `mid` instead selects the grid point $$Q_{\rm int}^{-1}\left\lfloor\frac{a+b}{2}\right\rfloor.$$ For an interval of real width $w$, its distance from every enclosed number is at most $w/2+Q_{\rm int}^{-1}$. The origin and block estimates below explicitly charge this error; midpoint selection is never used as an enclosure operation.

<a id="cert:reciprocal"></a>
Here are the coefficient identities underlying the computation. If $z(\theta)=\sum_{i\ge0}z_i\theta^i$ and $b(\theta)=1/z(\theta)=\sum_{i\ge0}b_i\theta^i$, then $$\label{cert:reciprocal}
 b_0=z_0^{-1},\qquad
 b_i=-z_0^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^i z_t b_{i-t}\quad(i\ge1).$$ Writing a dot for any one parameter variation gives, for $i\ge0$, <a id="cert:tangent-reciprocal"></a>$$\label{cert:tangent-reciprocal}
 \dot b_i=-z_0^{-1}
 \left(\sum_{t=0}^i\dot z_t b_{i-t}
             +\sum_{t=1}^i z_t\dot b_{i-t}\right).$$ The second sum is empty at $i=0$. In the first sum the coefficient $b_i$ has already been computed by <a href="#cert:reciprocal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:reciprocal">[cert:reciprocal]</a>. This is exactly the ordering in `recip`; it includes the variation of the constant coefficient $z_0$.

<a id="cert:origin-recurrence"></a>
At the origin, write $r=s^{3/2}$ locally and $$z=1+p=1+d+kr+\sum_{i\ge2}a_i r^i.$$ Equation <a href="#prof:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:ode">[prof:ode]</a> gives $$\label{cert:origin-recurrence}
 a_i=\frac{b_{i-2}-\mathbf1_{\{i=2\}}}
 { (27/8)(i-1)(i+9)(i+10)},\qquad i\ge2,$$ where now $b_i$ are the coefficients of the reciprocal series in $r$. The loop in `origin` computes $a_2,\ldots,a_{60}$, together with their two variations under the parameter map <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a>. The weights for evaluation of $p,p',p''$ at $s=1$ are $$1,\qquad \frac{3i}{2},\qquad
 \frac{3i}{2}\left(\frac{3i}{2}-1\right).$$ The code subtracts the constant $1$ from $z$ only in the zeroth state entry, as required.

<a id="cert:step-recurrence"></a>
For an ordinary Taylor step, let $\ell=1/h$ be the block length, $s_c$ the step’s initial point, $\theta=h(s-s_c)$, and $\mathfrak r=hs_c+\theta$. The equation for $z=1+p$, with $E=1+d$, is <a id="cert:scaled-ode"></a>$$\label{cert:scaled-ode}
 4\mathfrak r^3 z_{\theta\theta\theta}
 +120\mathfrak r^2 z_{\theta\theta}
 +751\mathfrak r z_\theta-1215(z-E)
 =\frac{4}{h^3}\mathfrak r^3(z^{-1}-1).$$ This identity also gives the four tangent equations upon differentiation. For completeness, put $\mathfrak R=8hs_c$ and $F_i=b_i-\mathbf1_{\{i=0\}}$, with negative-index coefficients zero. The scalar coefficient update used in `evolve` is $$\begin{aligned}
 4(i+1)(i+2)(i+3)\mathfrak R^3z_{i+3}
 ={}&\frac4{h^3}\sum_{a=0}^3
     c_a\mathfrak R^{3-a}F_{i-a}\notag\\
 &-(96i+960)\mathfrak R^2(i+1)(i+2)z_{i+2}\notag\\
 &-64\mathfrak R(i+1)
       \bigl(12i(i-1)+240i+751\bigr)z_{i+1}\notag\\
 &-512\bigl(4i(i-1)(i-2)+120i(i-1)+751i-1215\bigr)z_i\notag\\
 &-1215\cdot512E\mathbf1_{\{i=0\}},
 \qquad(c_0,c_1,c_2,c_3)=(1,24,192,512).
 \label{cert:step-recurrence}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed $c_a\mathfrak R^{3-a}$ are the coefficients of $(\mathfrak R+8\theta)^3$. The initial three coefficients are $z_0,z_\theta(0),z_{\theta\theta}(0)/2$. For a tangent column one differentiates every term of <a href="#cert:step-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:step-recurrence">[cert:step-recurrence]</a>; the constant $E$ has variation $0,0,0,1$ in the four columns. Thus the same update computes both state and tangent coefficients. The values $i=0,\ldots,43$ produce coefficients through degree $46$. Horner evaluation at $\theta=1/8$, after the appropriate one or two differentiations, evaluates the three state entries through degrees $46,45,44$, respectively. The analytic justification for their common remainder allowance is given in Subsection <a href="#cert:disks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:disks">7.3</a>.

<a id="cert:origin-error"></a>
#### The complete error at the origin

<a id="cert:origin-majorant"></a>
The parameter family of Lemma <a href="#prof:origin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:origin">7</a> is holomorphic on the complex polydisc $$|k-k_0|\le .1,\qquad |d-d_0|\le .1,$$ and its coefficients satisfy $$\label{cert:origin-majorant}
 \sum_{i\ge1}|a_i|3^i<.8,\qquad a_1=k.$$ One can check the constants directly from <a href="#cert:origin-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:origin-recurrence">[cert:origin-recurrence]</a>: the nonfree denominators exceed $440$, $3|k|<.585$, and $|1+d|>1.30$. The majorant improves itself because $$.585+\frac9{440}\left(1+\frac1{1.30-.8}\right)
 =\frac{711}{1100}<.8.$$ The reciprocal remains nonsingular throughout this construction. In particular the real solutions are positive up to $s=1$. Also $|d|<.501$, and <a href="#cert:origin-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:origin-majorant">[cert:origin-majorant]</a> gives $$|p(1)|<.501+\frac{.8}3<.768,\quad
 |p'(1)|\le .8\max_{i\ge1}\frac{3i/2}{3^i}\le .4,\quad
 |p''(1)|\le .8\max_{i\ge1}
       \frac{(3i/2)(3i/2-1)}{3^i}<.534.$$ In particular the uniform bound $2$ for every jet entry is available on the parameter polydisc.

Let <a id="cert:origin-tail"></a>$$\label{cert:origin-tail}
 E_{60}=.8\,\frac{(3\cdot61/2)^2}{3^{61}}
       <5.267\cdot10^{-26}.$$ The sequence $(3i/2)^2/3^i$ decreases for $i\ge61$. Consequently $E_{60}$ bounds the omitted tail of each of the three jet entries. Cauchy’s estimate in either parameter bounds the corresponding first derivative of this tail at the center by $10E_{60}$. For the matrix in <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a>, the sum of the absolute values of all four entries, including the factor $10^{-8}$, is $.01169933$. Thus the row norm of the omitted tail of the two $z_*$-variation columns is at most $.12E_{60}$.

<a id="cert:parameter-tail"></a>
Each of the two original parameter displacements has absolute value at most $.011R$. The multivariable Cauchy estimate, with $t_*=.11R$, bounds the terms of total degree at least two in the parameter expansion of any jet entry by $$\label{cert:parameter-tail}
 E_{\rm par}
 =2\sum_{j\ge2}(j+1)t_*^j
 =2\bigl((1-t_*)^{-2}-1-2t_*\bigr)
 <1.816\cdot10^{-20}.$$ The factor $j+1$ counts the monomials of total degree $j$ in two parameters.

<a id="cert:initial-error"></a>
The width assertion in `origin` bounds every computed state or variation interval by $10^{-20}$. Let $U_0\in\mathbb{R}^3$ be the three selected state midpoints. Let $Z$ have their two selected variation columns in its first three rows, and in its fourth row the exact second row of the parameter map <a href="#prof:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:parameters">[prof:parameters]</a>. The code stores intervals containing that exact fourth row; its matrix estimates therefore apply to this choice of $Z$. Combining the state midpoint error, the state and variation tails, the two variation midpoint errors, and <a href="#cert:parameter-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:parameter-tail">[cert:parameter-tail]</a>, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\|
 \begin{pmatrix}p(1)\\p'(1)\\p''(1)\\d\end{pmatrix}
 -\begin{pmatrix}U_0\\d_0\end{pmatrix}-Zz_*
 \right\|_\infty
 &\le 5\cdot10^{-21}+E_{60}+E_{\rm par}
        +.12E_{60}R+10^{-20}R+\frac3{Q_{\rm int}}\notag\\
 &<2.316\cdot10^{-20}<2\cdot10^{-19}.
 \label{cert:initial-error}
\end{aligned}$$ The fourth component of the error is exactly zero. All uncertainty in the interval construction of the central rational parameters is already included in the width assertion.

<a id="cert:disks"></a>
#### Analytic continuation and Taylor remainder on a step

The 288 blocks run from $s=1$ to $s=56$, with the following lengths: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 \text{range}&\ell&\text{number of blocks}\\ \hline
 {[1,2]}&1/32&32\\
 {[2,4]}&1/16&32\\
 {[4,8]}&1/8&32\\
 {[8,56]}&1/4&192.
 \end{array}$$ Each block consists of eight Taylor steps of length $\ell/8$. At every step start the interval assertions imply, for the exact reference state, <a id="cert:step-start"></a>$$\label{cert:step-start}
 -.76<p(0)<.4,\qquad |p_\theta(0)|<.06,\qquad
 |p_{\theta\theta}(0)|<.01.$$ Every entry in the first three rows of the four-column scaled variation matrix has modulus less than $6$; its fourth row is $(0,0,0,1)$. The step schedule gives $hs_c\ge32$.

<a id="cert:disk-h2"></a>
We now justify an analytic bound on the full complex disk $|\theta|\le1/2$, using only these starting bounds. On a disk where $|p(\theta)-p(0)|\le.1$, one has $$|\mathfrak r|\ge31.5,\qquad |1+p|>.14,\qquad
 |p|<.86,\qquad |p-d_0|<1.3.$$ Let $H_2$ be the supremum of $|p_{\theta\theta}|$ on any smaller concentric disk. Integrate the equation for $p_{\theta\theta}$ along radial line segments and apply the integral Gronwall inequality. Since $$\frac{30}{31.5}<.954,\qquad
 \frac{B}{31.5^2}<.190,\qquad
 \frac{|C|}{31.5^3}<.011,\qquad h^{-3}\le.015625,$$ and $|p_\theta|\le .06+H_2/2$, this gives $$\label{cert:disk-h2}
 H_2\le
 e^{.954/2}\left[
 .01+\frac12\left\{.190(.06+H_2/2)
               +.011\cdot1.3+.015625\frac{.86}{.14}\right\}\right].$$ The coefficient of $H_2$ on the right is less than one. Solving this linear inequality yields $H_2<.123602<.13$. Therefore <a id="cert:disk-displacement"></a>$$\label{cert:disk-displacement}
 |p(\theta)-p(0)|\le .06/2+.13/8=.04625<.1.$$ This strictly improves the bootstrap. On every disk of radius at most $1/2$, the state and its first two derivatives are bounded and both denominators $\mathfrak r$ and $1+p$ stay uniformly away from zero. The local holomorphic ODE theorem then continues the solution across any putative boundary of its disk of existence. Uniqueness makes the continuations agree. Thus the solution is holomorphic on a neighborhood of the closed disk of radius $1/2$, with the displayed bounds.

<a id="cert:scaled-jacobian"></a>
In the scaled state $(p,p_\theta,p_{\theta\theta},d)$, the Jacobian is $$\label{cert:scaled-jacobian}
 \mathcal J(\theta)=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 0&1&0&0\\
 0&0&1&0\\
 -C/\mathfrak r^3-h^{-3}/(1+p)^2&
       -B/\mathfrak r^2&-30/\mathfrak r&C/\mathfrak r^3\\
 0&0&0&0
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ On the complex disk its norm is bounded by the maximum of $1$ and $$\frac{30}{31.5}+\frac{751/4}{31.5^2}
       +\frac{1215/2}{31.5^3}+\frac{1/64}{.14^2}<2.1.$$ Each actual variation column, with initial norm at most $6$, consequently has norm at most $6e^{2.1/2}<17.146<32$ throughout the disk. The state entries themselves also have modulus less than $32$. These estimates apply to the actual block variation already propagated from the block’s initial identity matrix; a new identity is not assumed at each substep.

If a scalar holomorphic function is bounded by $32$ on this disk, Cauchy’s coefficient estimate gives a tail after degree $44$, at $\theta=1/8$, bounded by <a id="cert:step-tail"></a>$$\label{cert:step-tail}
 32\sum_{i\ge45}\left(\frac{1/8}{1/2}\right)^i
 =\frac{128}{3}\,4^{-45}<48\,4^{-45}.$$ Every state entry and every tangent entry is approximated to at least degree $44$. Hence the allowance $48\,4^{-45}$ added by `evolve` encloses all their Taylor tails. The interval recurrences then inductively enclose the exact reference solution and its exact first variation through all eight substeps.

On a real substep, the sharper displacement bound is <a id="cert:real-step-displacement"></a>$$\label{cert:real-step-displacement}
 |p(\theta)-p(0)|
 \le .06/8+\frac{.13}{2\cdot8^2}
 =.008515625<.01.$$ Accordingly, the smallest lower starting endpoint for $1+p$, reduced by $(\lfloor Q_{\rm int}/100\rfloor+1)/Q_{\rm int}>.01$, is a valid lower bound $m_j$ on the entire reference block. This is the variable `mini` divided by $Q_{\rm int}$.

<a id="cert:ledger"></a>
#### The finite inequalities and their exact interpretation

Write the block endpoints as $s_0=1,\ldots,s_N=56$, $N=288$. On block $j$, the reference solution has initial physical jet $U_j$ and parameter $d_0$; it is evolved exactly on that block. The rounded jet selected for the next block is $U_{j+1}$. Let $M_j$ be the exact $4\times4$ transfer of the linearized four-component physical state along this reference block.

<a id="cert:local-errors"></a>
The conversion from scaled derivatives to physical derivatives is essential. With $$S_j=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(1,\ell_j,\ell_j^2,1),$$ a scaled transfer $T$ becomes $S_j^{-1}TS_j$. Thus its entry in row $a$, column $b$, for $a,b\in\{0,1,2\}$, is multiplied by $h_j^a/h_j^b$; column $3$ is multiplied by $h_j^a$. The fourth row remains $(0,0,0,1)$. The code performs this conversion before asserting the widths. Consequently its assertions imply $$\label{cert:local-errors}
 \big\|U_{j+1}- (p_{\rm ref},p'_{\rm ref},p''_{\rm ref})
                         (s_{j+1})\big\|_\infty<10^{-19},
 \qquad
 |(M_j-\widetilde M_j)_{ab}|<10^{-15},$$ where $\widetilde M_j$ is the selected matrix of transfer midpoints. The state and transfer widths are, respectively, less than $10^{-19}$ and $10^{-15}$; the stated looser midpoint bounds follow from the midpoint estimate in Subsection <a href="#cert:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:arithmetic">7.1</a>. All fourth-component errors in <a href="#cert:local-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:local-errors">[cert:local-errors]</a> are zero.

<a id="cert:finite-products"></a>
Define the products of these point matrices by $$\widetilde T_{i,j}=
 \widetilde M_{i-1}\cdots\widetilde M_j\quad (i>j),
 \qquad \widetilde T_{i,i}=I_4.$$ The exact integer comparisons in `check` give $$\begin{aligned}
 m_j&>.23,\qquad \|Z\|<.02,\notag\\
 \max_{i\ge j}\|\widetilde T_{i,j}\|&\le8500,
 &\max_i\sum_{j<i}\|\widetilde T_{i,j+1}^{(3)}\|&\le570000,\notag\\
 \max_i\sum_{j<i}\frac{\ell_j}{m_j^3}
                \|\widetilde T_{i,j+1}^{(3)}\|&\le246000,
 &\max_i\|\widetilde T_{i,0}Z\|&\le2.7.
 \label{cert:finite-products}
\end{aligned}$$ Here and below a maximum over endpoints includes endpoint $0$; the assertion for it follows from $\|Z\|<.02$.

To explain the summation indices, for each endpoint $i$ the inner loop starts with the identity and takes $j=i-1,\ldots,0$. Immediately before multiplying by $\widetilde M_j$, its current interval matrix encloses $\widetilde T_{i,j+1}$. The update `T=mm(T,allm[j])` therefore produces the desired forward product in the correct order. If $r_j=Q_{\rm int}m_j$ and $\mathfrak d$ is the integer interval bound for the leading $3\times3$ norm, then $$\frac{\mathfrak d Q_{\rm int}^{\,2}}{h_jr_j^3}
 =\frac{\ell_j}{m_j^3}\frac{\mathfrak d}{Q_{\rm int}}.$$ The expression used for `K` is its floor plus one, hence an upper bound even when the quotient is integral. Thus the interval product arithmetic and the weighted sum both round in the required direction.

<a id="cert:finite-matching"></a>
Let $P_{\rm a}(d)=(p_{\rm a},p'_{\rm a},p''_{\rm a})(56)$, and put $Y=(\widetilde T_{N,0}Z)_{\mathrm{top}\,3}$. The final comparisons in `check` give $$\begin{aligned}
 \|U_N-P_{\rm a}(d_0)\|_\infty&<.04R,&
 \|\Lambda(U_N-P_{\rm a}(d_0))\|_\infty&<.006R,\notag\\
 \|(\Lambda Y)_{1,\cdot}\|_{\rm row}&<.116,&
 \|(\Lambda Y)_{\mathrm{bottom}\,2}-I_2\|&<.001.
 \label{cert:finite-matching}
\end{aligned}$$ The jet factors used for the term $d_m s^{-3m}$ are $1,-3m,3m(3m+1)$, with denominators $56^{3m}$, $56^{3m+1}$, $56^{3m+2}$, respectively. After multiplication by $d_m$, these give its value and first two derivatives at $s=56$. The index test in the last assertion subtracts the $2\times2$ identity from the two nonreal-coordinate rows.

The listed comparisons are finite integer statements: the displayed source constructs their operands and asserts them. Subsection <a href="#cert:provenance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:provenance">7.8</a> gives the command for checking them with assertions enabled. The preceding recurrence and disk arguments prove that these finite statements bound the specified analytic trajectories and variations.

<a id="cert:transfer-errors"></a>
#### Transfer errors and estimates within a block

<a id="cert:product-perturbation"></a>
<a id="cert:exact-sums"></a>
<a id="cert:linear-size"></a>
Let $T_{i,j}=M_{i-1}\cdots M_j$ be the exact products. By <a href="#cert:local-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:local-errors">[cert:local-errors]</a>, $\|M_j-\widetilde M_j\|<4\cdot10^{-15}$. Expand a product of at most $288$ factors, choosing the error factor at $k$ positions. Each intervening, possibly empty, product of approximate factors has norm at most $8500$, by <a href="#cert:finite-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:finite-products">[cert:finite-products]</a>. Summing the bounds for $k\ge1$ gives $$\label{cert:product-perturbation}
 \|T_{i,j}-\widetilde T_{i,j}\|
 \le8500\left[(1+4\cdot10^{-15}\cdot8500)^{288}-1\right]
 <.001.$$ In particular $\|T_{i,j}\|<8501$. Since the lengths of all the blocks sum to $55$, <a href="#cert:finite-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:finite-products">[cert:finite-products]</a> implies $$\label{cert:exact-sums}
 \max_i\sum_{j<i}\|T_{i,j+1}^{(3)}\|\le580000,\qquad
 \max_i\sum_{j<i}\frac{\ell_j}{m_j^3}
             \|T_{i,j+1}^{(3)}\|\le250000,$$ using the elementary comparisons $$570000+288\cdot.001<580000,\qquad
 246000+\frac{55\cdot.001}{.23^3}<250000.$$ Also $$\label{cert:linear-size}
 \|T_{i,0}Z\|\le2.7+.001\cdot.02<2.72.$$

<a id="cert:partial-transfer"></a>
We need two estimates for transfers between any two ordered points of one reference block, including points in different Taylor substeps. The real scaled Jacobian <a href="#cert:scaled-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:scaled-jacobian">[cert:scaled-jacobian]</a> has norm at most $$L_{\rm real}=
 \max\left\{1,\frac{30}{32}+\frac{751/4}{32^2}
                  +\frac{1215/2}{32^3}
                  +\frac{1/64}{.23^2}\right\}.$$ An interval of at most one block unit therefore has scaled transfer norm at most $e^{L_{\rm real}}<4.3$. Let $\Phi_j(s,t)$ denote its physical transfer. Conjugation by $S_j$ does not increase either the first-row norm or the maximum norm of the third column: in the first row the scaling factors are $1,\ell_j,\ell_j^2,1$, and in the third column they are $\ell_j^2,\ell_j,1,\ell_j^2$. Thus, with $e_3=(0,0,1,0)^t$, $$\label{cert:partial-transfer}
 \|(\Phi_j(s,t))_{1,\cdot}\|_{\rm row}\le4.3,\qquad
 \|\Phi_j(s,t)e_3\|_\infty\le4.3
 \quad(s_j\le t\le s\le s_{j+1}).$$ Only these two parts of a physical transfer are needed. Its full norm need not satisfy the same estimate.

All exponential comparisons just used, and the one in <a href="#cert:disk-h2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:disk-h2">[cert:disk-h2]</a>, can be checked by rational arithmetic. For $0\le x<82$, let $$E_{80}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{80}\frac{x^j}{j!}
 +\frac{x^{81}}{81!}\frac1{1-x/82}.$$ The positive exponential tail has successive ratios at most $x/82$, so $e^x\le E_{80}(x)$. Substituting the displayed rational values in this formula verifies all the strict numerical comparisons. The supplementary `verification/arithmetic_allowances.py` performs these rational checks, including <a href="#cert:initial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:initial-error">[cert:initial-error]</a>, <a href="#cert:product-perturbation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:product-perturbation">[cert:product-perturbation]</a>, and the remaining comparisons below.

<a id="cert:nonlinear"></a>
#### Uniform nonlinear error and closure of the bootstrap

<a id="cert:nonlinear-remainder"></a>
Fix $z_*\in[-R,R]^2$. On each block compare its actual shot with the reference solution on that block, and write $\delta p=p-p_{\rm ref}$. For the only nonlinear term in the physical third-component equation, $$-\frac{p}{1+p}
 +\frac{p_{\rm ref}}{1+p_{\rm ref}}
 +\frac{\delta p}{(1+p_{\rm ref})^2}
 =\frac{(\delta p)^2}{(1+p_{\rm ref})^2(1+p)}.$$ It follows that the difference equation, beyond the reference linearization, has forcing $e_3\mathcal N$, where $$\label{cert:nonlinear-remainder}
 |\mathcal N|=
 \frac{|\delta p|^2}{|1+p_{\rm ref}|^2|1+p|}.$$ The $d$-dependence is linear and is already included in the fourth variation component.

Temporarily assume $|\delta p|\le14R$ throughout the portions of the blocks reached so far. Since $m_j>.23$ and $m_j/(m_j-14R)<1.001$, <a href="#cert:nonlinear-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:nonlinear-remainder">[cert:nonlinear-remainder]</a> implies $$|\mathcal N|\le\frac{1.001(14R)^2}{m_j^3}.$$ The error accumulated by the end of block $j$, before multiplication by subsequent complete-block transfers, has zero fourth component. Its norm is bounded by $$10^{-19}
  +4.3\,\ell_j\frac{1.001(14R)^2}{m_j^3}.$$ The first term is the reference jump <a href="#cert:local-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:local-errors">[cert:local-errors]</a>; the second follows by variation of constants and the third-column estimate in <a href="#cert:partial-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:partial-transfer">[cert:partial-transfer]</a>.

<a id="cert:endpoint-error"></a>
Define the endpoint error $e_i$ by $$\begin{pmatrix}p(s_i)\\p'(s_i)\\p''(s_i)\\d\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}U_i\\d_0\end{pmatrix}
          +T_{i,0}Zz_*+e_i.$$ Its fourth component is zero. By <a href="#cert:initial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:initial-error">[cert:initial-error]</a>, <a href="#cert:exact-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:exact-sums">[cert:exact-sums]</a>, and the preceding local error estimate, $$\begin{aligned}
 \|e_i\|_\infty
 &\le580000\cdot10^{-19}+8501\cdot(2\cdot10^{-19})
       +4.3\cdot250000\cdot1.001(14R)^2\notag\\
 &=.1055747504R<.11R.
 \label{cert:endpoint-error}
\end{aligned}$$ For an interior point of a block, the first-row estimate in <a href="#cert:partial-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:partial-transfer">[cert:partial-transfer]</a>, <a href="#cert:linear-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:linear-size">[cert:linear-size]</a>, and the forcing estimate give <a id="cert:bootstrap-improvement"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 |\delta p|
 &\le4.3(2.72+.11)R
      +4.3\frac{1.001(14R)^2}{.23^3}\notag\\
 &<12.169035R<14R.
 \label{cert:bootstrap-improvement}
\end{aligned}$$ Here replacing the remaining block length by $1$ is a harmless overestimate. This improves the bootstrap strictly both at block starts and in block interiors, including the reference jumps. At $s=1$ it starts from $\|Z\|<.02$ and <a href="#cert:initial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:initial-error">[cert:initial-error]</a>. Continuation over the finitely many blocks therefore proves <a href="#cert:endpoint-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:endpoint-error">[cert:endpoint-error]</a> at every block endpoint.

For clarity, existence is part of this continuation argument. As long as the bootstrap holds, $1+p\ge .23-14R>0$. On the finite interval $1\le s\le56$, the equations for $p'$ and $p''$ form a linear system with bounded coefficients and bounded forcing once $p$ stays in this range. Gronwall’s inequality bounds these derivatives on every such interval. Thus no other finite-time escape can interrupt continuation. Positivity before $s=1$ was established in Subsection <a href="#cert:origin-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:origin-error">7.2</a>. This proves the existence and positivity statements of Proposition <a href="#cert:shoot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:shoot">24</a>.

<a id="cert:projection"></a>
#### Projection at the matching point

Let $\Gamma$ denote the exact real coordinate matrix of <a href="#prof:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:coordinates">[prof:coordinates]</a>. Lemma <a href="#prof:root-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:root-bounds">8</a> gives $$|\Gamma_{ab}-\Lambda_{ab}|<.001,
 \qquad
 \|\Gamma_{1,\cdot}\|_{\rm row}<.92,\qquad
 \|\Gamma_{a,\cdot}\|_{\rm row}<.68\quad(a=2,3).$$ Thus $\|\Gamma-\Lambda\|<.003$. The polynomial $P_{\rm a}$ also satisfies <a id="cert:pa-variation"></a>$$\label{cert:pa-variation}
 \|P_{\rm a}(d)-P_{\rm a}(d_0)\|_\infty
 \le .01|d-d_0|\le .01\cdot.011R;$$ this follows by differentiating the four explicit coefficients in <a href="#prof:asymptotic-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prof:asymptotic-polynomial">[prof:asymptotic-polynomial]</a>, as detailed in the proof of Lemma <a href="#prof:tail-residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prof:tail-residual">9</a>.

Set $A_*=U_N-P_{\rm a}(d_0)$. The endpoint error and transfer error give $$\begin{pmatrix}p\\p'\\p''\end{pmatrix}(56)-P_{\rm a}(d)
 = A_*+Yz_*+\epsilon_*
       -\bigl(P_{\rm a}(d)-P_{\rm a}(d_0)\bigr),
 \qquad
 \|\epsilon_*\|_\infty\le
       \bigl(.11+.001\cdot.02\bigr)R.$$ In fact $\epsilon_*$ is the sum of the top three entries of $e_N$ and $((T_{N,0}-\widetilde T_{N,0})Zz_*)_{\mathrm{top}\,3}$. Apply one row of $\Gamma$. The contribution of $A_*$, using <a href="#cert:finite-matching" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:finite-matching">[cert:finite-matching]</a>, is at most $(.006+.003\cdot.04)R$. For the first row the linear contribution is bounded by $(.116+.003\cdot2.7)R$. For row $a=2,3$, after subtracting the corresponding coordinate of $z_*$, it is bounded by $(.001+.003\cdot2.7)R$. The remaining contribution is bounded by the row norm of $\Gamma$ times $$\bigl(.11+.001\cdot.02+.01\cdot.011\bigr)R.$$ Consequently, in units of $R$, the two required estimates are $$\begin{aligned}
 |q|/R
 &\le .006+.003\cdot.04+.116+.003\cdot2.7
       +.92(.11+.001\cdot.02+.01\cdot.011)\\
 &=.2315396<.25,\\
 \big\|(\Re\xi,\Im\xi)-z_*\big\|_\infty/R
 &\le .006+.003\cdot.04+.001+.003\cdot2.7
       +.68(.11+.001\cdot.02+.01\cdot.011)\\
 &=.0901084<.13.
\end{aligned}$$ These are exact rational comparisons and establish <a href="#cert:conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:conclusion">[cert:conclusion]</a>, completing the proof of Proposition <a href="#cert:shoot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:shoot">24</a>.

<a id="cert:provenance"></a>
#### Executable source and reproducibility

The complete source printed in Appendix <a href="#app:code" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:code">8</a> is `verification/certificate.py`, comprising $6594$ UTF-8 bytes. Its SHA-256 digest is

<div class="center">

263389fd7217d84d0e5b66869797fc9f7998  
309d524a95f7ad7d3d0f538ea742

</div>

where the line break is for typesetting only. It uses no imported libraries, floating-point operations, external data, or platform-dependent numerical routines. From the paper directory, the verification command is

> python3 verification/certificate.py

with Python assertions enabled (no `-O` or `-OO`, and no `PYTHONOPTIMIZE` setting that disables assertions). The program is silent when every assertion succeeds.

The supplementary rational check

> python3 verification/arithmetic_allowances.py

verifies the analytic allowances used above by exact fraction arithmetic; its decimal report is descriptive only. The optional script `verification/tail-check.py` checks the explicit root and tail estimates in Section <a href="#sec:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:profile">[sec:profile]</a> by exact rational arithmetic. The finite integer assertions, the analytic enclosure argument in Subsections <a href="#cert:origin-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:origin-error">7.2</a>–<a href="#cert:disks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:disks">7.3</a>, and the nonlinear argument in Subsections <a href="#cert:transfer-errors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:transfer-errors">7.5</a>–<a href="#cert:projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:projection">7.7</a> together prove the finite shooting proposition.

<a id="app:code"></a>
### The exact-arithmetic certificate

The following Python program is the certificate used in Appendix <a href="#sec:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate">[sec:certificate]</a>. It uses only Python’s standard library and integer arithmetic. Run it with assertions enabled. Successful termination means that all its assertions hold; the analytic interpretation of those assertions is proved in Appendix <a href="#sec:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate">[sec:certificate]</a>.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The cutoff regions in [glue:cutoffs]. Distances are schematic. Solid bands indicate the value one, blank bands the value zero, and hatched bands the intervals where a cutoff may vary in $[0,1]$; no shape or monotonicity of the transition is assumed.",
      "figure_id": "fig:cutoffs",
      "pdf_page": 36,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:cutoffs",
          "exact_quote": "The cutoff regions in \\eqref{glue:cutoffs}. Distances are\nschematic. Solid bands indicate the value one, blank bands the value\nzero, and hatched bands the intervals where a cutoff may vary in\n$[0,1]$; no shape or monotonicity of the transit",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=.96cm,y=.6cm,font=\\small]\n\\definecolor{cutone}{RGB}{40,83,111}\n\\tikzset{oneregion/.style={fill=cutone,draw=cutone},\n transition/.style={pattern=north east lines,pattern color=black!55,draw=black!55}}\n\\foreach \\x/\\lab in {0/0,1/{\\beta/4},2/{\\beta/2},3/{\\beta},4.5/{2\\beta},6/{4\\beta}} {\n  \\draw[black!18] (\\x,.45)--(\\x,4.6);\n  \\node[above] at (\\x,4.6) {$\\lab$};\n}\n\\draw[->] (0,4.6)--(6.8,4.6) node[right] {$r$};\n\\foreach \\yy/\\lab in {4/{\\chi_i},3/{\\chi_b},2/{\\chi_o},1/{\\kappa}} {\n \\draw[black!30] (0,\\yy-.18) rectangle (6.6,\\yy+.18);\n \\node[left] at (-.25,\\yy) {$\\lab$};\n}\n\\filldraw[oneregion] (0,3.82) rectangle (3,4.18);\n\\filldraw[transition] (3,3.82) rectangle (4.5,4.18);\n\\filldraw[oneregion] (0,2.82) rectangle (4.5,3.18);\n\\filldraw[transition] (4.5,2.82) rectangle (6,3.18);\n\\filldraw[transition] (2,1.82) rectangle (3,2.18);\n\\filldraw[oneregion] (3,1.82) rectangle (6.6,2.18);\n\\filldraw[transition] (1,.82) rectangle (2,1.18);\n\\filldraw[oneregion] (2,.82) rectangle (6.6,1.18);\n\\filldraw[oneregion] (-.3,-.13) rectangle (.15,.13);\n\\node[right] at (.22,0) {identically $1$};\n\\filldraw[transition] (2.35,-.13) rectangle (2.8,.13);\n\\node[right] at (2.87,0) {may vary};\n\\draw[black!30] (4.85,-.13) rectangle (5.3,.13);\n\\node[right] at (5.37,0) {identically $0$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal axis labeled $r$ with arrow pointing right and tick marks at $0$, $\\beta/4$, $\\beta/2$, $\\beta$, $2\\beta$, $4\\beta$. Below the axis are four horizontal segmented bars aligned with the ticks:\n- Row labeled $\\chi_i$: solid band from $0$ to $\\beta$, diagonally hatched band from $\\beta$ to $2\\beta$, blank band from $2\\beta$ to $4\\beta$.\n- Row labeled $\\chi_b$: solid band from $0$ to $2\\beta$, diagonally hatched band from $2\\beta$ to $4\\beta$.\n- Row labeled $\\chi_o$: blank band from $0$ to $\\beta/2$, diagonally hatched band from $\\beta/2$ to $\\beta$, solid band from $\\beta$ to $4\\beta$.\n- Row labeled $\\kappa$: blank band from $0$ to $\\beta/4$, diagonally hatched band from $\\beta/4$ to $\\beta/2$, solid band from $\\beta/2$ to $4\\beta$.\nLegend at the bottom shows: solid square labeled \"identically 1\", hatched square labeled \"may vary\", blank square labeled \"identically 0\"."
    }
  ],
